# Airbnb Arbitrage Roadmap 2.0: Preston's 27 New Lessons and Resources

Complete teaching content. Prepared 25 September 2026 from the Roadmap 2.0 new-creation brief and the live production tracker.

This file holds the material Preston teaches from: learning outcomes, full narrative, worked examples, exercises, decision tools, and templates. The slide decks in `slides/` are built from it, and every slide names the section here that it maps to.

Education only. Nothing here is legal, tax, lending, or insurance advice. Rules for short-term rentals depend on the exact address. Tax treatment depends on the taxpayer and tax year. Loan terms depend on the borrower, the property, and the lender's current product. Each lesson ends with a **Who confirms what** section naming the professional who must review its examples before recording.

## How to use this file

- Lessons appear in tracker order within each part. Headings carry the tracker code, so `PA.6.3` is the third section of lesson PA.6.
- Two hypothetical examples recur throughout: the **Alder Street unit** (arbitrage) and the **Cedar Ridge house** (ownership). Their full numbers are in the next section. Every figure in them is an assumption.
- Links sit beside the claims they support. Each lesson ends with its source list and the date checked.
- Sam's demo lessons are referenced by code, never re-taught. Each Preston lesson says what to bring back from Sam's demo.
- The appendix lists facts that are still unresolved and who has to confirm them.

## The two recurring examples

All figures are **assumptions for teaching**, computed by `tools/examples.py`. Lessons quote these figures unchanged.

### Example A: the Alder Street unit (arbitrage)

A hypothetical two-bedroom, two-bath apartment (Unit 4B, 218 Alder Street) in a mid-size city we do not name, 10 minutes from a hospital campus and downtown. Sleeps 5. The student leases it from a small landlord who owns the four-unit building. The city is left unnamed on purpose: rules attach to an exact address, so lessons test the same unit against the official NYC and Los Angeles rules to show how the answer changes.

| Assumption | Value |
|---|---:|
| Monthly rent | $1,950 |
| Average nightly rate (ADR) | $205 |
| Occupancy | 70% |
| Average stay | 3.0 nights |
| Cleaning fee charged to guest | $110 |
| Cleaner pay per turnover | $95 |
| Airbnb single (host-only) service fee | 15.5% of nightly + cleaning |
| Utilities + internet | $260 / month |
| Supplies | $14 / stay |
| STR liability insurance | $1,300 / year |
| Software | $90 / month |
| Repairs reserve | $120 / month |
| Permit / registration | $250 / year |

Occupancy taxes are collected from guests and passed through, so they are left out of revenue and expenses here (lesson 3.3 covers them).

Booked nights 255, stays 85, ADR $205, occupancy 70%.

Total cash needed on day one: **$20,370**. Payback of sunk setup at the base-case net: $12,050 / $1,018 = **11.8 months**. The landlord has offered a 24-month lease.

#### B5. Three ways to compute a coverage ratio on the DSCR quote

- Gross booking revenue / annual PITIA: 2.42 (weak case 1.87).
- Gross revenue discounted 20% / annual PITIA: 1.94.
- NOI before property tax and insurance / annual PITIA: 1.18.

Same house, three different answers. The lender's written definition decides which one counts. The teaching point: a lender ratio above 1.0 does not mean positive cash flow for the owner.

Illustrative only: at an assumed 32% marginal rate, year-1 deductions of about $107,072 *could* reduce tax by about $34,263, **only if** the losses are usable against other income (material participation, average-stay tests, and loss limits). If the activity is passive, the loss may be suspended and carried forward. First-year straight-line uses a full year here for simplicity; the real first year depends on the placed-in-service month and convention.

# Part One: Airbnb Arbitrage Roadmap 2.0

<a id="lesson-1.3-market-reality-check"></a>
## 1.3  Is Arbitrage Still Viable in 2026? A Market Reality Check

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part One: Airbnb Arbitrage Roadmap 2.0 / Module 1 - Foundations & the 2026 Market Reality |
| Code | 1.3 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's 4.1-4.5 (market and property research demos); Module 3 (permission gate: 3.1, 3.2, 3.3, 3.4, 3.5); lesson 1.5 (the 2.5x rule); resource SR.2 underwriting spreadsheet |

### 1.3.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you can take one specific apartment and decide whether a lease on it has room for profit. You will compute its contribution per stay, its break-even occupancy, a stress case, and the months it takes to earn back your setup cash. You will then run it through three gates (address-level permission, written landlord permission, conservative math) and record a sign, investigate, or walk decision.

### 1.3.1 The question you're really asking

People ask me whether arbitrage still works in 2026. That question has no useful answer at the national level. A lease is a promise to pay a fixed rent at one address for a fixed term. So the only question that matters is about this unit. At this rent, in this building, under this city's rules, does it earn more than it costs in an ordinary bad month?

National numbers can look strong while your unit loses money. Airbnb's [Q2 2026 results](https://news.airbnb.com/airbnb-q2-2026-financial-results/) reported revenue up 17% to $3.6 billion and nights and seats booked up 10% year over year. Gross booking value was $27.2 billion, up 16%. That tells you travelers are still booking on the platform. It says nothing about the rent your landlord wants, the listings opening in your building, or whether your city lets you host at all.

AirDNA's [2026 outlook](https://www.airdna.co/outlook-report) points the other way at the listing level. As summarized on its site, AirDNA expected supply to grow slightly faster than demand in 2026. It projected occupancy down about 1%, average nightly rate up about 1.5%, and revenue per available night up about half a percent. Its [midyear update](https://www.airdna.co/blog/u.s.-mid-year-outlook-report-2026) is titled "A Better Year to Own Than to Buy" and says a tougher economy favored existing hosts.

Read both as weather reports. A platform can grow while the average listing's occupancy slips, because the new demand spreads across more listings. Your job as a new operator is to find out two things. Can your unit win its share of that demand? And does the rent leave a margin? Sam's 4.1 through 4.5 lessons show you how to pull the market data. This lesson shows you what to do with it once you have it.

### 1.3.2 Why a strong travel market does not rescue a weak lease

An arbitrage deal has two kinds of costs. Some rise and fall with bookings: the platform fee, the cleaner, supplies. Others arrive every month whether anyone books or not: rent, utilities, insurance, software, a repair reserve, your permit fee.

Rent is the big fixed one. On most leased units it's the largest line on the page. A travel boom can lift your nightly rate a bit. It can't lower your rent. So when the fixed costs are too high for the unit's realistic revenue, more tourists in the city won't close the gap. They book the listings with better locations, better reviews, or lower prices first.

That's why I want you to think in contribution margin. Contribution is what each stay leaves behind after the costs that come with that stay. Those contributions pile up during the month. The pile has to cover the fixed costs before you keep a dollar.

### 1.3.3 Contribution margin in plain terms

Here's the chain for one stay:

1. Booked nights times the nightly rate gives nightly revenue.
2. Add the cleaning fee the guest pays. That's the stay's gross revenue.
3. Subtract the platform service fee. Airbnb's [service fee page](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1857) describes a single fee structure where most hosts pay 15.5%. It also describes a split fee, where the host pays 3% and the guest pays a separate fee. Our example uses the 15.5% single fee on nightly plus cleaning.
4. Subtract what you pay the cleaner and the supplies that stay uses.
5. What's left is the stay's contribution.

Then add up every stay's contribution for the year and subtract fixed costs. That's your net before income tax.

If a local manager takes a percentage of revenue, that fee also moves with bookings. Put it in the variable costs. Lesson 6.5 covers when a manager makes sense. For now, notice that a 20% manager fee on Alder Street turns a positive year into a small loss (canonical what-if A3: net -$107 per year). Self-management is baked into the base case below.

Occupancy taxes are a separate stream. Guests pay them on top of the price, and they get passed through to the tax authority. They are not your revenue. Lesson 3.3 covers who collects which tax on each channel.

### 1.3.4 Break-even occupancy

Break-even occupancy is the share of nights you must book, at a given rate, for contributions to exactly cover fixed costs. Below it, you lose money. Above it, you keep something.

The formula is simple once you have the pieces:

- Break-even stays = annual fixed costs / contribution per stay
- Break-even nights = break-even stays x average stay length
- Break-even occupancy = break-even nights / 365

What makes break-even useful is the gap between it and your expected occupancy. That gap is your cushion. If you expect 70% and break even at 50%, you can lose a lot of bookings before you lose money. If you expect 62% and break even at 57%, one soft quarter wipes you out.

Break-even also moves with price. When you cut your rate to fill nights, each stay contributes less, so you need more stays to cover the same rent. Check break-even at the rate you'd charge in a slow month, not just your average.

### 1.3.5 Seasonality: the months that hurt

An annual average hides the months when you lose money. Most markets have a slow season. In a slow month your fixed costs stay put while bookings and rates both drop.

This matters for cash, not just profit. A deal can make money over a year and still run out of cash in February. You pay rent on the first of the month. You get paid by the platform after guests check in, according to its payout timing. If you enter a slow stretch with no cash reserve, you end up funding rent from your personal account.

So for each deal, sketch the slow months separately. Ask what occupancy and rate are realistic in the weakest two or three months. Then confirm your operating reserve covers the shortfall. Sam's 4.3 AirDNA tutorial shows where to see monthly history for comparable listings. Bring those monthly figures back to this worksheet.

### 1.3.6 Nearby listings and new supply

Your unit competes with every similar listing a guest sees in the same search. If five new two-bedrooms open within a few blocks, some guests who would have booked yours will book theirs. You feel that as lower occupancy, lower rates, or both.

Two things to check before you sign:

- How many comparable listings sit near the address today, and how their occupancy and rates look across the last 12 months.
- Whether new supply is coming. Look for new apartment buildings, new listings in the last few months, and demand that depends on one employer or one event.

AirDNA's outlook describes supply growing a little faster than demand in 2026. Whether that happens on your block is what Sam's 4.2 lesson (supply vs. revenue and oversupply screening) teaches you to check. For this lesson, turn it into a number: how much occupancy could new competition take from you before you hit break-even?

### 1.3.7 The stress case

A base case is what you expect. A stress case is what you can survive. I want you to build both before you sign anything.

A useful stress case changes the two inputs you control least: nightly rate and occupancy. It can also raise rent to test a landlord's renewal increase. Keep every other line the same, so the only thing you see is the effect of the stress.

If the stress case is still positive, the deal has room. If it's barely positive, you're betting on the base case. If it's negative, the lease has no margin for a normal slow year.

### 1.3.8 Startup cash and payback against the lease term

Setting up a unit costs money you don't get back: furniture, linens, safety gear, photos, the permit application. That's sunk setup cost. Other cash is tied up but not spent: the security deposit, prepaid rent, an operating reserve.

Payback is sunk setup divided by monthly net. It tells you how many months until the business returns the money you put in. Compare that with the lease term. If payback takes longer than the lease, a landlord who declines to renew leaves you with used furniture and a loss.

### 1.3.9 The three gates before signing

I use three gates. A deal must pass all three before you sign a lease.

**Gate 1: address-level permission.** The city, county, and state rules for this exact address must allow the use you plan (unhosted, whole unit, short stays). The building, zoning district, and any HOA or condo rules must allow it too. Module 3 teaches this gate. Lesson 3.1 explains the layers, Sam's 3.2 shows the live search, and 3.3 covers occupancy tax and registration numbers.

**Gate 2: written landlord permission.** The owner must approve short-term rental use in writing, in the lease or a signed addendum. A verbal "sure, that's fine" is not permission. Airbnb's own [hosting guidance](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1397) tells hosts to read their lease and check with their landlord and HOA before hosting. Lessons 3.5, 5.3, and 5.7 cover the addendum and the landlord conversation.

**Gate 3: conservative deal math.** The stress case must survive. Break-even must sit well below your expected occupancy. Payback must fit inside the lease term with room to spare.

The order matters. Check permission first, because it's the cheapest to check and the most absolute. Great math on a unit you can't legally operate is worth nothing.

### 1.3.10 Worked example: Alder Street

Alder Street is Unit 4B, 218 Alder Street: a hypothetical two-bedroom, two-bath apartment that sleeps 5. It's 10 minutes from a hospital campus and downtown in a mid-size city we don't name. The landlord owns the four-unit building and has offered a 24-month lease. All figures are assumptions from the course's canonical example.

**Step 1: contribution per stay at the base rate.**

| Line | Per stay | How |
|---|---:|---|
| Nightly revenue | $615.00 | 3.0 nights x $205 |
| Cleaning fee charged | $110.00 | |
| Stay gross revenue | $725.00 | |
| Platform fee | -$112.38 | 15.5% x $725 |
| Cleaner pay | -$95.00 | |
| Supplies | -$14.00 | |
| **Contribution per stay** | **$503.63** | About 69% of stay revenue |

That's about $167.88 of contribution per booked night.

**Step 2: annual fixed costs.**

| Fixed cost | Annual |
|---|---:|
| Rent ($1,950 x 12) | $23,400 |
| Utilities and internet ($260 x 12) | $3,120 |
| STR liability insurance | $1,300 |
| Software ($90 x 12) | $1,080 |
| Repairs and replacement reserve ($120 x 12) | $1,440 |
| Permit / registration | $250 |
| **Total fixed** | **$30,590** |

Monthly, that's about $2,549 you owe before the first guest arrives.

**Step 3: base case (canonical A1).** At 70% occupancy you book 255 nights, which is 85 stays of 3 nights.

- 85 stays x $503.63 = about $42,808 of contribution
- $42,808 - $30,590 = **$12,218 net before income tax**, about $1,018 a month

That matches the canonical table: gross booking revenue $61,625, total costs $49,407, net $12,218. Gross revenue is 2.63x annual rent. Lesson 1.5 explains how to use that multiple.

**Step 4: break-even occupancy at $205.**

- Break-even stays = $30,590 / $503.63 = 60.7 stays
- Break-even nights = 60.7 x 3 = 182.2 nights
- Break-even occupancy = 182.2 / 365 = **49.9%**

Expected occupancy is 70%. The cushion is about 20 points of occupancy.

**Step 5: stress case (canonical A2).** Nightly rate drops 12% to $180 and occupancy falls to 60%.

- Stay revenue: 3 x $180 + $110 = $650
- Contribution per stay: $650 - $100.75 fee - $95 - $14 = $440.25
- 219 nights, 73 stays: 73 x $440.25 = about $32,138
- $32,138 - $30,590 = **$1,548 net**, about $129 a month

Break-even at $180 is $30,590 / $440.25 = 69.5 stays, or 208.4 nights, or **57.1%** occupancy. In the stress case you're booking 60%, so you're only about three points above break-even.

**Step 6: stress plus a rent increase.** Suppose the landlord asks for $2,150 at renewal (canonical A3). At the base case, net drops to **$9,818** a year ($818 a month), and break-even rises to 53.8%. Combine that rent with the stress case and net is **-$852** a year (about -$71 a month), with break-even at 61.6% (assumption: derived from the canonical inputs with `tools/examples.py`).

**Step 7: new competition.** If new listings nearby pull occupancy from 70% to 64% at the same $205 rate (assumption), you'd book 234 nights and 78 stays. Net falls to **$8,693** a year, about $724 a month. The deal still works. Your cushion shrinks, though, and a rate cut on top would push you toward the stress case.

**Step 8: one slow month.** Say your weakest month books 12 of 30 nights at $180 (assumption: 40% occupancy). That's 4 stays.

- 4 x $440.25 = $1,761 of contribution
- Monthly fixed costs are about $2,549
- Net for the month: about **-$788**

The canonical operating reserve is $4,420 (two months of rent plus utilities). That covers about 5.6 months like this one. Three slow months in a row would use about $2,365 of it, which the reserve covers.

**Step 9: startup cash and payback (canonical A4).**

| Cash to start | Amount |
|---|---:|
| Sunk setup (furniture, linens, safety gear, photos, permit application) | $12,050 |
| Tied up (deposit, first month's rent, operating reserve) | $8,320 |
| **Total cash on day one** | **$20,370** |

- Base-case payback: $12,050 / $1,018 = **11.8 months**. The lease runs 24 months.
- Over 24 months at the base case, net totals about $24,432. After recovering setup, that leaves about $12,382.
- Stress-case payback: $12,050 / $129 = about **93 months**. That's almost four times the lease term.
- Over 24 months at the stress case, net totals about $3,096. You'd still be about $8,954 short of your setup cost.

**Step 10: run the gates.**

- Gate 1, address-level permission: unknown. The city is unnamed on purpose. Lesson 3.1 tests this same unit against the official New York City and Los Angeles rules. It fails both, for different reasons. That's the point: the math above is worthless until Gate 1 passes at the real address.
- Gate 2, written landlord permission: the landlord has offered a lease. The lease or a signed addendum still has to say short-term rental use is allowed, on which channels, and with what conditions.
- Gate 3, conservative math: the base case passes comfortably. The stress case survives by $1,548. The stress case plus a rent increase fails. Payback fits the lease only in the base case.

**Decision for Alder Street:** investigate. The math is good enough to keep going, but it depends on holding a 70% base case. Before signing, I'd want two things. First, a written cap on the rent at renewal, or a longer term. Second, market data from Sam's 4.1 to 4.5 lessons showing that 60% at $180 is a floor, not a typical month. If Gate 1 fails, the answer becomes walk, no matter what the numbers say.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### 1.3.11 Exercise: the viability decision sheet

Fill this in for one real address you're considering. Use Sam's 4.1-4.5 lessons for the market inputs. Use the SR.2 underwriting spreadsheet if you want the formulas done for you. Every number you enter is an estimate until you can cite a source.

**Part A: inputs**

| Input | Your figure | Source and date |
|---|---:|---|
| Address and unit | | |
| Monthly rent (quoted) | | Lease draft |
| Lease term offered (months) | | Lease draft |
| Expected nightly rate (ADR) | | Comparable listings, 12 months |
| Expected occupancy | | Comparable listings, 12 months |
| Average stay (nights) | | Comparable listings |
| Cleaning fee charged / cleaner pay | | Local cleaner quote |
| Platform fee % | | Platform fee page, your fee structure |
| Supplies per stay | | |
| Utilities + internet per month | | Utility history or estimate |
| Insurance per year | | Insurer quote |
| Software per month | | |
| Repairs reserve per month | | |
| Permit / registration per year | | City fee schedule |
| Sunk setup cost | | Furniture budget, quotes |
| Deposit + first month + reserve | | Lease draft, your plan |

**Part B: calculations**

| Output | Formula | Result |
|---|---|---:|
| Stay revenue | nights per stay x ADR + cleaning fee | |
| Contribution per stay | stay revenue - platform fee - cleaner - supplies | |
| Annual fixed costs | rent + utilities + insurance + software + repairs + permit | |
| Base-case net | stays per year x contribution - fixed | |
| Break-even occupancy | (fixed / contribution x stay length) / 365 | |
| Stress net (ADR -12%, occupancy -10 points) | same, with stressed inputs | |
| Stress net with rent +10% | same, with higher rent | |
| Weakest month net | slow-month stays x slow-month contribution - fixed / 12 | |
| Months of reserve at weakest month | reserve / weakest-month loss | |
| Payback, base (months) | sunk setup / base monthly net | |
| Payback, stress (months) | sunk setup / stress monthly net | |

**Part C: gates**

| Gate | Pass / fail / unknown | Evidence (link, document, date) |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Address-level permission (city, county, state, zoning, building, HOA) | | |
| 2. Written landlord permission (lease clause or signed addendum) | | |
| 3a. Stress case net is positive | | |
| 3b. Expected occupancy is at least 10 points above break-even | | |
| 3c. Base payback is under half the lease term | | |

The 10-point and half-the-lease thresholds are my suggested screens (assumption), not industry rules. Tighten them if the unit depends on one employer or one event.

**Part D: decision**

- **Sign:** all gates pass with evidence.
- **Investigate:** Gate 1 and Gate 2 pass or are in progress, and one part of Gate 3 is marginal. Write down what would change your answer (a rent cap, a longer term, better comparables).
- **Walk:** Gate 1 or Gate 2 fails, or the stress case is negative with no way to fix rent or term.

Decision: ______  Date: ______  What would change it: ______

### 1.3.12 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the city or county licensing office, in writing. They confirm that the planned use (unhosted, whole unit, short stays) is allowed at this exact address, and which permit it needs.

**Confirm with:** the landlord, in a signed lease clause or addendum. It should say short-term rental use is permitted, on which channels, and that you may use the address on a permit application. An attorney should review the addendum.

**Confirm with:** an insurer, in a written quote. It should show that the policy covers short-term rental guests at this address, at the premium you used in the model.

**Confirm with:** a CPA, before you rely on any after-tax number. The net figures in this lesson are before income tax.

**Confirm with:** the platform's current fee page, on the day you model the deal, which fee structure applies to your account.

### 1.3.13 Next lesson

Next in the course is 1.4, Mindset & My Personal Journey, a retained lesson. After that, 1.5 rebuilds the income math around the 2.5x rule, which uses the gross-to-rent multiple you just computed. Gate 1 is the one most students skip, and it's the one that can end a deal outright. Module 3 teaches it, starting with 3.1, which tests Alder Street against the New York City and Los Angeles rules.

### 1.3 sources

- [Airbnb Q2 2026 financial results](https://news.airbnb.com/airbnb-q2-2026-financial-results/): revenue $3.6 billion (+17%), nights and seats booked +10%, gross booking value $27.2 billion (+16%). Context only. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to news.airbnb.com.
- [AirDNA US 2026 Short-Term Rental Outlook](https://www.airdna.co/outlook-report): supply reaccelerating slightly faster than demand; occupancy about -1%, ADR about +1.5%, RevPAR about +0.5%. Context only. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airdna.co (search summary).
- [AirDNA 2026 and 2027 Outlook Midyear Update](https://www.airdna.co/blog/u.s.-mid-year-outlook-report-2026): title and statement that a tougher economy favored existing hosts. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airdna.co (search summary).
- [Airbnb service fees (help article 1857)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1857): single 15.5% host-only fee for most hosts; split-fee alternative. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- [Airbnb general info about hosting places to stay (help article 1397)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1397): check the lease, landlord, and HOA rules before hosting. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- Alder Street figures: `reference/recurring-examples.md` (A1-A4) and `tools/examples.py`. Derived figures (stress plus rent increase, 64% occupancy, slow month, 24-month totals) computed with python on 2026-09-25 from the same inputs.

<a id="lesson-3.1-how-str-regulation-works"></a>
## 3.1  How STR Regulation Actually Works: Licensing, Zoning & Primary-Residence Rules

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part One: Airbnb Arbitrage Roadmap 2.0 / Module 3 - STR Regulations & Compliance |
| Code | 3.1 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's 3.2 How to Research Regulations Before You Sign Anything (live search); 3.3 Occupancy Taxes & Platform-Verified Registration; 3.4 Red Flags; 3.5 Sublease Legality; resource SR.1 Regulatory compliance checklist |

### 3.1.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can list every layer of permission that applies to one exact address, from the state down to the lease. You can tell whether a city's rules allow the model you plan: hosted or unhosted, primary residence or not, how many nights, how long each stay. You'll also know which answers must come in writing, and from whom. The output is a completed permission ladder for one address with a permitted, prohibited, or unresolved result.

### 3.1.1 Why "Is Airbnb legal in this city?" is the wrong question

Students often ask whether a city is "Airbnb-friendly." That label doesn't help you. Permission attaches to an address, a use, and a person. A city may allow short stays in general. The building, the zoning district, the permit category, or your lease can still block the unit you want.

Here's a simple way to picture it. Think of a ladder with rungs. Each rung is a separate authority that can say no. You need a yes on every rung for your exact use. One no anywhere ends the deal, even if every other rung says yes.

Airbnb's own [regulations overview](https://www.airbnb.com/resources/hosting-homes/a/learn-about-hosting-regulations-7) says many cities require you to register, get a permit, or get a license before you list or accept guests. It also points out zoning rules and taxes. Treat that page as a starting point. It isn't the final word for any address. The final word is the official ordinance, the city office that administers it, and the documents that bind your building and your lease.

### 3.1.2 The layers of permission

Work from the widest authority down to the narrowest. Each layer has its own source document.

**State.** Some states set rules that apply everywhere in the state, such as statewide lodging tax registration or limits on what cities may regulate. Many states leave most short-term rental rules to local governments. Check whether your state has a law on the subject before you assume it's purely local.

**County or city land use and zoning.** Zoning decides what uses a parcel may have. A residential zone may treat short stays as a commercial lodging use and prohibit it. It may allow short stays only as an accessory use to someone's home. The city's zoning map tells you the district. The zoning code tells you what that district allows.

**Short-term rental permit or registration.** Many cities run a separate licensing program on top of zoning. This is where you find the permit category, the eligibility test, fees, caps, renewal, and the registration number you must display. Some programs sit in the planning department. Others sit in housing, finance, or a special enforcement office.

**Building, fire, and safety rules.** Building and fire codes can set occupancy limits, smoke and carbon monoxide alarm rules, and exit requirements. Some cities inspect before issuing a permit. Some programs also bar short stays in certain building types, such as rent-regulated or affordable housing.

**HOA, condo, or co-op rules.** Private rules recorded against the property can ban short rentals, even where the city allows them. Read the declaration, bylaws, and house rules. Board minutes sometimes show a ban in progress.

**Lease and landlord.** For arbitrage, this is the rung you control least. The lease must allow the use, in writing. Some cities make the landlord's written consent part of the permit application. Lesson 3.5 and the updated 5.3 addendum lesson cover the documents.

Two practical layers sit alongside these. Your insurer must agree to cover guests at this address. The booking platform must accept your registration number where the city requires one. Lesson 3.3 covers the platform side.

### 3.1.3 Hosted vs. unhosted stays

A hosted stay means the host lives in the unit and is present during the stay. Guests usually rent a room while the host is home. An unhosted stay means guests have the whole unit and nobody lives there.

Cities treat these very differently. Hosted stays look like a resident earning income from a spare room. Unhosted stays look like a hotel room carved out of the housing supply. Many cities allow the first and restrict or ban the second.

Arbitrage is almost always unhosted. You lease a whole unit and you don't live there. So when you read an ordinance, find the words that decide whether the host must be present. That one clause often settles the question before anything else.

### 3.1.4 Primary-residence rules

A primary-residence rule says you may only host in the home where you actually live. Cities usually define it by time, such as living there more than half the year. Some also require that it be the only property where you host.

For an arbitrage operator, a primary-residence rule is usually a hard stop. You can't make a unit your primary residence by signing a lease on it while you live somewhere else. Cities that use this rule often ask for proof such as a driver's license, voter registration, or tax records at that address.

### 3.1.5 Caps: nights, permits, and units

Caps come in several forms:

- A night cap limits how many nights per year you may host. Some cities cap unhosted nights and leave hosted nights uncapped.
- A permit cap limits how many permits the city issues, sometimes by neighborhood or zoning district. New applicants may join a waitlist.
- A unit cap limits how many permits one person or one building may hold.

Night caps hit arbitrage hardest. Your rent is due 12 months a year. If the city lets you host only part of the year, the math has to work on those nights alone. The worked example below shows how quickly that fails.

### 3.1.6 Minimum-stay rules

Many cities define a "short-term rental" by stay length, often under 30 nights. Stays at or above the line are treated as ordinary residential tenancies, with different rules.

Count carefully. New York City explains that under state law the check-in date is day zero. So a 29-night stay is a short-term rental and a 30-night stay is permanent occupancy ([NYC FAQ for prospective hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-prospective-hosts.page)).

Longer stays aren't a loophole. Once a guest stays past the threshold, tenant protections can apply. Depending on the state and city, that can mean eviction procedures and rent rules. Treat month-plus furnished rentals as a separate business and have an attorney review it.

### 3.1.7 Transferability: does the permit follow the operator?

Ask who the permit belongs to. Some cities issue it to the property owner. Some issue it to the host, meaning the person who lives there or runs the rental. Some tie it to both.

This matters in three situations:

- You take over a unit that "already has a permit." If the permit belongs to the previous host, it may not cover you.
- The landlord sells the building. A permit issued to the owner may end with the sale.
- You add a partner or form an LLC. Some programs require the host to be a natural person, so an entity may not qualify.

Never assume a permit transfers. Ask the licensing office in writing and keep the answer.

### 3.1.8 Two cities, two rulebooks

Here are two large cities with very different rules. I'm using them to show how much the answer changes with the address. Rules change, so check the official pages on the day you research.

**New York City (Local Law 18 registration).** The city's Office of Special Enforcement (OSE) administers it. Here's what its official pages say:

- Hosts of short-term rentals in private dwellings or Class A multiple dwellings must register with the city, or the rental is illegal. The application fee is $145, non-refundable ([OSE tips for hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/tips-for-hosts.page)).
- The host must be a natural person and the permanent occupant of the unit. The applicant must certify that a lease or other agreement doesn't prohibit short-term rentals ([OSE tips for hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/tips-for-hosts.page)).
- Short-term rentals are allowed only if the host stays in the same unit as the guests. No more than two paying guests may stay at a time. Host and guests must share a common household, and guests must have access to the whole unit. Interior doors that lock guests into their room count as a lack of access ([OSE tips for hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/tips-for-hosts.page)).
- You cannot rent out an entire apartment or home for less than 30 days, even if you own or live in the building. That applies to all permanent residential buildings ([OSE FAQ for prospective hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-prospective-hosts.page)).
- A registered host must include the registration number in every advertisement ([OSE registration rules and laws](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/registration-rules-and-laws.page)).
- Booking services such as Airbnb and Vrbo may not collect a fee for a short-term rental unless they've checked the city's electronic verification system. The check confirms a valid registration number and that the host's legal name and address match ([OSE booking services FAQ](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-booking-services.page); [Local Law 18 text](https://legistar.council.nyc.gov/View.ashx?GUID=B39F0576-95A1-425C-9005-DB2111F440AF&ID=10898489&M=F)).
- Building owners can put a building on a Prohibited Buildings List by certifying that leases there prohibit short-term rentals. OSE must deny registration for units in listed buildings ([OSE Prohibited Buildings List](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/pbl.page)).

Airbnb's [New York City page](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/868) says hosts waiting for approval may set a 30-night minimum while OSE reviews eligibility.

**Los Angeles (Home-Sharing Ordinance).** City Planning runs registration, and the housing department explains who is eligible. Here's what the official pages say:

- Home-sharing is limited to your primary residence, meaning where you live more than six months of the year. It must also be the only property where you home-share ([LA Home-Sharing FAQ](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/homesharing/FAQ.pdf); [What is the Home-Sharing Program?](https://planning.lacity.gov/blog/what-home-sharing-program)).
- Standard home-sharing allows up to 120 days per calendar year. Going past 120 requires an Extended Home-Sharing registration, which has extra criteria. One is holding a valid home-sharing registration for at least six months ([LAHD Home Sharing Ordinance](https://housing.lacity.gov/articles/home-sharing-ordinance); [LA Home-Sharing FAQ](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/1dab409a-d9cb-47f2-9a29-0348389cf752/FAQ.pdf)).
- Units covered by the Rent Stabilization Ordinance (RSO) are not eligible. That covers most multifamily units built before October 1978. Legally restricted affordable housing and certain units taken off the rental market are also excluded ([LAHD Home Sharing Ordinance](https://housing.lacity.gov/articles/home-sharing-ordinance); [Council District 4 illegal home-sharing page](https://cd4.lacity.gov/illegal-homesharing/)). The same city pages say you can check RSO status by address in the city's ZIMAS parcel tool, under the Housing tab.
- Hosting must be in a structure built for residential use, with no pending citations or violations on the property ([What is the Home-Sharing Program?](https://planning.lacity.gov/blog/what-home-sharing-program)).
- A renter must submit the landlord's written, notarized approval with the application ([Landlord's Authorization affidavit](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/b044ea0f-be5d-4da5-8144-18b0c3fad092/AFFIDAVIT.pdf); [LA Home-Sharing FAQ](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/1dab409a-d9cb-47f2-9a29-0348389cf752/FAQ.pdf)).
- Hosts must register for the city's Transient Occupancy Tax and post their registration number on every listing ([What is the Home-Sharing Program?](https://planning.lacity.gov/blog/what-home-sharing-program)).
- The city's FAQ lists an $89 registration or renewal fee. Extended Home-Sharing review costs $850 more, or $5,660 when discretionary review is required ([LA Home-Sharing FAQ](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/1dab409a-d9cb-47f2-9a29-0348389cf752/FAQ.pdf)). Confirm current fees with City Planning, since FAQ documents can lag fee updates.

Airbnb's [Los Angeles page](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/864) says stays of 1 to 29 days need a home-sharing permit on the listing before it's published. It also says the registration is valid for one year and must be renewed annually.

Compare the two. New York City allows hosted stays with the host present and two guests at most. Los Angeles allows unhosted stays in your own primary residence, with a night cap and building-type exclusions. Both cities allow some short-term renting. Neither allows the Alder Street model, and they block it for different reasons.

### 3.1.9 Worked example: Alder Street against New York City and Los Angeles

Alder Street is Unit 4B, 218 Alder Street: a hypothetical two-bedroom that sleeps 5, in a four-unit building. The student leases it and lives elsewhere. The plan is unhosted, whole-unit stays averaging 3.0 nights, at 70% occupancy (255 nights a year). All figures are assumptions from the course's canonical example. The city is unnamed on purpose. Here we drop the same unit into two real rulebooks.

**Test 1: if Alder Street were in New York City.**

| Rule | Alder Street plan | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Host is a natural person and permanent occupant | Operator lives elsewhere | Fails |
| Host present in the unit during the stay | Unhosted | Fails |
| No more than two paying guests | Sleeps 5 | Fails |
| No entire unit for less than 30 days | Whole unit, 3-night stays | Fails |
| Building not on the Prohibited Buildings List | Unknown | Check |
| Registration number on every ad; platform verifies it | Operator can't register | Fails |

Result: **prohibited** as planned. The entire-unit rule alone ends it. A 30-night-plus furnished rental is a different business under tenant law, and that falls outside this model. A hosted room with the host present isn't arbitrage.

**Test 2: if Alder Street were in Los Angeles.**

| Rule | Alder Street plan | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Primary residence (host lives there more than 6 months a year) | Operator lives elsewhere | Fails |
| Only property where the host home-shares | Would be a second property | Fails |
| Not an RSO unit | Four-unit building; RSO status depends on build date and other facts | Check in ZIMAS |
| Up to 120 days a year unless Extended | Plan is 255 nights | Fails |
| Notarized written landlord approval | Landlord offered a lease; approval not yet signed | Open |
| TOT registration and number on every listing | Not yet | Open |

Result: **prohibited** as planned. The primary-residence rule alone ends it.

Now suppose someone tried to fix it. Could they move in, make it their primary residence, and host within the standard cap? Here's what 120 nights does to the numbers, keeping every other canonical input the same:

- 120 nights at $205 = $24,600 nightly revenue
- 40 stays x $110 cleaning = $4,400
- Gross booking revenue = $29,000
- Platform fee at 15.5% = -$4,495
- Cleaner and supplies: 40 x ($95 + $14) = -$4,360
- Fixed costs (rent, utilities, insurance, software, repairs, permit) = -$30,590
- **Net = -$10,445 a year**, about -$870 a month

To break even at 120 nights, the nightly rate would need to reach about $309 (assumption: solved with python from the canonical inputs). That's roughly 50% above the $205 base rate. And the student would now be living in the unit, which changes everything else about the plan. Under a 120-night cap, the arbitrage model stops working at this rent.

**What the example teaches.** The same unit, the same lease, the same math, gets two different "no" answers for two different reasons. In the unnamed city where Alder Street actually sits, you still don't know the answer. You find it by working the ladder in 3.1.10 at the real address.

### 3.1.10 The permission flow, in words

Here's the flow I want you to follow for every address. Picture it as a vertical chain of boxes, each with a "stop" exit on the right side.

1. **Write down the exact plan.** Address, unit, parcel number, hosted or unhosted, maximum guests, typical stay length, nights per year. Every answer depends on these facts.
2. **Identify every jurisdiction.** Is the address inside city limits or unincorporated county? Is it in a special district? Many students check the wrong city because of a mailing address. Use the city or county parcel lookup.
3. **Find the official ordinance.** Use the city's code and the licensing program's own page. Airbnb's city pages and regulation overview can point you there. A blog post, a Facebook group, or another host's opinion is not a source. Stop exit: the ordinance prohibits your model outright.
4. **Check the parcel and building.** Zoning district, building type, rent-regulation or affordable-housing status, and any building-level list like New York City's Prohibited Buildings List. Stop exit: the district or building type is excluded.
5. **Check private rules.** HOA, condo, or co-op documents and the lease. Stop exit: a recorded ban or a lease that prohibits the use.
6. **Check permit mechanics.** Category, eligibility, caps, waitlist, fees, renewal, transferability, required local contact, safety inspection, and the rule for displaying the number. Stop exit: you can't meet eligibility, or the cap is full.
7. **If anything is unclear, ask the licensing office in writing.** Describe the plan in the terms from step 1. Ask a specific yes-or-no question. Save the reply with the date and the name of the person who answered.
8. **Preserve the evidence.** Save a PDF or dated screenshot of each official page, the ordinance section, the zoning map, and every written answer. Put them in the deal folder with the SR.1 checklist.
9. **Record a decision.** Permitted, prohibited, or unresolved, with a recheck date. Unresolved means you do not sign a lease yet.

Sam's 3.2 lesson runs this flow live on a real address. Watch how he finds the official source and where he stops trusting summaries.

### 3.1.11 Exercise: the permission ladder

Fill this in for one real address. Use the SR.1 regulatory compliance checklist if you want the full version. Every "Yes" needs a source link and a date.

**Plan facts**

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Full address and unit | |
| Parcel number | |
| Jurisdiction (city / unincorporated county / special district) | |
| Hosted or unhosted | |
| Maximum guests | |
| Typical stay length (nights) | |
| Planned nights per year | |
| Who will be the named host or permit holder | |

**The ladder**

| Rung | Question | Yes / No / Unclear | Official source URL | Date checked | Written answer saved? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| State | Does a state law restrict or register STRs here? | | | | |
| Zoning | Does this district allow the planned use? | | | | |
| Permit category | Is there a category my plan fits? | | | | |
| Hosted / unhosted | Is unhosted use allowed? | | | | |
| Primary residence | Is a primary-residence test required? Can I meet it? | | | | |
| Night cap | Is there a cap? Does my plan fit under it? | | | | |
| Permit cap / waitlist | Are permits available now? | | | | |
| Minimum stay | What stay length is "short-term"? How is the check-in day counted? | | | | |
| Building type | Is the building excluded (rent-regulated, affordable, listed)? | | | | |
| Safety / inspection | What inspections or equipment are required? | | | | |
| HOA / condo / co-op | Do recorded rules allow it? | | | | |
| Lease | Does the lease or signed addendum allow it? Does the city require landlord consent on the application? | | | | |
| Transferability | Who holds the permit? Does it survive a sale or a change of operator? | | | | |
| Number display | Where must the number appear? Does the platform verify it? | | | | |

**Decision:** Permitted / Prohibited / Unresolved. Recheck date: ______. Reviewer: ______.

A single "No" on any rung means prohibited for this plan. "Unclear" on any rung means unresolved. Don't sign a lease on an unresolved address.

### 3.1.12 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the city or county licensing office, in writing. Ask whether your exact plan fits a permit category at this address, and who the permit holder must be.

**Confirm with:** the zoning or planning department that the parcel's zoning district allows the use.

**Confirm with:** an attorney licensed in the state. They confirm that the lease and any addendum allow the use, and whether stays of 30 nights or more create a tenancy.

**Confirm with:** the HOA, condo, or co-op board, in writing, if the property has one.

**Confirm with:** the landlord, in a signed document, that you may use the address on a permit application. Some cities require a specific form, as Los Angeles does with its notarized affidavit.

**Confirm with:** an insurer, that the policy covers short-term guests at this address under the permit category you'll hold.

### 3.1.13 Next lesson

Next is Sam's 3.2, How to Research Regulations Before You Sign Anything. He runs the permission flow live on a real address. Bring back three things: the official ordinance link, the permit category that fits your plan, and any question you still need to send to the licensing office. Then come back for 3.3, Occupancy Taxes & Platform-Verified Registration. There we cover tax registration, who collects which tax on each channel, and how your permit number gets verified on the listing.

### 3.1 sources

- [Airbnb: Learn about hosting regulations](https://www.airbnb.com/resources/hosting-homes/a/learn-about-hosting-regulations-7): many cities require registration, a permit, or a license; zoning and taxes may apply. Starting point only. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- [NYC OSE: Tips for hosting a legal short-term rental](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/tips-for-hosts.page): registration required; $145 fee; natural person and permanent occupant; lease certification; host present; no more than two guests; common household. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to nyc.gov (direct fetch blocked).
- [NYC OSE: FAQ for prospective hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-prospective-hosts.page): no entire unit under 30 days; check-in date is day zero. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to nyc.gov.
- [NYC OSE: Registration rules and laws](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/registration-rules-and-laws.page): registration number in every advertisement. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to nyc.gov (search summary).
- [NYC OSE: FAQ for booking services](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-booking-services.page) and [Local Law 18 of 2022 text](https://legistar.council.nyc.gov/View.ashx?GUID=B39F0576-95A1-425C-9005-DB2111F440AF&ID=10898489&M=F): booking services must check the electronic verification system before collecting fees; name and address must match. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to nyc.gov.
- [NYC OSE: Prohibited Buildings List](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/pbl.page): owners can list buildings; OSE must deny registration there. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to nyc.gov.
- [Airbnb: New York, NY](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/868): 30-night minimum while awaiting OSE approval; add registration details once registered. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- [LAHD: Home Sharing Ordinance](https://housing.lacity.gov/articles/home-sharing-ordinance): primary residence; 120-day standard limit; RSO exclusion. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to lacity.gov (direct fetch blocked).
- [LA City Planning: What is the Home-Sharing Program?](https://planning.lacity.gov/blog/what-home-sharing-program): six-month primary residence; eligibility list including TOT registration and number on every listing. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to planning.lacity.gov.
- [LA Home-Sharing FAQ](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/1dab409a-d9cb-47f2-9a29-0348389cf752/FAQ.pdf) and [earlier FAQ](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/homesharing/FAQ.pdf): sole property definition; $89, $850, $5,660 fees; notarized landlord approval; Extended Home-Sharing six-month registration criterion. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to planning.lacity.gov.
- [LA Landlord's Authorization to Conduct Home-Sharing affidavit](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/b044ea0f-be5d-4da5-8144-18b0c3fad092/AFFIDAVIT.pdf): landlord consent form exists. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (title only).

- [Airbnb: Los Angeles, CA](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/864): permit needed for 1-29 day stays before publishing; one-year registration; annual renewal. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.

<a id="lesson-3.3-occupancy-taxes-registration"></a>
## 3.3  Occupancy Taxes & Platform-Verified Registration

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part One: Airbnb Arbitrage Roadmap 2.0 / Module 3 - STR Regulations & Compliance |
| Code | 3.3 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | 3.1 (permission layers); Sam's 3.2 (live regulation search); 3.4 Red Flags; Sam's 9.11/9.12 (direct booking and Guesty); 9.8 Bookkeeping and 9.9 Taxes & Working With a CPA (updated lessons); resource SR.1 |

### 3.3.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can build a channel-by-channel tax worksheet for one address. It shows which lodging taxes apply, who collects and remits each one on Airbnb, Vrbo, and direct bookings, and what you still owe. You'll know how to enter and maintain a permit or registration number on a listing, and what happens when it lapses. And you can reconcile a quarter of bookings against your tax filings before the agency finds the gap.

### 3.3.1 Three registrations people mix up

Separate three things students blur together. Each has its own agency, number, and deadline.

**The operating permit or registration.** This is permission to run a short-term rental at the address. The city's licensing or planning office issues it. It comes with a number you display on listings. Lesson 3.1 covered how to get it.

**The tax registration.** This is an account with each tax agency that collects lodging tax from you. It comes with its own account number and a filing schedule. Having a permit doesn't open a tax account. Having a tax account doesn't give you a permit. Los Angeles, for example, lists Transient Occupancy Tax registration as its own eligibility step for home-sharing hosts ([LA City Planning](https://planning.lacity.gov/blog/what-home-sharing-program)).

**Federal income tax.** This is tax on your profit, reported to the IRS once a year. Occupancy tax is money the guest pays. Income tax is money you pay on what's left. The IRS explains in [Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527) that rental income normally goes on Schedule E. If you provide substantial services for the guest's convenience, such as regular cleaning or changing linens, it may go on Schedule C instead. Your CPA decides which applies. Lesson 9.9 covers working with a CPA. This lesson stays on occupancy taxes.

### 3.3.2 The tax layers

Lodging taxes stack. One stay can carry several:

- State: a state sales tax that applies to lodging, a state hotel occupancy tax, or both.
- County: a county lodging or tourism tax.
- City: a city transient occupancy tax, often the largest local piece.
- Special districts: tourism improvement districts, convention center districts, or similar. Some charge a percentage. Some charge a flat amount per occupied night.

Each layer can have its own agency, its own form, and its own due date. Sometimes one agency collects several layers on a single return. Sometimes you file separately with each one.

The rules for each layer differ in four ways you must check:

- Taxable stay length. Most lodging taxes apply to stays under a set number of nights. Texas, for example, defines a short-term rental for its hotel tax as 29 days or less ([Texas Comptroller hotel tax FAQ](https://comptroller.texas.gov/taxes/hotel/faq.php)). Your city's line may differ.
- What's in the taxable base. Nightly rate, cleaning fee, pet fee, extra-guest fee. Airbnb says that where it collects, cleaning fees are included in the taxable base ([Airbnb help 1036](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1036)). Your city's own rules decide what you must include on bookings you collect yourself.
- Exemptions. Some agencies exempt certain guests or stays, and they publish the list. Texas publishes [its hotel tax exemptions](https://comptroller.texas.gov/taxes/publications/96-224.php). Don't apply an exemption unless the agency's own document says it applies and you keep the paperwork it asks for.
- Filing frequency and zero returns. Monthly, quarterly, or annually, depending on the agency and sometimes on your volume. Many agencies expect a return every period once you're registered, even when you owe nothing. Texas, for example, requires "No Tax Due" hotel tax reports to be filed electronically ([Texas Comptroller hotel tax FAQ](https://comptroller.texas.gov/taxes/hotel/faq.php)). A missed zero return can bring a penalty in some jurisdictions.

### 3.3.3 Who collects on Airbnb

Airbnb collects and remits some occupancy taxes automatically in some places. It lists those places in its help center ([Airbnb help 2509](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2509)). The system decides which taxes apply from the listing address you entered, so an address error can mean the wrong taxes ([Airbnb help 2509](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2509)).

The part people miss is the gap. Airbnb says that even where it collects some taxes, you may still need to collect others. Its example is Airbnb collecting regional taxes but not local ones. Hosts in those areas remain responsible for assessing all other obligations ([Airbnb help 2509](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2509); [Airbnb help 1036](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1036)).

For taxes Airbnb doesn't collect, you have two choices. You can collect them yourself outside the platform. Or you may be able to use Airbnb's custom tax tool, if you're eligible. It lets you set a tax as a percentage per booking, or as a fee per guest, per night, or per guest per night. You also choose what it applies to ([Airbnb help 2523](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2523)). Airbnb charges those taxes to the guest and pays them to you separately from your booking payout. You remain responsible for the amount, the filing, and the payment ([Airbnb help 2523](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2523)).

Airbnb also says that whether or not it collects taxes, your payout is the same: your price minus Airbnb's service fee ([Airbnb help 1036](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1036)). The tax sits on top.

### 3.3.4 Who collects on Vrbo

Vrbo works on a similar split. Where Vrbo is liable for a lodging tax, it collects and remits that tax on a return filed under HomeAway.com, Inc.'s taxpayer account. Where it isn't, the owner or manager is responsible for collecting and remitting ([Vrbo: collection and remittance of taxes](https://help.vrbo.com/articles/What-Stay-Taxes-Lodging-Taxes-does-HomeAway-collect-and-remit)). Vrbo publishes the jurisdictions where it collects by state ([Vrbo tax by jurisdiction](https://help.vrbo.com/articles/Tax-by-jurisdiction)).

For the taxes you owe, Vrbo describes three ways to handle them ([Vrbo: manage your lodging taxes](https://help.vrbo.com/articles/How-can-I-manage-my-lodging-taxes)):

1. Collect, file, and pay them yourself outside the platform.
2. Have Vrbo collect them from travelers and send them to you with your payout. You still file and pay.
3. Have Vrbo collect them with Avalara MyLodgeTax preparing, filing, and paying. Vrbo describes this as available at no extra cost.

Vrbo says it always collects and remits where the law requires it, whichever option you pick. Under option 2, the tax lands in your bank account and still belongs to the agency.

### 3.3.5 Direct bookings

On a direct booking, nobody collects for you. You quote the tax, charge it, hold it, report it, and pay it for every layer. Your booking engine or PMS needs the correct rates and base for each layer. Sam's 9.11 and 9.12 lessons show the direct-booking and Guesty setup. Before your first direct booking, confirm the tax settings in that tool against your worksheet.

The Texas Comptroller spells out this split plainly. Owners must collect and remit state hotel tax on rentals through their own website or a platform that doesn't collect. When a collecting platform handles state tax, the owner should still contact the county and city about local hotel tax ([Texas Comptroller hotel tax FAQ](https://comptroller.texas.gov/taxes/hotel/faq.php)). That's one state's rule. The pattern is common, though: platform collection often covers some layers, not all.

### 3.3.6 Entering and maintaining your registration number

Where a city requires a permit or registration number, the platform usually has a field for it. On Airbnb you add it under the listing's regulations settings. Go to the listing, find the laws and regulations section, and add the permit number ([Airbnb help 3953](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3953)). Some cities let a listing show "City registration pending" while an application is in process. Others allow "Exempt" for categories the city exempts, such as a hotel ([Airbnb help 1634](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1634)). Only use those statuses when the city's rules allow them for your listing.

In some cities the platform checks the number against the city's records. New York City is the clearest case. A booking service may not collect a fee for a short-term rental unless it has checked the city's electronic verification system. The check confirms the registration number is valid and the host's legal name and address match ([NYC OSE booking services FAQ](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-booking-services.page)). Airbnb's New York City page says hosts waiting for approval may set a 30-night minimum in the meantime ([Airbnb help 868](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/868)).

Numbers expire. Airbnb's Los Angeles page says the Home-Sharing Registration is valid for one year and must be renewed annually. The renewal window opens 30 days before expiration. If the registration lapses, you must apply again and enter the new number on the listing ([Airbnb help 864](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/864)). Failing to renew may affect your ability to host.

What happens when a number fails verification or expires depends on the city and the platform's arrangement with it. In a city like New York, a platform may not be able to take a booking without a verified number. So treat a lapse as lost revenue plus a compliance problem. Put every expiration date on your calendar with a reminder 60 days out (assumption: my suggested buffer). File the renewal as soon as the window opens.

Keep the name and address identical on the permit, the tax accounts, and the listing, down to the unit number. Save a dated screenshot of the listing showing the number after every renewal.

### 3.3.7 Reconciling booked revenue to tax reports

Reconciliation means proving that the tax you filed matches the stays you hosted. You do it every filing period, before you file. It catches four common errors. A direct booking gets the wrong rate. A tax layer gets left off. A cancellation stays in taxable receipts. Or platform-remitted receipts get reported as if you collected them.

You need three records for the period:

1. Your reservation report from the PMS: every stay, channel, nights, check-in date, nightly revenue, cleaning fee, status (completed, cancelled, refunded).
2. Each platform's earnings or transaction report, which shows payouts and any tax lines paid out to you.
3. Your tax worksheet, showing each layer's rate, base, and who collects it on each channel.

The steps:

1. Start from the reservation report. Remove cancelled stays with full refunds. Adjust partially refunded stays to what the guest actually paid.
2. Split taxable receipts by channel.
3. For each layer, separate the receipts where a platform remitted the tax from the receipts where you owe it.
4. Compute tax owed by layer on your share.
5. Add up the tax you actually collected by layer: platform tax payouts, Vrbo pass-throughs, and direct-booking tax charged.
6. Compare owed with collected. Explain every difference.

A shortfall usually means you forgot to charge something. You owe it anyway, out of your own pocket. An overage usually means you charged a guest tax that didn't apply. Ask the agency how to handle it. Some returns also ask you to report gross receipts including platform-remitted stays, then deduct them. Follow the form's instructions for each agency.

### 3.3.8 Worked example: Alder Street tax worksheet

Alder Street is Unit 4B, 218 Alder Street, a hypothetical two-bedroom in an unnamed city. Its canonical numbers leave occupancy taxes out of revenue and expenses, because guests pay them and they pass through. This worksheet shows what that pass-through looks like.

**Every rate, rule, and collection arrangement below is an assumption for teaching.** They describe a made-up jurisdiction, "Alder City (hypothetical)." They are not any real city's rates. Before you use a worksheet like this, replace each row with the official agency's rate, its link, and the date you checked it.

**The hypothetical tax layers (all assumptions)**

| Layer | Rate | Base | Taxable stays | Filed with | Frequency |
|---|---:|---|---|---|---|
| State lodging tax | 6.0% | Nightly + cleaning | 29 nights or fewer | State revenue department | Quarterly |
| County lodging tax | 2.0% | Nightly + cleaning | 29 nights or fewer | County treasurer | Quarterly |
| City transient occupancy tax | 5.0% | Nightly + cleaning | 29 nights or fewer | City finance office | Monthly |
| Tourism district assessment | $2.00 per occupied night | Nights | 29 nights or fewer | City finance office, on the city return | Monthly |

**Who collects, by channel (all assumptions)**

| Layer | Airbnb | Vrbo | Direct |
|---|---|---|---|
| State 6% | Airbnb collects and remits | Vrbo collects and remits | Operator |
| County 2% | Airbnb collects and remits | Operator (Vrbo collects and passes through) | Operator |
| City 5% | Operator (custom tax tool) | Operator (Vrbo collects and passes through) | Operator |
| District $2/night | Operator (custom tax tool) | Operator (Vrbo collects and passes through) | Operator |

**Channel mix (assumption).** The canonical base case has 85 stays of 3 nights, 255 nights, at $205 plus a $110 cleaning fee. Split those stays: 64 on Airbnb, 13 on Vrbo, 8 direct. The canonical A1 model charges the 15.5% Airbnb fee on all revenue. Vrbo and direct fees differ, but we don't re-run A1 here. This split only drives the tax worksheet.

**Step 1: taxable receipts by channel.**

| Channel | Stays | Nights | Nightly ($205 x nights) | Cleaning ($110 x stays) | Taxable receipts |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Airbnb | 64 | 192 | $39,360 | $7,040 | $46,400 |
| Vrbo | 13 | 39 | $7,995 | $1,430 | $9,425 |
| Direct | 8 | 24 | $4,920 | $880 | $5,800 |
| **Total** | **85** | **255** | **$52,275** | **$9,350** | **$61,625** |

The total matches canonical gross booking revenue.

**Step 2: tax by layer and channel, for the year.**

| Channel | State 6% | County 2% | City 5% | District $2/night | Total tax |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Airbnb | $2,784.00 | $928.00 | $2,320.00 | $384.00 | $6,416.00 |
| Vrbo | $565.50 | $188.50 | $471.25 | $78.00 | $1,303.25 |
| Direct | $348.00 | $116.00 | $290.00 | $48.00 | $802.00 |
| **Total** | **$3,697.50** | **$1,232.50** | **$3,081.25** | **$510.00** | **$8,521.25** |

Guests pay about $8,521 in occupancy taxes on $61,625 of bookings, roughly 13.8% on top. For one typical stay: $725 x 13% = $94.25, plus 3 nights x $2 = $6. That's $100.25 of tax on a $725 stay.

**Step 3: who remits what.**

| Layer | Remitted by platforms | Remitted by operator |
|---|---:|---:|
| State | $3,349.50 (Airbnb $2,784.00 + Vrbo $565.50) | $348.00 (direct) |
| County | $928.00 (Airbnb) | $304.50 (Vrbo $188.50 + direct $116.00) |
| City | $0 | $3,081.25 (all channels) |
| District | $0 | $510.00 (all channels) |
| **Total** | **$4,277.50** | **$4,243.75** |

Half the tax flows through the operator's own accounts in this setup. That's with Airbnb carrying 75% of the stays. A student who assumes "Airbnb handles taxes" would miss $4,243.75 of filings. That includes every dollar of the city and district tax.

**Step 4: a quarterly reconciliation.** Take one quarter (assumption: 24 stays of 3 nights at $205, split 17 Airbnb, 4 Vrbo, 3 direct). One direct stay cancelled with a full refund. On the two remaining direct stays, the operator's booking tool charged state, county, and city tax but was missing the district fee.

*Receipts:*

| Channel | Stays kept | Taxable receipts | Note |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Airbnb | 17 | $12,325 | 51 nights |
| Vrbo | 4 | $2,900 | 12 nights |
| Direct | 2 | $1,450 | 3 booked, 1 refunded ($725 removed) |
| **Total** | **23** | **$16,675** | 69 nights |

*Platform-remitted this quarter (not on the operator's returns as tax owed):* Airbnb state $739.50 and county $246.50. Vrbo state $174.00.

*Tax the operator owes, by layer:*

| Layer | Calculation | Owed |
|---|---|---:|
| State | Direct $1,450 x 6% | $87.00 |
| County | (Vrbo $2,900 + direct $1,450) x 2% | $87.00 |
| City | $16,675 x 5% | $833.75 |
| District | 69 nights x $2 | $138.00 |
| **Total owed** | | **$1,145.75** |

*Tax the operator actually collected:*

| Source | Layers | Collected |
|---|---|---:|
| Airbnb custom tax payout | City $616.25 + district $102.00 | $718.25 |
| Vrbo pass-through | County $58.00 + city $145.00 + district $24.00 | $227.00 |
| Direct bookings | State $87.00 + county $29.00 + city $72.50 | $188.50 |
| **Total collected** | | **$1,133.75** |

*Variance:* $1,145.75 owed - $1,133.75 collected = **$12.00 short**. That's the district fee on the two direct stays: 2 stays x 3 nights x $2. The operator pays the $12 from operating cash and fixes the booking-tool setting before the next direct booking.

Twelve dollars is small. The same mistake across several units and years isn't, and neither is a whole missed layer like the $3,081 of city tax in Step 3.

*A check on the Airbnb payout:* $12,325 of bookings less the 15.5% service fee ($1,910.38) is a booking payout of $10,414.62. The $718.25 of custom taxes arrives separately. If your bank deposits and the platform report don't reconcile to those two figures, find out why before you file.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### 3.3.9 Exercise: channel-by-channel tax worksheet

Fill this in for one real address. Every rate needs an official link and a date, or the word "unresolved".

**Part A: registrations (keep them separate)**

| Registration | Agency | Account or permit number | Issued | Expires / renews | Filing frequency | Official URL | Date checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| STR permit / registration | | | | | n/a | | |
| State tax account | | | | | | | |
| County tax account | | | | | | | |
| City tax account | | | | | | | |
| District tax account (if any) | | | | | | | |
| Federal income tax treatment | CPA decides | n/a | | | Annual | IRS Pub 527 | |

**Part B: layers**

| Layer | Rate or flat fee | Taxable base (nightly, cleaning, other fees) | Taxable stay length | Exemptions that apply | Zero return required? | Official URL | Date checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| State | | | | | | | |
| County | | | | | | | |
| City | | | | | | | |
| District | | | | | | | |

**Part C: who collects**

| Layer | Airbnb (collects / operator via custom tool / operator outside) | Vrbo (collects / pass-through / operator) | Direct | Source (help page for this address, date) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| State | | | Operator | |
| County | | | Operator | |
| City | | | Operator | |
| District | | | Operator | |

**Part D: quarterly reconciliation**

| Line | Airbnb | Vrbo | Direct | Total |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Stays completed | | | | |
| Nights | | | | |
| Taxable receipts (after refunds) | | | | |
| Tax remitted by platform, by layer | | | n/a | |
| Tax owed by operator, by layer | | | | |
| Tax collected by operator, by layer | | | | |
| Variance (owed - collected) | | | | |
| Explanation of variance | | | | |

**Part E: registration number checks**

| Check | Status | Date |
|---|---|---|
| Number entered on every listing, exactly as issued | | |
| Name and address match permit, tax accounts, listing | | |
| Expiration date on calendar with 60-day reminder | | |
| Dated screenshot of listing saved | | |
| Platform verification status (if the city verifies) | | |

### 3.3.10 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** each tax agency (state, county, city, district), in writing or on its official site. Confirm the rate, taxable base, taxable stay length, exemptions, filing frequency, and zero-return rule.

**Confirm with:** each platform's help page for your exact address, on the day you set up the listing, which taxes it collects and remits there. Recheck when you renew your permit, because collection agreements change.

**Confirm with:** the city licensing office, how the permit number must appear, whether platforms verify it, and what happens to bookings if it lapses.

**Confirm with:** a CPA, on three points. How collected occupancy taxes and platform fees appear in your books. Whether your rental income belongs on Schedule E or Schedule C. Your estimated income tax payments. Lesson 9.9 covers that relationship.

**Confirm with:** your bookkeeper or PMS vendor, that the direct-booking tax settings match your worksheet before the first direct reservation. Lesson 9.8 covers bookkeeping.

### 3.3.11 Next lesson

Next is 3.4, Red Flags: Markets, Cities & Leases to Avoid in 2026. You now have the permission ladder from 3.1, Sam's live search from 3.2, and the tax worksheet from this lesson. Lesson 3.4 turns them into a fast reject, investigate, or advance screen, so you can drop a bad address in minutes. Bring your completed worksheet.

### 3.3 sources

- [Airbnb help 2509: Areas where tax collection and remittance by Airbnb is available](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2509): collection areas; taxes determined by listing address; hosts may still owe other taxes. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com (direct fetch blocked).
- [Airbnb help 1036: How tax collection and remittance by Airbnb works](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1036): cleaning fees in the taxable base where Airbnb collects; hosts responsible for other taxes; payout unchanged. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- [Airbnb help 2523: Adding taxes to a home listing](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2523): custom tax tool, collection methods, separate payout, host responsibility. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- [Airbnb help 3953: Add rental permits to your listing](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3953): where to add a permit number. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- [Airbnb help 1634: "Pending" or "exempt" registration numbers](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1634): pending and exempt statuses. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- [Airbnb help 864: Los Angeles, CA](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/864): one-year registration; 30-day renewal window; lapse requires a new application and new number. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- [Airbnb help 868: New York, NY](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/868): 30-night minimum while awaiting approval. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to airbnb.com.
- [NYC OSE: FAQ for booking services](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-booking-services.page): booking services must check the verification system before collecting fees; name and address match. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to nyc.gov.
- [Vrbo: Collection and remittance of taxes and lodging taxes](https://help.vrbo.com/articles/What-Stay-Taxes-Lodging-Taxes-does-HomeAway-collect-and-remit): Vrbo remits under HomeAway.com, Inc.'s account where liable; owners responsible otherwise. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to help.vrbo.com.
- [Vrbo: Manage your lodging taxes](https://help.vrbo.com/articles/How-can-I-manage-my-lodging-taxes): three handling options including pass-through and Avalara MyLodgeTax. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to help.vrbo.com.
- [Vrbo: Tax by jurisdiction](https://help.vrbo.com/articles/Tax-by-jurisdiction): jurisdiction lists. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (title and URL).
- [Texas Comptroller: Hotel occupancy tax FAQ](https://comptroller.texas.gov/taxes/hotel/faq.php): 29-day definition; owner collects on own site and non-collecting platforms; contact county and city for local tax; "No Tax Due" reports filed electronically. Real-world illustration only. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to comptroller.texas.gov.
- [Texas Comptroller: Hotel occupancy tax exemptions](https://comptroller.texas.gov/taxes/publications/96-224.php): example of a published exemption list. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (title and URL).
- [LA City Planning: What is the Home-Sharing Program?](https://planning.lacity.gov/blog/what-home-sharing-program): TOT registration is a separate eligibility step. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to planning.lacity.gov.
- [IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527): Schedule E normally; Schedule C when substantial services are provided. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch restricted to irs.gov.
- Alder Street base figures: `reference/recurring-examples.md` (A1). All tax rates, collection arrangements, channel mix, and quarterly figures are hypothetical assumptions computed with python on 2026-09-25.
