<a id="lesson-6.5-local-property-manager"></a>
## 6.5  When to Bring In a Local Property Manager

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part One, Airbnb Arbitrage Roadmap 2.0 / Module 6 (Your local team) |
| Code | 6.5 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Lessons 6.1 Cleaners, 6.2 Handyman, 6.3 Neighbors, 6.4 Stagers & Interior Designers; 12.x Co-Hosting Masterclass; Preston's 5.7 (local contact promises); Module 7 next |

### 6.5.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you can decide which of four local-help setups fits a unit: cleaner only, cleaner plus an on-call vendor, a co-host, or a full manager. You'll compare each one's cost against the net cash flow it leaves you, in both the base and stress cases. You'll also have a contract and vetting checklist, and you'll know which Airbnb permissions to give and which controls to keep.

### 6.5.1 The jobs that need a local person

Remote operation works because most of the business is digital. Pricing, messaging, bookkeeping, and listing changes happen from anywhere. But some jobs need someone who can physically reach the unit.

| Task | How often at Alder Street | How fast it needs a response | Who can usually do it |
|---|---|---|---|
| Turnover cleaning | 85 times a year, about 7 a month | Same day, between checkout and check-in | Cleaner (lesson 6.1) |
| Supply restocks | Most turnovers | Before next check-in | Cleaner, if paid to restock |
| Maintenance access | A few times a month (assumption) | Hours to days | Handyman (lesson 6.2) or on-call vendor |
| Emergencies (leak, lockout, alarm, no heat) | Rare but certain | Within an hour or two, any time | On-call vendor, co-host, or manager |
| Inspections | Monthly (assumption) | Scheduled | Cleaner, vendor, or co-host |
| Guest issues needing a visit | Occasional | Same day, often evenings | Co-host or manager |

The turnover count comes from the canonical example: 255 booked nights at a 3-night average stay is 85 stays a year. Divided by 12, that's about 7.1 cleans a month.

Look at the response column. The cleaner covers the scheduled work. What remote operators struggle with is the unscheduled work: the 11 p.m. lockout, the leak on a Sunday, the guest who can't get the heat on. That's the gap local help fills. It's also what you promised the landlord in lesson 5.7: a named local contact who answers day and night.

### 6.5.2 The four setups

Here are the four levels of local help, from lightest to heaviest.

**Cleaner only.** You run everything remotely. Your cleaner turns the unit and reports problems. When something breaks, you find and dispatch a vendor yourself. This is the Alder Street base case. It works when you live within reach, or when you're very good at remote dispatch and the unit is simple.

**Cleaner plus on-call vendor.** You add a local handyman or small service company on a monthly retainer. They agree to respond within a set time, do a monthly inspection, and handle access for repairs. You still run guests, pricing, and the platform.

**Co-host.** A local person or company takes on a defined share of the hosting: guest messaging, in-person issues, cleaner coordination, sometimes pricing. On Airbnb, a co-host gets account permissions on your listing. You pay a percentage or a fixed amount per booking.

**Full manager.** A property management company runs the whole operation: guests, cleaners, maintenance, often pricing and listing. You approve the budget and review performance. Fees are usually a percentage of revenue.

Each step up removes more of your time and more of your response obligation. Each step up also costs more, and in arbitrage every dollar of fee comes out of a margin you share with the landlord's rent.

### 6.5.3 When to bring in help: the threshold

Use five tests. If two or more are true, you need at least an on-call vendor. If you're still failing them after adding one, move up a level.

1. Distance: you can't reach the unit within two hours (assumption) at night.
2. Missed responses: in the last 90 days, a guest waited longer than your promised response time for an in-person fix more than once.
3. Your hours: you're spending more hours a month on the unit than you planned, and it's crowding out finding the next deal.
4. Written promises: your lease addendum, the city permit, or your own house rules require a local contact you don't have. Some cities require a local contact as a permit condition. Check your SR.1 research.
5. Volume: you're adding units in the same area. Help that is expensive for one unit may be efficient across three.

The tests are about response risk and your time. None of them says "hire a manager because hosting feels stressful." Stress is real, but it doesn't tell you which level of help fixes it.

### 6.5.4 What you keep no matter who you hire

Local help can take tasks off your plate. Certain controls stay with you because you carry the consequences.

You keep bank and payout ownership. Revenue flows to your account. A helper gets paid from it, not the other way around.

You keep platform ownership. The listing sits in your account. If the relationship ends, you remove the helper and keep your reviews, calendar, and history.

You keep compliance decisions. Permits, tax registrations, occupancy limits, and lease obligations are yours. A manager can remind you of a renewal date. You decide and you sign.

You keep pricing approval. A helper can recommend rates or run a pricing tool, but you set the floor, the ceiling, and minimum stays. Sam's 9.10 and 9.13 lessons cover the pricing side.

You keep performance review. Every month you look at occupancy, nightly rate, response times, cleaning scores, and complaints. The helper reports; you judge.

### 6.5.5 Airbnb co-host permissions and payouts

If you choose a co-host, set up Airbnb access deliberately. Airbnb offers different co-host permission levels. A co-host with full access can see messages, the calendar, and transaction history. They can also manage the listing and change other co-hosts' permissions ([Airbnb Help, article 1534](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534); [Airbnb Co-Host Additional Terms](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3264)). Lower levels limit access to the calendar and messaging. You can change a co-host's permissions at any time ([Airbnb Help, article 1534](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534)).

Give the least access the job needs. A co-host who only handles guest messages and in-person issues may not need full access. Full access includes the power to change other co-hosts' permissions, so treat it like handing over your keys.

Payouts are the second control. Airbnb says only the listing owner can set up payouts for a co-host, and co-hosts can't set up payouts for themselves ([Airbnb Help, article 3389](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3389)). The listing owner can set a percentage per booking, with or without the cleaning fee, or a fixed amount per booking. Airbnb calculates co-host percentage payouts from the host's earnings on each booking ([Airbnb Help, article 3389](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3389)). That basis matters, as you'll see in the worked example. Regional limits may apply.

Responsibility doesn't transfer. Airbnb's co-host terms say the host alone is responsible for choosing co-hosts and managing their access. The host stays responsible for a co-host's actions taken before removal ([Airbnb Co-Host Additional Terms](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3264); [Airbnb Help, article 1534](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534)). If a co-host breaks a rule, the listing and account at risk are yours.

**Confirm with:** Airbnb's current help pages for the permission levels and payout options available in your region before you set up a co-host.

### 6.5.6 Contract and vetting checklist

Whoever you hire, the agreement should answer these questions in writing.

| Item | What to pin down | Red flag |
|---|---|---|
| Service area | Exact addresses covered | "We cover the metro area" with no named zone |
| Coverage hours | Days and hours, including nights and holidays | Business hours only, with emergencies "best effort" |
| Response targets | Time to acknowledge, time on site, by issue type | No numbers |
| Fees | Percentage or flat fee, and the base it's calculated on | Base left undefined |
| Markup | Whether they add a percentage to vendor bills or supplies | Markup not disclosed |
| Authorization limits | Dollar amount they can spend without your approval | No limit, or "as needed" |
| Backup coverage | Who covers when they're sick or away | One person, no backup |
| Insurance | Their general liability and workers' compensation, with certificates | Can't produce a certificate |
| Reporting | Monthly report contents and due date; photo proof of turnovers | Reports "on request" |
| Data and access | Who holds codes, guest data, platform logins | Wants your platform password |
| Termination | Notice period, handback of keys, codes, and records | Long lock-in or fees to leave |
| Licensing | Whether local law requires a license to manage short-term rentals for others | They don't know |

The licensing row needs care. Some states regulate people who manage rentals for someone else. Tennessee, for example, licenses Vacation Lodging Services. These businesses manage, market, book, and rent units owned by others for stays of up to 14 days. Each needs a firm license and a designated agent license ([Tennessee Real Estate Commission, Vacation Lodging Service](https://www.tn.gov/commerce/regboards/trec/license/license-how-to/vacation-lodging-service.html)). Other states handle this under general real estate or property management licensing. Some have no specific rule. Arbitrage adds a wrinkle: you lease the unit, so the helper works for a tenant, not the owner. Whether that changes the licensing answer depends on the state.

Vet before you sign. Ask for two references from current clients with similar units, their certificate of insurance, a sample monthly report, and their fee schedule in writing. Then run a test: ask how they'd handle a lockout at 11 p.m. on a Saturday. A specific answer with names and times is a good sign.

**Confirm with:** the state real estate commission or licensing board whether the helper needs a license for these services. Ask an attorney if the answer is unclear.

### 6.5.7 Worked example: Alder Street under four setups

Start with the canonical base case (A1). Alder Street books 255 nights a year at a $205 ADR, with 85 stays. Gross booking revenue is $61,625. Subtract the platform fee, cleaner, utilities, supplies, insurance, software, repairs, permit, and $23,400 in rent. Net before income tax is $12,218 a year, or $1,018 a month. The cleaner is already paid $95 per turnover in these numbers.

Now add each level of help. All fee rates and hours below are assumptions unless marked canonical. Every figure was computed with python from the canonical model.

**Setup 1: cleaner only (canonical A1).**

- Added cost: $0
- Net: $12,218 a year, $1,018 a month

**Setup 2: cleaner plus on-call vendor.**

Assume a $150 monthly retainer that includes one inspection and on-call response, plus a $600 yearly budget for billed call-outs.

- Retainer: $150 × 12 = $1,800
- Call-out budget: $600
- Added cost: $2,400 a year
- Net: $12,218 minus $2,400 = $9,818 a year, $818 a month

**Setup 3: co-host at 10%.**

The percentage base changes the fee. First, 10% of gross booking revenue:

- Fee: $61,625 × 10% = $6,162 (rounded)
- Net: $12,218 minus $6,162 = $6,056 a year, $505 a month

Second, 10% calculated on host earnings, which is how Airbnb calculates percentage co-host payouts. Host earnings here are gross minus the platform fee:

- Host earnings: $61,625 minus $9,552 = $52,073
- Fee: $52,073 × 10% = $5,207 (rounded)
- Net: $12,218 minus $5,207 = $7,011 a year, $584 a month

Same "10%," a $955 difference in yearly cost. Write the base into the contract.

**Setup 4: full manager at 20% of gross (canonical A3).**

- Fee: $61,625 × 20% = $12,325
- Net: $12,218 minus $12,325 = -$107 a year, -$9 a month

At 20%, the manager takes the whole margin. The landlord still gets paid, the manager gets paid, the guests get a good stay, and you earn nothing for your capital and risk. The cleaner is still paid separately in this model. If a manager's fee also covered cleaning coordination, you'd still owe the cleaner.

Now run the same four setups through the stress case (A2: ADR $180, 60% occupancy, gross $47,450, net $1,548).

| Setup | Added cost, base | Net per month, base | Added cost, stress | Net per month, stress |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1. Cleaner only | $0 | $1,018 | $0 | $129 |
| 2. Cleaner + on-call vendor | $2,400 | $818 | $2,400 | -$71 |
| 3a. Co-host 10% of gross | $6,162 | $505 | $4,745 | -$266 |
| 3b. Co-host 10% of host earnings | $5,207 | $584 | $4,010 | -$205 |
| 4. Full manager 20% of gross | $12,325 | -$9 | $9,490 | -$662 |

Break-even occupancy tells the same story from another angle:

| Setup | Break-even at $205 ADR | Break-even at $180 ADR |
|---|---:|---:|
| 1. Cleaner only | 49.9% | 57.1% |
| 2. Cleaner + on-call vendor | 53.8% | 61.6% |
| 3a. Co-host 10% of gross | 58.3% | 67.0% |
| 4. Full manager 20% of gross | 70.1% | 81.0% |

With a full manager, Alder Street needs about 70% occupancy just to break even at the base rate. That's the base case itself. There's no cushion.

Two more numbers help you negotiate. A fee of 19.8% of gross wipes out the base-case net entirely. A fee of about 10.1% leaves you $500 a month. So for this unit, any percentage deal above roughly 10% of gross leaves you less than $500 a month in a good year.

Finally, compare cost with the time returned. Assume (assumption) the vendor saves you 4 hours a month, the co-host 14 hours, and the manager 20 hours.

- Vendor: $2,400 ÷ 12 = $200 a month, or $50 per hour saved
- Co-host (10% of gross): $6,162 ÷ 12 = $514 a month, or about $37 per hour saved
- Manager: $12,325 ÷ 12 = $1,027 a month, or about $51 per hour saved

The co-host is the cheapest per hour saved, but it pushes the stress case negative. The vendor is the cheapest in total and keeps the base case healthy.

For Alder Street, I'd pick setup 2 if you live far away. It covers the emergency gap you promised the landlord and keeps $818 a month in the base case. In a bad year it turns a $129 monthly gain into a $71 monthly loss, which the $4,420 operating reserve can carry. A co-host becomes reasonable only after rent comes down or the comps support a higher rate. The full manager doesn't fit this unit's economics at 20%.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### 6.5.8 Exercise: local help decision tool

Fill this in for one unit.

**Part A: threshold tests**

| Test | True / False | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Can't reach the unit within two hours at night | | |
| More than one missed in-person response in 90 days | | |
| Hours on the unit exceed your plan | | |
| A lease, permit, or house rule requires a local contact you lack | | |
| Adding units in the same area | | |

Two or more true: add at least an on-call vendor.

**Part B: cost comparison**

| Setup | Fee terms (rate and base) | Added cost per year | Net per month, base | Net per month, stress | Break-even occupancy | Hours saved per month | Cost per hour saved |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Cleaner only | | | | | | | |
| 2. Cleaner + on-call vendor | | | | | | | |
| 3. Co-host | | | | | | | |
| 4. Full manager | | | | | | | |

**Part C: decision rule**

Choose the lightest setup that passes the threshold tests and keeps the stress-case net at or above your floor. Write your floor here: $______ a month. If no setup meets it, fix the deal before you fix the staffing. Go back to your 3.4 scorecard.

**Part D: controls you keep**

| Control | Where it's written (contract clause or platform setting) |
|---|---|
| Payouts go to your account | |
| Listing stays in your account | |
| Permission level granted | |
| Spending limit without approval | |
| Compliance decisions reserved to you | |
| Pricing floor and ceiling set by you | |
| Monthly report due date | |

### 6.5.9 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the state real estate commission or licensing board whether your helper needs a license to manage short-term rentals for others. Ask whether it matters that you're a tenant.

**Confirm with:** your insurance broker whether your STR policy covers acts of a co-host or vendor, and what certificates you should require from them.

**Confirm with:** an attorney the management or co-host agreement terms, especially termination, authorization limits, and data access.

**Confirm with:** the local licensing office whether your permit requires a named local contact and response time.

**Confirm with:** Airbnb's current help pages the co-host permission levels and payout options in your region.

### 6.5.10 Next lesson

You've decided who covers the unit on the ground. Module 7 turns to the unit itself, starting with lesson 7.1, Why Design Matters. Bring your cost comparison from Part B. Furnishing choices change turnover time, damage risk, and nightly rate, and those feed straight back into the numbers you just ran.

### 6.5 sources

- [Airbnb Help, article 1534: What co-hosts can do](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534): co-host permission levels; full access scope; hosts can change permissions; host responsibility. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [Airbnb Help, article 3264: Co-Host Additional Terms of Service](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3264): full-access definition; host alone responsible for choosing and managing co-host access; host remains responsible for co-host actions before removal. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [Airbnb Help, article 3389: How co-host payouts work](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3389): only listing owner sets up co-host payouts; percentage or fixed options; calculated on host's potential earnings; regional limits. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [Tennessee Real Estate Commission: Vacation Lodging Service Licensure](https://www.tn.gov/commerce/regboards/trec/license/license-how-to/vacation-lodging-service.html): firm and designated agent licenses for businesses managing units owned by others for stays up to 14 days. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- Canonical Alder Street figures (A1, A2, A3): `reference/recurring-examples.md`; vendor, co-host, stress-case, break-even, fee-threshold, and cost-per-hour figures computed with python on 2026-09-25.

# Part Two: STR Concierge

<a id="lesson-0.1-welcome-to-str-concierge"></a>
## 0.1  Welcome to STR Concierge: What's Different About Owning

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Module 0 - Ownership vs. Arbitrage |
| Code | 0.1 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Preston's 0.2 and 0.3 (next); PA.1 buy box; Sam's PA.2 to PA.5 demos; Sam's OP.10 30/90-day review; Part One arbitrage lessons (1.3) for contrast |

### 0.1.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you can explain, in plain terms, what changes when you own the house instead of leasing it. You can name who holds title, who carries the loan, who pays for a new roof, and who makes the final call on each decision. You can read the STR Concierge course as a sequence of decision gates. You know which gate can end a deal, and roughly how much money is at risk when you reach it. You also know which professional on your team answers which question.

### 0.1.1 Why this part of the course exists

Part One of this course taught arbitrage. You lease a unit, get written permission to host, furnish it, and run it. Your downside has edges. The lease has a term, the furniture has a price, and the rent is a known monthly number.

Owning removes those edges. You buy the building, the land under it, and every problem inside the walls. You sign a loan that runs for decades. Your cash goes in as a down payment and closing costs, and it does not come back out unless you refinance or sell.

That is the whole reason STR Concierge is a separate program. The skills from Part One still apply: pricing, cleaning, guest communication, local rules. What's new is the size of the commitment and the number of people whose sign-off you need before you commit.

This lesson is the map. It does not teach any single step in depth. Each later lesson does that. Here you'll see the order of the decisions, who helps with each one, and the points where walking away is the right answer.

### 0.1.2 Owner vs. arbitrage operator: what actually changes

Start with the legal and financial facts. The table below compares the two models on the items that change most.

| Item | Arbitrage operator (Part One) | Owner (Part Two) |
|---|---|---|
| Title | The landlord owns the property. You hold a lease. | You (or your entity) hold title. |
| Loan | None on the property. You may finance furniture. | A mortgage secured by the house. The lender may also want a personal guarantee, depending on the loan. |
| Up-front cash | Deposit, first month, furniture, setup, reserve | Down payment, closing costs, furniture, repairs, reserves |
| Monthly fixed cost | Rent set by the lease | Principal and interest, property tax, insurance, plus any HOA |
| Maintenance | Usually the landlord's for structure and systems, per the lease | Yours, all of it |
| Capital repairs (roof, HVAC, septic, water heater) | Usually the landlord's, per the lease | Yours, and they come in large amounts at irregular times |
| Local approvals | You need the city's permission and the landlord's written permission | You need the city's permission, and any HOA's. No landlord to ask. |
| Insurance | Your STR liability policy; the landlord insures the building | You insure the building and the liability, and the lender will require coverage |
| Who makes final decisions | The landlord can refuse, end, or not renew the lease | You decide, and you carry the result |
| Exit | Lease ends or you negotiate an early exit | Sell, refinance, change use, or hold. Each costs time and money. |
| What you build | Cash flow and an operating skill | Cash flow, loan paydown, possible change in value, and possible tax timing |

Read the "Maintenance" and "Capital repairs" rows twice. In arbitrage, a failed water heater is usually the landlord's problem. You lose some nights and call them. As an owner, it's your bill and your emergency. You pick the plumber, approve the cost, and pay it the same week.

The "Local approvals" row also changes shape. In arbitrage, a landlord can say no, and many do. As an owner, there's no landlord gate. But the city, the county, and any homeowners association still apply. Airbnb tells hosts to check HOA or co-op rules and local laws before hosting ([Airbnb Help Center, article 1397](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1397)). Owning the deed does not give you the right to run a short-term rental at that address. Only the local rules do.

The last row is where 0.2 picks up. An owner can earn from four places at once. An operator earns mainly from one. Four possible streams sound better, and sometimes they are. But each one carries its own cost, and some of them never show up in your bank account until you sell. Lesson 0.2 tests that claim with numbers.

### 0.1.3 The course path, stage by stage

The STR Concierge course runs in one direction: from "what should I buy" to "is it performing the way I underwrote it". Here is the path, with the lessons that carry each stage. Preston's lessons teach the decisions. Sam's lessons are the live demonstrations.

| Stage | What you decide | Lessons (tracker order) |
|---|---|---|
| Readiness | Should I own at all, and can I afford the full cash stack? | 0.1, 0.2, 0.3 (Preston) |
| Buy box | What exactly am I shopping for, and when do I pass? | PA.1 (Preston) |
| Market and deal review | Which market, which properties, and does this one pencil? | PA.2, PA.3, PA.4, PA.5 (Sam) |
| Financing and offer | Which loan, and what is my walk-away price? | PA.6, PA.7, PA.8 (Preston) |
| Diligence | Does the house and the STR business survive inspection? | PA.9 (Preston) |
| Structure | Who holds title, who operates, how is it taxed? | PA.10 (Preston) |
| Closing | Verify documents, move money safely, take possession | PA.11 (Preston) |
| Handoff to operations | Insurance, utilities, safety, launch packet | PA.12 (Preston) |
| Furnishing and launch | Set up listings, the tech stack, and pricing; furnish; build the team; write SOPs | OP.1 to OP.5 (Sam); OP.6, OP.7, OP.8, OP.9 (Preston) |
| 30/90-day review | Compare actual results to the underwriting | OP.10 (Sam) |
| Growth and exit | Property two, refinancing, selling, or exchanging | OP.11, OP.12 (Preston) |

Two notes on the map.

First, the tracker is the course. The full tracker order for Part Two starts with 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, and PA.1. Then come Sam's PA.2 to PA.5, then PA.6 to PA.12. After that: Sam's OP.1 to OP.5, then OP.6 to OP.9, Sam's OP.10, and finally OP.11 and OP.12. The older [STR Concierge Program Outline V1](https://docs.google.com/document/d/13h0c_qqbJTJjEhQTYohPnzh0fcqGdmW9WjGye5KCXfo/edit) is background reading. It does not add lessons to this list.

Second, the order matters because each stage feeds the next one a document. PA.1 produces a buy box. Sam's PA.2 and PA.3 use that buy box to pick a market and build a list of properties. PA.4 produces an underwriting package. PA.8 uses that package to set an offer ceiling. PA.9 checks the package against what the inspector and the city actually say. If you skip a stage, the next one has nothing reliable to work from.

### 0.1.4 Your working team, and what each person answers

Owning brings more people into the deal. Each has a narrow job. Trouble starts when you ask one of them to answer a question that belongs to someone else. A real estate agent is not your tax advisor. A lender is not your insurer.

| Role | What they answer | What they do not answer |
|---|---|---|
| Buyer's agent | Comparable sales, offer terms, contingency timing, local market practice | Whether STR use is legal at the address; your tax result |
| Lender | Whether you qualify, the rate and points, required reserves, what income they will count | Whether the deal is a good investment for you |
| Home inspector | The physical condition of the house and its systems | Market value (that is the appraiser's job) |
| Appraiser | Market value for the lender | Condition problems an inspection would find |
| Insurance agent | Whether they will write an STR policy on this house, what it costs, what it excludes | Whether platform protection replaces a policy (it does not) |
| Title / escrow | Who owns the property now, liens, easements, how money moves at closing | Zoning or STR permission |
| CPA | How depreciation, losses, personal use, and sale tax apply to your household | Whether the lender will approve you |
| Real estate attorney (as needed) | Contract terms, HOA documents, entity and title questions, local STR law questions | Market rents |
| Local operators (cleaner, handyman, co-host if used) | Turnovers, access, repairs, guest issues on the ground | Pricing strategy, compliance decisions, your finances |
| STR Concierge team | The course process and worksheets | Legal, tax, lending, or insurance advice. Ask the team what support your membership includes. |

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau makes the inspector versus appraiser point directly. The appraiser estimates market value for the lender. The buyer is responsible for hiring the inspector ([CFPB on home inspections](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/schedule-a-home-inspection/)). Title coverage splits the same way. Lender's title insurance protects the lender. An owner's policy is what protects your equity ([CFPB, owner's title insurance](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-owners-title-insurance-en-164/); [CFPB, lender's title insurance](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-lenders-title-insurance-en-163/)).

One more role deserves a line. If you add a co-host on Airbnb later, you can set their permission level. You as the listing owner remain responsible for your co-hosts, including ones added by a full-access co-host ([Airbnb Help Center, article 1534](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534)). You can hand off tasks. You can't hand off accountability. OP.7 covers this in detail.

You make the final decision on every gate. Each professional gives you a fact or an opinion inside their lane. You combine them.

### 0.1.5 The decision gates that can end a deal

A deal moves forward only when it clears a gate. At each gate, one answer ends it. Ending a deal early is cheap. Ending it late costs real money.

Here is the flow. Read it top to bottom. Any "No" sends you back to the property list, not forward.

```
GATE 0  Readiness (0.3)
        Can we fund the full cash stack and keep our own emergency savings?
        No  -> wait, improve liquidity, or use the lease path (Part One)
        Yes |
            v
GATE 1  Buy box fit (PA.1)
        Does this property match the buy box on every hard requirement?
        No  -> pass
        Yes |
            v
GATE 2  Address-level STR permission (PA.2, SR.1)
        Is unhosted short-term rental use allowed at this exact address,
        including any HOA rules?
        No or unclear -> pass, or get a written answer before spending more
        Yes |
            v
GATE 3  Underwriting (PA.4)
        Does the weak case stay inside our downside limit at a real loan quote?
        No  -> pass, or lower the price (PA.8)
        Yes |
            v
GATE 4  Financing (PA.6, PA.7)
        Do we have a written quote we qualify for, with reserves we can meet?
        No  -> pass or restructure
        Yes |
            v
GATE 5  Offer ceiling (PA.8)
        Will the seller accept a price at or below our walk-away number?
        No  -> walk
        Yes |
            v
GATE 6  Diligence (PA.9)
        Inspection, insurance quote, title, appraisal, permit: any finding
        that breaks the numbers or the plan?
        Yes -> renegotiate or cancel inside the contingency window
        No  |
            v
GATE 7  Closing (PA.11)
        Do the Closing Disclosure and wire instructions match what we agreed?
        No  -> stop and verify before any money moves
        Yes |
            v
GATE 8  Launch readiness (PA.12, OP.6 to OP.8)
        Insured, safe, permitted, furnished, team in place?
        No  -> do not publish the listing yet
        Yes |
            v
GATE 9  30/90-day review (OP.10)
        Are actual results inside the range we underwrote?
        No  -> fix pricing, costs, or operations; revisit the plan
```

Three things about this flow.

Gates 2 and 3 are where most deals should die. They cost almost nothing to run. The city's rules are public. Sam's PA.4 underwriting uses your own spreadsheet. A buyer who treats these gates as formalities tends to find the problem at Gate 6. By then the inspections are paid for and earnest money is on the line.

Gate 6 has a clock. Inspection and financing contingencies run on deadlines in your purchase contract. If you miss a deadline, your options can shrink, depending on the contract. PA.8 and PA.9 cover how to set those windows.

Gate 9 is not a formality either. It is the first time the underwriting meets reality. OP.10 is Sam's walkthrough of that review. OP.11 uses it to decide whether a second property is on the table at all.

### 0.1.6 Worked example: Cedar Ridge against Alder Street

The two recurring examples in this course make the difference concrete. Alder Street is the Part One arbitrage unit. Cedar Ridge is the house Maya and Chris are considering. All figures are assumptions from the course's canonical example file.

**The properties.** Alder Street is a leased two-bedroom apartment. The rent is $1,950 a month on a 24-month lease. Cedar Ridge is a three-bedroom, two-bath house in a drive-to lake market about two hours from a metro area. Maya and Chris have a $385,000 contract price against a $399,000 asking price.

**Cash on day one.**

| | Alder Street (lease) | Cedar Ridge (own, DSCR quote) |
|---|---:|---:|
| Sunk setup / furnishing | $12,050 | $32,000 |
| Deposit, first month, operating reserve | $8,320 | (not in this line) |
| Down payment | none | $96,250 |
| Closing costs incl. 1 point | none | $14,438 |
| **Cash in before owner reserves** | **$20,370** | **$142,688** |

Step by step for Cedar Ridge: the down payment is 25% of $385,000, which is $96,250. Closing costs are 3% of price ($11,550) plus 1 point on the $288,750 loan ($2,888), for $14,438. Cash to close is $96,250 + $14,438 = $110,688. Add the $32,000 furnishing budget and the total is $142,688.

That is 7 times the Alder Street figure ($142,688 / $20,370 = 7.0). And the Cedar Ridge number still has no lender reserves or personal emergency savings in it. Lesson 0.3 adds those.

**Monthly fixed obligation.** Alder Street's rent is $1,950. Cedar Ridge's principal, interest, taxes, and insurance (PITIA) on the DSCR quote is $2,557. The owner's number also comes with repairs and capital replacements the tenant usually doesn't carry. The canonical model sets aside 5% of gross for repairs and another 5% for capital items. At base-case revenue, that is $3,714 each, $7,428 a year together.

**Bad-year exposure.** In Alder Street's stress case, the unit still nets $1,548 for the year. In Cedar Ridge's weak case, the owner loses $5,475 for the year after debt service on the DSCR quote. That money comes from reserves or from the household.

**Way out.** At Alder Street, the worst case is usually the remaining lease obligation plus the furniture. Twenty-four months of rent is $46,800, and the real exposure depends on the lease's early-termination terms. At Cedar Ridge, the way out is a sale. The canonical five-year sale model shows selling costs of $31,242 at 7% of an assumed $446,321 value. That is before any income tax on the sale.

**What Cedar Ridge offers that Alder Street doesn't.** In year one on the DSCR quote, the loan balance falls by $2,795. That is equity, though you can't spend it this year. The house may change in value. And depreciation may shift tax timing, depending on facts only a CPA can confirm. Lesson 0.2 separates those four streams and tests them.

The point of this comparison is not "ownership is worse" or "ownership is better". The commitment is seven times larger in cash, and the bad-year loss lands on you instead of being capped by a lease. Before you shop, you should know that.

### 0.1.7 Money at risk at each gate

One more view of Cedar Ridge helps you see why early gates matter. The table shows the money Maya and Chris would have committed or spent by the time each gate is decided. Figures marked (assumption) are new illustrative numbers for this lesson.

| Gate | Money spent or at risk if the deal dies here |
|---|---|
| 0 to 3: readiness, buy box, permission, underwriting | Time, and possibly a data subscription. No deposit yet. |
| 4: financing | Possibly an application or credit report fee, per the lender |
| 5: offer accepted | Earnest money of $3,850, 1% of price (assumption). Whether it is refundable depends on the contract and contingencies. |
| 6: diligence | Earnest money at risk, plus inspection fees of about $1,500 (assumption) for general, septic, and other specialist checks |
| 7: closing | $110,688 cash to close on the DSCR quote |
| 8: launch | Plus $32,000 furnishing, plus carrying costs until the first booking |

The first four gates cost almost nothing to fail. The last three cost thousands, then tens of thousands. So the work you do in PA.1 and in Sam's PA.2 to PA.4 is the cheapest protection you will buy in this program.

### 0.1.8 Exercise: your gate log

Use this log for every property you seriously consider, starting with the first one Sam's PA.3 pipeline produces. Fill in one row per gate as you reach it. Leave a gate blank until you have a written answer.

| Gate | Question | Evidence (document, link, or email) | Who confirmed | Date | Result: pass / fail / open | If fail, what ended it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 Readiness | Full cash stack funded, emergency savings kept? | 0.3 scorecard | You | | | |
| 1 Buy box | Every hard requirement met? | PA.1 buy box | You | | | |
| 2 STR permission | Allowed at this address and under HOA rules? | Official source URL, city email | City / county office; attorney if unclear | | | |
| 3 Underwriting | Weak case inside our downside limit? | PA.4 package | You; review with Concierge worksheet | | | |
| 4 Financing | Written quote we qualify for, reserves met? | Loan Estimate or term sheet | Lender | | | |
| 5 Offer | Price at or below walk-away? | Signed contract | You and agent | | | |
| 6 Diligence | No finding that breaks the numbers? | Inspection reports, insurance quote, title commitment, appraisal | Inspector, insurer, title, appraiser | | | |
| 7 Closing | Closing Disclosure and wire verified? | Closing Disclosure; call log to a verified number | Title / escrow, lender | | | |
| 8 Launch | Insured, safe, permitted, furnished, team ready? | Policy declarations, permit, PA.12 packet | Insurer, city, you | | | |
| 9 30/90-day review | Actuals inside underwriting range? | OP.10 review | You | | | |

Before you move on, answer two questions in writing:

1. Which gate do you expect to be hardest for you, and why?
2. Who on your list above do you not yet have a name for? Write down how you'll find one before PA.4.

### 0.1.9 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the city or county licensing office, before an offer, that STR use is allowed at the exact address and under any HOA rules. Use an STR attorney if the answer is unclear.

**Confirm with:** your lender what reserves, credit, down payment, and guarantees their loan requires, in writing.

**Confirm with:** your insurance agent that they will write an STR policy for the specific house, and what it excludes, before your diligence window closes.

**Confirm with:** your real estate agent or attorney how the earnest money, inspection, and financing contingencies in your contract work, including every deadline.

**Confirm with:** your CPA how ownership, depreciation, and personal use affect your household's taxes before you count any tax benefit in a decision.

### 0.1.10 Next lesson

You now have the map and the gates. Lesson 0.2, Why Ownership Wins: Cash Flow + Tax Savings + Equity, tests the "four possible streams" row on Cedar Ridge. You'll separate the money you can spend from the money locked in the house. And you'll see one case where owning works and one where it does not.

### 0.1 sources

- [Airbnb Help Center, General info about hosting places to stay (article 1397)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1397): hosts should check leases, HOA or co-op rules, and local laws before hosting. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to airbnb.com.
- [Airbnb Help Center, What co-hosts can do (article 1534)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534): listing owners are responsible for co-hosts, including those added by full-access co-hosts; permission levels. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to airbnb.com.
- [CFPB, Schedule a home inspection](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/schedule-a-home-inspection/): inspector vs. appraiser; the buyer arranges the inspection. Brief-provided link; content checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to consumerfinance.gov.
- [CFPB, What is owner's title insurance?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-owners-title-insurance-en-164/) and [What is lender's title insurance?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-lenders-title-insurance-en-163/): lender's policy protects the lender; owner's policy protects the owner's equity. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to consumerfinance.gov.
- [STR Concierge Program Outline V1](https://docs.google.com/document/d/13h0c_qqbJTJjEhQTYohPnzh0fcqGdmW9WjGye5KCXfo/edit): background only; the production tracker is the course map. Brief-provided, not independently opened.
- Cedar Ridge and Alder Street figures: `reference/recurring-examples.md` (all assumptions). New figures in 0.1.7 (earnest money 1%, inspection fees $1,500) are assumptions computed with python.

<a id="lesson-0.2-why-ownership-wins"></a>
## 0.2  Why Ownership Wins: Cash Flow + Tax Savings + Equity

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Module 0 - Ownership vs. Arbitrage |
| Code | 0.2 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Preston's 0.1 (before) and 0.3 (next); Sam's PA.4 underwriting and PA.5 cost segregation; SR.3 cost segregation estimator; Preston's PA.10 and OP.12 for tax and exit |

### 0.2.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you can split an ownership return into four streams: cash flow, principal paydown, change in value, and tax timing. You can say which of the four you can actually spend this year. You can compare owning a house with leasing a similar one on the same assumptions. And you can run a simple test that tells you whether "ownership wins" is true for a specific deal, or only true on a slide.

### 0.2.1 Treat "wins" as a claim you have to test

The title of this lesson makes a claim: ownership wins. Sometimes it does. Sometimes it loses badly. The only honest way to use that title is as a question you answer for one property, one loan, and one household.

The case for owning usually lists three benefits: cash flow, tax savings, and equity. I'm going to split equity into two parts, because they behave differently. That gives four possible return streams:

1. Operating cash flow: what's left after operating costs and the loan payment.
2. Principal paydown: the part of each loan payment that reduces what you owe.
3. Change in value: what the house is worth later compared with what you paid.
4. Tax timing: deductions, mainly depreciation, that may lower your tax bill now and may be partly taken back when you sell.

Each stream has a cost or a risk attached. And only one of them lands in your bank account on a normal month. The mistake I want you to avoid is adding all four together and calling the total "income". It is not income. Three of the four are either locked inside the house or depend on facts that won't be known for years.

### 0.2.2 Stream one: operating cash flow

This is the only stream you can spend this year. It is the money left after the house pays for itself.

For Cedar Ridge, the canonical base case works like this. All figures are assumptions from the course example file.

- Gross booking revenue: 212 booked nights at $300, plus 61 cleaning fees of $175, for $74,275.
- Operating costs, including platform fees, cleaner, utilities, repairs reserve, capital reserve, property tax, and insurance: $45,066.
- Net operating income (NOI): $74,275 minus $45,066, which is $29,209.
- Annual principal and interest on the DSCR quote: $23,637.
- Cash flow: $29,209 minus $23,637, which is $5,572 a year, or about $464 a month.

That $5,572 is real money, if the base case happens. Notice what it already pays for: a 5% repairs reserve and a 5% capital reserve. If you drop those reserves to make the number look bigger, you are borrowing from the next roof.

Now the cost side. Against $142,688 of cash in (down payment, closing costs, and furnishing), $5,572 is a 3.9% cash-on-cash return. And in the weak case, with ADR at $265 and occupancy at 50%, the same house loses $5,475 a year after debt service. So stream one is small in a good year and negative in a bad one.

### 0.2.3 Stream two: principal paydown

Each month, part of the loan payment reduces the loan balance. That part is not an expense in the economic sense. It turns into equity.

On the Cedar Ridge DSCR quote, the balance falls by $2,795 in year one. That number grows a little each year because more of each payment goes to principal as the balance shrinks.

Here's the catch. You paid that $2,795 out of your cash flow already. It's inside the $23,637 of debt service. You can't spend it. You get it back only when you sell or refinance. The canonical refinance model at the end of year two shows a cash-out of $15,725 after costs. That is how locked equity becomes cash, and it comes with a new, larger loan payment. The same model shows base-case cash flow falling from $5,572 to $4,752 after the refinance.

Principal paydown also depends on the house holding its value. If the value falls, you can pay down the loan and still have less equity than you started with.

### 0.2.4 Stream three: change in value

This is the stream people talk about most and control least.

For teaching, the canonical example assumes Cedar Ridge's value grows 3% a year (assumption). In year one that would be 3% of $385,000, or $11,550. It is an assumption, not a forecast. Values can fall. Lake markets can see new supply, rule changes, or a slow year.

Three rules for this stream:

- Never add modeled appreciation to spendable monthly income. $11,550 of assumed value change is not about $960 a month you can use.
- You only collect it by selling or borrowing against it, and both cost money.
- Selling costs are large. The canonical model uses 7% of the sale price. On an assumed $446,321 value after five years, that is $31,242.

Here is a test worth running on any deal. What happens if the value is flat for five years? For Cedar Ridge, that means selling at $385,000. Subtract 7% selling costs of $26,950 and the year-five loan balance of $272,519. Net proceeds before tax are $85,531. You put in $142,688. The shortfall is only covered if cash flow and tax timing make up the difference. Section 0.2.8 runs this.

### 0.2.5 Stream four: tax timing

Depreciation is a paper deduction for wear on the building. You don't write a check for it, but it can reduce taxable income. Whether it reduces your tax bill depends on several facts. Each one is a question for your CPA, not a number to assume.

**Basis and land.** You depreciate the building, not the land. IRS Publication 527 states that you can never depreciate land ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). So the first input is how much of the price belongs to the land. The canonical example assumes 20% (assumption). On a basis of $391,000 (price plus $6,000 of capitalized closing costs, assumption), that leaves $312,800 for the building.

**Placed in service.** Depreciation starts when the property is ready and available for rent, not when the first guest arrives. Publication 527 gives an example: a house ready for rent in July and first rented in September starts depreciating in July ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). For Cedar Ridge, that means the month the house is furnished, safe, and listed matters. A late-year launch gives a small first-year deduction on the building.

**Recovery period and classification.** The canonical file shows the building on two schedules. At 27.5 years it's $11,375 per full year; at 39 years, $8,021. Short average stays can change how the property is classified. Your CPA decides which applies to your facts.

**Accelerated depreciation.** A cost segregation study can move some of the building into 5, 7, and 15-year property. Furnishings are also short-life property. IRS guidance describes a permanent 100% first-year (bonus) depreciation deduction for eligible property acquired after January 19, 2025 ([IRS newsroom, Notice 2026-11 guidance](https://www.irs.gov/newsroom/treasury-irs-issue-guidance-on-the-additional-first-year-depreciation-deduction-amended-as-part-of-the-one-big-beautiful-bill)). It applies to eligible property, not to land and not to the whole building. Sam's PA.5 walks through the study itself.

In the canonical illustration, a 22% reclassification (assumption) puts $68,816 of the building into short-life property. Add the $32,000 of furnishings, and the year-one bonus amount is $100,816, if eligible. Add a full year of 39-year depreciation on the remaining building ($6,256) and year-one deductions are about $107,072. At an assumed 32% marginal rate, that could lower tax by about $34,263.

Read the word "could" carefully. That result depends on the next three facts.

**Passive activity rules.** Losses from passive activities generally offset only passive income. IRS Publication 925 explains the rules ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). Two parts of it matter most for short-term rentals:

- An activity is not a "rental activity" under these rules if the average period of customer use is 7 days or less. Publication 925 says you figure the average by dividing total days in all rental periods by the number of rentals. Cedar Ridge's canonical average stay is 3.5 nights: 212 nights over 61 stays.
- That exception does not make the losses non-passive by itself. It means the activity is tested like a business. The losses are non-passive only if you materially participate.

Publication 925 lists the material participation tests. Three of them matter most here. You participated more than 500 hours. Or your participation was substantially all the participation by anyone. Or you participated more than 100 hours and at least as much as any other individual, owner or not ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). That last point matters for an STR. A cleaner who turns the house over 61 times a year may log more hours than you do. If you hire a full-service co-host, their hours may exceed yours by a wide margin.

**The $25,000 allowance does not rescue a high-income household.** Publication 925 also describes a special allowance of up to $25,000. It applies to rental real estate with active participation. It is reduced by 50% of modified adjusted gross income above $100,000 and is gone at $150,000 ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). Maya and Chris have $185,000 of combined W-2 income. If their modified AGI is near that figure, the allowance would give them nothing, even if it applied to this activity at all.

**Personal use.** Maya and Chris plan about 10 personal days a year. Publication 527 has a day test. A dwelling is used as a home if personal use exceeds the greater of 14 days or 10% of fair-rental days. It also requires dividing expenses between rental and personal use by days ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). At 212 rented nights, 10% is 21.2 days, so the threshold is 21.2 days. Ten days is below it. More personal use would change the picture.

**It's timing, not a gift.** Depreciation lowers your basis. When you sell, some of the deduction can come back as taxable gain. That's why this stream is called "tax timing". Lesson OP.12 treats the tax on sale as a CPA input.

**Confirm with:** a CPA whether the activity is a rental activity for your household and whether your actual hours meet a participation test. Also confirm which recovery periods apply, and whether any loss is usable this year or suspended.

### 0.2.6 Owning compared with leasing a similar house

Part One of this course is about leasing. So a fair question is: why not lease a house like Cedar Ridge instead of buying it?

To compare fairly, keep the operating assumptions identical. Same revenue, same cleaner, same utilities. Change only what differs between owning and leasing. The lease figures below are a hypothetical arbitrage-style lease on a comparable house. **All figures in this table are assumptions.** Rent is $2,900 a month on a 12-month lease with written STR permission. The tenant carries minor repairs of $1,200 a year and a renter's STR liability policy of $1,800 a year. The landlord pays property tax, building insurance, and capital repairs.

| Line (annual) | Own Cedar Ridge (DSCR quote) | Lease a comparable house (assumption) |
|---|---:|---:|
| Gross booking revenue (base) | $74,275 | $74,275 |
| Platform fee, cleaner, utilities, supplies, software, handyman | -$30,588 | -$30,588 |
| Repairs and capital reserve | -$7,428 | -$1,200 |
| Property tax and building insurance / renter's liability policy | -$7,050 | -$1,800 |
| Principal and interest / rent | -$23,637 | -$34,800 |
| **Base-case cash flow** | **$5,572** | **$5,887** |
| Weak-case cash flow (ADR $265, 50%) | -$5,475 | -$6,856 |
| Base case with 18% co-host | -$7,798 | -$7,483 |
| Cash in (see below) | $142,688 | $44,500 |
| Cash-on-cash, base | 3.9% | 13.2% |
| Principal paydown, year 1 | $2,795 | none |
| Exposure to value change | Yes, both directions | None |
| Possible depreciation | Building, improvements, furnishings | Furnishings and your own improvements only |
| Exit | Sale (7% costs assumed) or refinance | Lease ends, or early-exit terms |

How the lease cash in adds up: $32,000 of furnishing, a $2,900 deposit, and $2,900 of first month's rent. Add a two-month operating reserve of $6,700 (rent plus $450 of utilities, times two). Total: $44,500.

Read the comparison slowly.

- Base-case cash flow is about the same. Leasing is $315 a year higher.
- Owning needs $98,188 more cash ($142,688 minus $44,500). That cash is the cost of streams two, three, and four.
- In the weak case, the lease loses more, because rent is fixed and higher than the owner's loan payment. The owner, though, also carries capital repairs that the reserve may not fully cover in a bad year.
- The owner builds equity and may get tax timing. The tenant gets neither.
- The tenant's exposure ends with the lease. The owner's continues until a sale.

This is also why the lease path is not a fantasy. For some households, $44,500 in a lease beats $142,688 in a house. For others, the long hold and the equity matter more than the cash-on-cash rate.

One practical caveat. In many drive-to lake markets, owners of three-bedroom houses may not lease them for short-term rental at all. The lease column is a teaching comparison. Whether a real lease exists is a market question for Sam's PA.2 and the Part One research lessons.

### 0.2.7 Worked example: stacking the four streams without cheating

Here is the right way to present Cedar Ridge's year one on the DSCR quote. Keep the streams in separate rows, and label each by whether you can spend it.

| Stream | Year 1 amount | Can you spend it this year? | What it depends on |
|---|---:|---|---|
| Operating cash flow | $5,572 | Yes | Base case revenue; weak case is -$5,475 |
| Principal paydown | $2,795 | No, locked until sale or refinance | Paying the loan every month |
| Change in value (3%, assumption) | $11,550 | No, and it may be negative | Market; only realized by selling or borrowing |
| Tax timing (illustrative, if usable) | up to about $34,263 | Only if the loss is usable against other income this year | Basis, land split, placed-in-service date, classification, passive rules, material participation, personal use, household tax rate; partly recaptured on sale |

Now look at return on cash in, one layer at a time:

- Cash flow alone: $5,572 / $142,688 = 3.9%.
- Cash flow plus principal paydown: $8,367 / $142,688 = 5.9%.
- Adding assumed appreciation: $19,917 / $142,688 = 14.0%.

Those three percentages describe three very different things. Only the first one pays a bill. The third one includes an assumption you can't check until you sell. When someone shows you a single "total return" number, ask for this table instead.

I left tax timing out of those percentages on purpose. It depends on your CPA's answers, and it can be zero in year one if the loss is suspended.

### 0.2.8 One case where ownership works, one where it doesn't

Now a five-year view, using the canonical sale model. Assumptions: base-case cash flow stays flat for five years (assumption), and the original DSCR loan stays in place. The house sells at the end of year five with 7% selling costs. Every figure is before income tax, and before any tax on the sale or depreciation recapture.

**Case A: ownership works (self-managed, base case, 3% value growth).**

- Five years of cash flow: 5 x $5,572 = $27,860.
- Net sale proceeds before tax: $142,560 (value $446,321, less loan balance $272,519, less selling costs $31,242).
- Total back: $27,860 + $142,560 = $170,420.
- Less cash in: $170,420 - $142,688 = $27,732 ahead, before tax.

Plus whatever tax timing your CPA confirms, less whatever tax is due on the sale. It is a modest result for five years and $142,688. But it is positive, it survives a year or two of weak revenue from reserves, and Maya and Chris keep control.

**Case B: ownership does not work (full-service co-host at 18%).**

Suppose Maya and Chris decide they don't want to run the house. They hire a full-service local co-host at 18% of booking revenue. The co-host costs $13,370 a year in the base case.

- Annual cash flow on the DSCR quote: -$7,798.
- Five years: 5 x -$7,798 = -$38,990.
- Total back: -$38,990 + $142,560 = $103,570.
- Less cash in: $103,570 - $142,688 = $39,118 behind, before tax.

In this case they pay about $650 a month to own the house. The only path to breaking even is appreciation above the 3% assumption or tax timing, and neither is spendable while you're writing checks every month. If revenue is also weak, the co-host case loses $15,794 a year: weak NOI of $18,162, less the co-host's $10,319, less $23,637 of debt service.

Two more versions of the same test:

- Weak case, self-managed, 3% value growth: 5 x -$5,475 = -$27,375 of cash flow. Add $142,560 of proceeds and subtract $142,688 of cash in. Result: $27,503 behind, before tax.
- Base case, flat value: $27,860 of cash flow plus $85,531 of proceeds, less $142,688. Result: $29,297 behind, before tax.

So Cedar Ridge "wins" only on specific facts. Revenue hits the base case or better. The owners manage with local help. The house holds or gains value. Change any one of those and it loses over five years before tax. This is what "test it" means.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### 0.2.9 Exercise: the four-stream test for your deal

Fill this in for any property that reaches Sam's PA.4 underwriting. Use your own lender quote. Do not fill in the tax row until your CPA answers the questions in 0.2.5.

**Part A. Year-one streams**

| Stream | Base case | Weak case | Spendable this year? (Y/N) | Source of the number |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| Operating cash flow (NOI minus principal and interest) | | | | PA.4 model |
| Principal paydown, year 1 | | | | Lender amortization schedule |
| Change in value (state your % assumption) | | | | Your assumption; write it down |
| Tax timing (CPA estimate, or blank) | | | | CPA |

**Part B. Five-year test (before income tax)**

| Line | Base, value +__% | Weak, value +__% | Base, value flat |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Five years of cash flow | | | |
| Sale value, year 5 | | | |
| Less loan balance, year 5 | | | |
| Less selling costs (__%) | | | |
| Net proceeds before tax | | | |
| Total back (cash flow + proceeds) | | | |
| Less total cash in | | | |
| **Ahead / behind** | | | |

**Part C. Same house, leased (if a lease is realistic in this market)**

| Line | Own | Lease |
|---|---:|---:|
| Cash in | | |
| Base-case cash flow | | |
| Weak-case cash flow | | |
| Cash-on-cash, base | | |
| Worst realistic exit cost | | |

**Decision rule.** Ownership "wins" for this deal only if all three are true:

1. The Part B base case is ahead.
2. The Part B weak case is behind by no more than you could accept losing.
3. In Part C, the extra cash for owning buys something you want: a long hold, personal use, or equity. If any one fails, write down which, and bring it to 0.3.

### 0.2.10 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** your CPA the land allocation, placed-in-service date, recovery period, and bonus depreciation eligibility. Also confirm the passive-rule treatment, your participation hours, personal-use days, and the tax on a future sale.

**Confirm with:** your lender the loan balance schedule (amortization) and the actual payment, so principal paydown uses real numbers.

**Confirm with:** a local agent or appraiser the value assumptions you use for the five-year test. Treat any growth rate as your assumption, not theirs.

**Confirm with:** a cost segregation provider and your CPA whether a study makes sense for the property, before counting accelerated depreciation (Sam's PA.5, SR.3).

### 0.2.11 Next lesson

You now know that ownership can win or lose on the same house depending on revenue, management, and value. Lesson 0.3, Is Ownership Right for You? A Capital & Readiness Checklist, asks the next question. Can Maya and Chris fund the full cash stack, including reserves and their own emergency savings? And can they get through a weak first season?

### 0.2 sources

- [IRS Publication 527, Residential Rental Property](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527): land is never depreciable; depreciation begins when property is ready and available for rent (July/September example); dwelling used as a home if personal use exceeds the greater of 14 days or 10% of fair-rental days; expense division between rental and personal use. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov (direct fetch blocked).
- [IRS Publication 925, Passive Activity and At-Risk Rules](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925): 7-day average period of customer use exception and how to compute the average; material participation tests (500 hours; substantially all; more than 100 hours and not less than any other individual); $25,000 special allowance with phase-out between $100,000 and $150,000 of modified AGI. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov (direct fetch blocked).
- [IRS newsroom, Treasury and IRS guidance on the additional first-year depreciation deduction (Notice 2026-11)](https://www.irs.gov/newsroom/treasury-irs-issue-guidance-on-the-additional-first-year-depreciation-deduction-amended-as-part-of-the-one-big-beautiful-bill): permanent 100% additional first-year depreciation for eligible property acquired after January 19, 2025. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov.
- Cedar Ridge figures (B1 to B8): `reference/recurring-examples.md`, all assumptions. New figures are assumptions computed with python from `tools/examples.py`: the 3% year-one value change of $11,550, the flat-value sale, five-year totals, and the lease comparison ($2,900 rent, $1,200 repairs, $1,800 renter's policy, $44,500 cash in).
