<a id="lesson-0.3-capital-readiness-checklist"></a>
## 0.3  Is Ownership Right for You? A Capital & Readiness Checklist

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Module 0 - Ownership vs. Arbitrage |
| Code | 0.3 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Preston's 0.2 (before) and PA.1 (next); Preston's PA.6 and PA.7 for financing detail; Part One lease path (1.3, 1.5); Preston's PA.10 for tax questions |

### 0.3.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you can add up the full cash an ownership deal requires, not just the down payment. You can compare it with the money you actually have, after keeping your own emergency savings. You can test whether you'd survive a delayed opening or a weak first season. And you finish with a one-page readiness scorecard. It sends you to one of four places: PA.1, better financing or liquidity first, the Part One lease path, or wait.

### 0.3.1 The down payment is the smallest question

Most buyers start with one number: the down payment. For Cedar Ridge, 25% of $385,000 is $96,250. Maya and Chris have $210,000 in liquid savings outside retirement accounts. On that math alone, they look ready twice over.

That math leaves out most of the bill. An owned short-term rental needs money for closing, furnishing, repairs, and permits. It needs money for the months before the first guest. And it needs reserves, for the lender and for you. It also needs your own household to stay safe if the house has a bad year.

This lesson lays out every layer, then runs Maya and Chris through it. Learning "not yet" here is much cheaper than at a closing table.

### 0.3.2 The full cash stack, layer by layer

Think of the cash requirement as a stack. Each layer sits on top of the one before it. You need all of them funded, or you are relying on the house to perform from day one.

| Layer | What it covers | Spent or held? |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Down payment | Your share of the price | Spent (becomes equity) |
| 2. Closing costs and points | Lender fees, title, escrow, prepaid items, discount points | Spent |
| 3. Furnishing and setup | Furniture, linens, kitchen, safety equipment, photos | Spent |
| 4. Initial repairs and safety punch list | Items from the inspection you accept, plus work needed before guests | Spent |
| 5. Permit, registration, inspections | Local fees and specialist inspections | Spent |
| 6. Launch carrying costs | Loan payment, utilities, software, and vendor retainers before the first booking | Spent |
| 7. Property reserves | The lender's required reserves, which after closing become your operating cushion for the house | Held |
| 8. Personal emergency savings | Your household's own safety net, separate from the house | Held |

Layers 1 to 6 are money you will spend. Layers 7 and 8 are money you must still have after spending it.

The most common failure is merging layers 7 and 8. A buyer counts the same dollars as the house's reserve and the family's emergency fund. Then the water heater fails in the same month a paycheck is late, and there's only one pile of cash for two problems.

### 0.3.3 Where the money can come from, and the limits on each source

Money for the stack can come from several places. Each has limits, and your lender may treat them differently.

**Liquid savings.** Checking, savings, and taxable brokerage accounts. This is the cleanest source. Lenders usually want to see where it came from and how long it has been there. Ask your lender what documentation they need for large recent deposits.

**Retirement accounts.** Maya and Chris's $210,000 excludes retirement accounts. Some lenders may count part of a retirement balance toward reserves, and withdrawals can carry taxes and penalties. Treat this as a question for your lender and CPA, not as spendable cash.

**Home equity.** Maya and Chris own their primary home. It's worth $520,000 with a $260,000 mortgage balance, so they have $260,000 of equity on paper. A home equity line of credit (HELOC) lets you borrow against it. The CFPB notes that HELOCs usually carry a variable rate, so payments can change, and your home is the collateral. If you can't repay, you could lose your home ([CFPB, What is a HELOC?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-home-equity-line-of-credit-heloc-en-107/)). A HELOC is borrowed money, not savings. It adds a payment to your household budget. Ask the lender whether HELOC funds can go toward a down payment or count as reserves. Also ask how the new payment affects your qualification. OP.11 covers HELOCs for scaling in more depth.

**Gifts or partners.** Lenders have rules on gift funds and co-borrowers. If a partner puts in money, ownership and loan paperwork must match (PA.10).

**Seller concessions.** A seller may pay part of your closing costs. That's a PA.8 negotiation item, not something to count on now.

For this lesson, we count only liquid savings. If the stack only works with borrowed money, record that as a finding.

### 0.3.4 Reserves: the lender's minimum is not your plan

Lenders measure reserves in months of the new loan's payment. Fannie Mae's Selling Guide section B3-4.1-01 measures reserves in months of the new loan's payment. That payment is principal, interest, taxes, insurance, and association dues (PITIA). The question is how many months your financial assets could cover ([Fannie Mae Selling Guide, B3-4.1-01 Minimum Reserve Requirements](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-4.1-01/minimum-reserve-requirements)).

A few points from that section, as they apply to a buyer like Maya and Chris:

- There is no minimum reserve requirement for a one-unit principal residence. Second homes and investment properties do have requirements.
- For loans run through Fannie Mae's automated underwriting system (Desktop Underwriter, or DU), DU determines the required reserves. For manually underwritten loans, the minimum is in Fannie Mae's Eligibility Matrix.
- If you have multiple financed properties and the new loan is on a second home or investment property, additional reserves apply. They are calculated as a percentage of the unpaid balances on your other financed properties, excluding the subject property and your principal residence. The guide uses 2% for one to four financed properties, 4% for five to six, and 6% for seven to ten.

Maya and Chris have two financed properties after this purchase: their home and Cedar Ridge. The other-property add-on would exclude both, so it would likely be zero for them. Their lender confirms the actual figure.

The DSCR quote in the canonical example is not a Fannie Mae loan. Each DSCR lender publishes or quotes its own reserve rule. For teaching, we assume the DSCR lender requires **6 months of PITIA (assumption)**. On the DSCR quote that is 6 x $2,557 = $15,342. We use the same 6 months (assumption) on the conventional quote so the two are comparable: 6 x $2,484 = $14,904. Get the real number from each lender in writing. PA.6 and PA.7 go deeper.

Now the important part. After closing, the lender stops watching. The reserve becomes your operating cushion for the house. The lender's number is a floor to get the loan. It is not a plan for a bad season. Maya and Chris will set their own reserve target in the worked example.

### 0.3.5 Credit, eligibility, and debt capacity

Three questions shape whether a lender will lend, before the house is even chosen. The fourth, liquidity, is the cash stack in 0.3.8.

**Credit.** Maya and Chris have scores around 740. Each lender sets its own minimum and prices its rate by score. Kiavi's DSCR guide, for example, says the lender looks at credit score and down payment, not only the property's coverage ratio ([Kiavi DSCR guide](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans)). That is one lender's approach, not a market standard.

**Eligibility of the loan type.** A DSCR loan qualifies mainly on the property's income compared with its payment. A conventional loan qualifies mainly on your household income and debts. Fannie Mae updated its rental income guidance in September 2026, including a section on subject-property short-term rental income ([Fannie Mae Selling Guide, B3-3.8-03](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-3.8-03/rental-income-subject-property-short-term-rental)). Whether a particular lender will count projected or actual STR income for your loan is their call. PA.7 compares the two paths.

**Debt capacity.** A conventional lender compares your monthly debts with your income (the debt-to-income ratio, or DTI). Here is a rough illustration for Maya and Chris. **All figures are assumptions** except their income and the Cedar Ridge PITIA.

| Line | Monthly |
|---|---:|
| Gross income ($185,000 / 12) | $15,417 |
| Primary home payment (assumption) | $2,400 |
| Other debts: cars, cards (assumption) | $600 |
| Cedar Ridge PITIA, conventional quote | $2,484 |
| **Total debts** | **$5,484** |
| **DTI with no STR income counted** | **35.6%** |

The same calculation with the DSCR quote's $2,557 gives 36.0%. The lender sets the maximum ratio and decides how much rental income, if any, to credit. This rough check only tells you whether a conventional path is worth asking about.

### 0.3.6 A delayed opening or a weak first season

Two things go wrong often enough that you should budget for both.

**Delayed opening.** Permits take longer than expected. Furniture arrives late. A contractor misses a week. Every month between closing and the first guest costs the full carrying cost with no revenue. For Cedar Ridge on the DSCR quote, that is $3,257 a month. It's the $2,557 PITIA plus the canonical $450 of utilities, $100 of software, and $150 handyman retainer.

The base stack includes two months of carrying costs (assumption), or $6,514. A three-month delay beyond that adds another 3 x $3,257 = $9,771. Paid from the 6-month property reserve of $15,342, that would leave $5,571.

**Weak first season.** A new listing has no reviews and may book below its long-run potential. The canonical weak case, ADR $265 at 50% occupancy, loses $5,475 a year on the DSCR quote. That is about $456 a month. The $15,342 property reserve would cover about 2.8 years of that loss, if nothing else went wrong.

A delay followed by a weak year would use most of the property reserve. Plan for that pairing. It's the one that forces sales at bad times.

### 0.3.7 Time, involvement, and risk tolerance

Cash isn't the only resource. Owning also takes time and a stomach for variance.

**Time.** In the canonical base case, Maya and Chris self-manage with a local cleaner and an on-call handyman. Assume they spend 1.5 hours per stay on messages, scheduling, and turnover checks (assumption). Add 4 hours a month on bookkeeping, pricing, and vendor calls (assumption). With 61 stays, that is 91.5 + 48 = about 140 hours a year, or 2.7 hours a week (assumption). Some weeks will take much more: a lockout at 11 p.m., a broken hot water heater on a Saturday.

That time estimate also matters for taxes. As 0.2 explained, whether losses are usable can depend on material participation. Publication 925's tests look at hours, including how your hours compare with those of other individuals, such as a cleaner ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). If the cleaner spends 4 hours on each of 61 turnovers (assumption), that is 244 hours, more than the owners' 140. Your CPA confirms how your hours count.

**Involvement choice.** If you don't want to spend those hours, the alternative is a co-host or manager. In 0.2, a full-service co-host at 18% turned Cedar Ridge's base-case cash flow from $5,572 to -$7,798 a year. Decide now which kind of owner you'll be, because it changes whether the deal works.

**Personal use.** Maya and Chris plan about 10 personal days a year. IRS Publication 527 treats a dwelling as used as a home if personal use exceeds the greater of 14 days or 10% of fair-rental days. It also requires dividing expenses by days ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). At 212 rented nights, that threshold is 21.2 days, so 10 days is below it. There is also a revenue cost. If those 10 nights would have booked at the $300 base ADR, they're worth up to $3,000 of nightly revenue (assumption). Plan your own stays for slower weeks.

**Risk tolerance.** In 0.2, the five-year test showed Cedar Ridge ahead $27,732 before tax in the base case and behind $27,503 in the weak case. Both of you should look at the second number and say out loud whether you could live with it. If the honest answer is no, that's a readiness result, not a failure.

### 0.3.8 Worked example: Maya and Chris's full cash stack

Maya and Chris have $210,000 in liquid savings. Their household's essential spending is $8,500 a month (assumption), and they want to keep 6 months of it as personal emergency savings (assumption): $51,000.

Here is the stack on each quote. Canonical figures come from the course file. New figures are marked (assumption).

| Layer | DSCR quote | Conventional quote |
|---|---:|---:|
| Down payment (25%) | $96,250 | $96,250 |
| Closing costs incl. points | $14,438 | $11,550 |
| **Cash to close** | **$110,688** | **$107,800** |
| Furnishing and setup | $32,000 | $32,000 |
| Initial repairs and safety punch list (assumption) | $4,000 | $4,000 |
| Inspections: general, septic, specialist (assumption) | $1,500 | $1,500 |
| Permit and registration fees (assumption) | $600 | $600 |
| Launch carrying costs, 2 months (assumption) | $6,514 | $6,368 |
| **Total spent (layers 1 to 6)** | **$155,302** | **$152,268** |
| Property reserve, 6 months PITIA (assumption) | $15,342 | $14,904 |
| Personal emergency savings, 6 months (assumption) | $51,000 | $51,000 |
| **Full stack** | **$221,644** | **$218,172** |
| Liquid savings | $210,000 | $210,000 |
| **Surplus / gap** | **-$11,644** | **-$8,172** |

Step by step for the DSCR column. Spent layers: $110,688 + $32,000 + $4,000 + $1,500 + $600 + $6,514 = $155,302. Add the $15,342 property reserve and the $51,000 emergency fund: $221,644. Subtract from $210,000: a gap of $11,644.

Two readings of the same table:

- **The lender's view.** After spending $155,302, Maya and Chris would still hold $54,698. That's about 21 months of the DSCR PITIA, well above a 6-month reserve rule. On paper, a lender may say yes.
- **Their own plan.** They can't fund the house's reserve and their own emergency savings at the same time. They are $11,644 short on the DSCR quote and $8,172 short on the conventional quote.

This is the gap the down payment check hides.

What can close the gap? Options, in the order I'd look at them:

1. **Time.** If they save $3,500 a month (assumption), the DSCR gap closes in about 3.3 months, the conventional gap in about 2.3 months. Searching, underwriting, and closing will take a while anyway.
2. **Financing.** The conventional quote needs $2,888 less at closing and has a lower payment, if they qualify for it. That's a PA.7 comparison.
3. **Price.** A lower price reduces the down payment and closing costs. PA.1 turns this into a hard cap.
4. **Furnishing plan.** A phased furnishing plan may cut the up-front budget. OP.6 covers this without cutting safety or listing accuracy.

What they should **not** do: shrink their emergency fund to make the table balance, or count a HELOC as savings.

### 0.3.9 The one-page readiness scorecard

Fill this in before you shop. Score each line Green, Yellow, or Red. The outcome rules follow the table.

**Readiness scorecard** (one page)

Name(s): ____________ Date: ____________ Quote used: DSCR / Conventional / Other ____________

| # | Check | Green | Yellow | Red | Your score | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Full cash stack (0.3.8) from liquid savings | Surplus of $0 or more | Gap you can close with normal saving within 6 months | Larger gap, or funded only with borrowed money | | |
| 2 | Personal emergency savings kept intact after closing | 6+ months of essential spending | 3 to 6 months | Under 3 months | | |
| 3 | Property reserve after closing | At or above the lender's rule and your own target | At lender rule only | Below lender rule | | |
| 4 | Credit | At or above the lender's written minimum, with room | At the minimum | Below the minimum | | |
| 5 | Lender fit | Written pre-qualification or term sheet for at least one loan path | Verbal only | None, or declined | | |
| 6 | Debt capacity (conventional) or property coverage (DSCR) | Inside the lender's stated limit | Near the limit | Outside it | | |
| 7 | Delayed opening: 3 extra months absorbed | From property reserve, emergency fund untouched | Needs part of emergency fund | Cannot be absorbed | | |
| 8 | Weak first season | Weak-case loss covered 2+ years by property reserve | 1 to 2 years | Under 1 year | | |
| 9 | Time available | Weekly hours available exceed the plan's estimate, plus a local backup | Just enough | Not enough, and no budget for a co-host | | |
| 10 | Involvement choice tested | Deal still works with your chosen management model (0.2) | Works only if you self-manage and you're unsure you will | Doesn't work with the model you'll actually use | | |
| 11 | Risk tolerance | Both owners accept the five-year weak-case result in writing | One owner unsure | Either owner can't accept it | | |
| 12 | Personal use plan | Below the Pub 527 threshold, or reviewed by CPA | Plan unclear | Heavy personal use, not reviewed | | |
| 13 | Lease path considered | 0.2 Part C done; owning chosen for a stated reason | Not compared | Leasing clearly fits better and you'd still buy | | |

**Outcome rules**

| Result | Where you go next |
|---|---|
| All Green, or Yellow only on line 1 with a dated funding plan | **Proceed to PA.1.** Write the funding condition into your buy box: no offer until the stack is funded. |
| Yellow or Red on lines 3 to 6 | **Improve financing or liquidity first.** Get written quotes (PA.6, PA.7), pay down debt, or save. Rescore in 60 to 90 days. |
| Red on line 1 or 2, and lines 9 to 11 look fine | **Use the lease path (Part One).** A lease needs far less cash. Alder Street needed $20,370 on day one. Start with lesson 1.3. |
| Red on line 9, 10, or 11 | **Wait.** Fix the time or risk question before money is at stake. If the time problem is the only issue, consider whether a co-host still leaves the deal positive. |
| Two or more Reds anywhere | **Wait.** Rescore in six months. |

### 0.3.10 Maya and Chris, scored

| # | Check | Score | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Full cash stack | Yellow | Gap of $11,644 (DSCR) or $8,172 (conventional); about 3.3 or 2.3 months of saving at $3,500 a month (assumption) |
| 2 | Emergency savings | Green | They refuse to spend below 6 months, $51,000 |
| 3 | Property reserve | Green | $15,342 planned against an assumed 6-month rule |
| 4 | Credit | Green, pending lender | Around 740; lender confirms minimum |
| 5 | Lender fit | Yellow | Hypothetical quotes only; need written ones |
| 6 | Debt capacity / coverage | Green, pending lender | 35.6% illustrative DTI with no STR income counted |
| 7 | Delayed opening | Green | 3-month delay costs $9,771; reserve covers it with $5,571 left |
| 8 | Weak first season | Green | $15,342 covers about 2.8 years of the $5,475 weak-case loss |
| 9 | Time | Green | About 2.7 hours a week estimated (assumption); they name a local backup in PA.1 |
| 10 | Involvement | Yellow | Works only if they self-manage (0.2 Case B loses with a co-host) |
| 11 | Risk tolerance | Green | Both reviewed the -$27,503 five-year weak case and accept it |
| 12 | Personal use | Green | 10 days, below the 21.2-day threshold; CPA to confirm |
| 13 | Lease path | Green | Compared in 0.2; they chose owning for a long hold and personal use |

**Outcome:** Proceed to PA.1 with conditions. The Yellows are on lines 1, 5, and 10. Line 1 has a dated funding plan: about 3 to 4 months of saving, which runs while PA.2 to PA.4 happen. Line 5 is resolved in PA.6 and PA.7 by getting written quotes before any offer. Line 10 becomes a hard requirement in their buy box: the deal has to work with self-management, and they must name a local backup.

### 0.3.11 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** each lender, in writing, the reserve months and how they're calculated, the minimum credit score, and the down payment. Also confirm acceptable sources of funds (HELOC funds, gifts, retirement assets) and how rental income is counted.

**Confirm with:** your CPA the tax effect of retirement withdrawals, how your hours count for material participation, and how personal-use days are treated.

**Confirm with:** your insurance agent the premium and when it's due, so layer 2 or layer 6 of the stack is accurate.

**Confirm with:** the local permit office the fees and processing time, so the launch carrying-cost assumption is realistic.

### 0.3.12 Next lesson

If your scorecard says proceed, go to PA.1, Discovery: Defining Your Buy Box (Market, Property Type, Price Range, Goals). It turns your goals and numbers into a written search specification. Maya and Chris will carry their funding condition, their self-management requirement, and their reserve targets straight into it. If your scorecard said lease path, go to Part One, lesson 1.3. If it said wait, set a rescore date on your calendar now.

### 0.3 sources

- [Fannie Mae Selling Guide, B3-4.1-01 Minimum Reserve Requirements](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-4.1-01/minimum-reserve-requirements): reserves measured in months of the subject loan's PITIA; DU determines reserves for DU loans and the Eligibility Matrix sets them for manual loans; no minimum for one-unit principal residence; two months for a DU second-home transaction; additional reserves for multiple financed properties at 2% / 4% / 6% of aggregate UPB of other financed properties (excluding subject and principal residence). Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to fanniemae.com (direct fetch blocked). The exact month count for an investment property transaction was not shown in search results.
- [Fannie Mae Selling Guide, B3-3.8-03 Rental Income from the Subject Property: Short-Term Rental](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-3.8-03/rental-income-subject-property-short-term-rental): section exists within the September 2026 rental income chapter. Brief-provided; existence confirmed via web search 2026-09-25; details not independently opened.
- [Kiavi, The Complete Guide to DSCR Rental Property Loans](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans): the lender considers credit score and down payment as well as coverage ratio. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to kiavi.com. One lender's terms, not a market standard.
- [CFPB, What is a home equity line of credit (HELOC)?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-home-equity-line-of-credit-heloc-en-107/): variable rate, home as collateral, draw and repayment periods. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to consumerfinance.gov.
- [IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925): material participation tests compare your hours with other individuals'. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov.
- [IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527): greater of 14 days or 10% personal-use threshold; expense division by days. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov.
- Cedar Ridge and Alder Street figures: `reference/recurring-examples.md`. New figures are assumptions computed with python: 6-month reserve rule, $8,500 essential spending, $4,000 repairs, $1,500 inspections, $600 permits, 2 launch months, $3,500 monthly saving, $2,400 and $600 debts, hours per stay.

<a id="lesson-PA.1-discovery-buy-box"></a>
## PA.1  Discovery: Defining Your Buy Box (Market, Property Type, Price Range, Goals)

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 1 - Property Acquisition |
| Code | PA.1 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Preston's 0.3 (before); Sam's PA.2 market selection, PA.3 deal sourcing, PA.4 underwriting, PA.5 cost segregation (next); Preston's PA.6 (after Sam's lessons); SR.1 regulatory compliance checklist |

### PA.1.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you have a written, one-page buy box. It ranks your goals, states your constraints, names your hard requirements, and describes the property you're looking for in terms someone else can search with. It ends with a pass rule: a sentence that tells you when to walk away from a listing without debate. Sam's PA.2 and PA.3 lessons use this page to choose a market and build your property pipeline.

### PA.1.1 What a buy box is for

A buy box is a search specification. It says what you'll buy, where, at what price, and under what conditions. Anything outside the box, you don't look at twice.

It exists to protect you from two things.

The first is drift. You start looking for a three-bedroom house at $380,000. A month later you're touring a $450,000 house with a pool, because the photos were good and the agent said it "books great". Nobody decided to raise the budget by $70,000. It just happened, one listing at a time.

The second is wasted work. Every property you seriously consider costs hours: reading rules, pulling comparable listings, underwriting, calling lenders. Sam's PA.3 lesson builds a pipeline of 5 to 10 qualified properties. Without a buy box, "qualified" means nothing, and the pipeline fills with houses you should never have looked at.

A good buy box is short and specific. It's written before you see listings. And it's hard to change: if you want to change it, you rewrite it on purpose, with numbers.

### PA.1.2 Start with a goal hierarchy

Before property details, rank your goals. People usually want several things from an STR purchase, and they conflict. A ranking tells you which one wins when they do.

The common goals:

- **Cash flow.** Money left each month after all costs and the loan.
- **Long-term ownership.** Holding for many years and building equity through loan paydown and possible value change.
- **Personal use.** Using the house yourself some days each year.
- **Tax planning.** Depreciation and possible use of losses, which depend on facts your CPA confirms (lesson 0.2).
- **Portfolio growth.** Using this property as a step toward a second one.
- **Desired involvement.** How many hours you want to put in, and which jobs you want to hand off.

Here's how they conflict in practice:

- Personal use pulls against cash flow. Your own peak-season week is a week you didn't rent.
- Tax planning can pull against everything else. A deal bought "for the depreciation" can lose money every month. The deduction may also be unusable, depending on your household facts.
- Low involvement pulls against cash flow. In 0.2, a full-service co-host at 18% turned Cedar Ridge from $5,572 a year of cash flow to -$7,798.
- Portfolio growth pulls against safety. Stretching to buy now so you can buy again sooner leaves less reserve for this house.

Rank your goals from 1 to 6. Only the top two or three should shape the search. The rest are tie-breakers. If both owners rank differently, settle it now. A disagreement you skip now tends to come back at the offer.

### PA.1.3 Write down your constraints

Constraints are limits you can't wish away. They come mostly from lesson 0.3.

**Money.** Two caps. First, the maximum cash to close: down payment plus closing costs and points. Second, the maximum total cash in: cash to close plus furnishing. Both must leave your property reserve and personal emergency savings untouched. If your 0.3 scorecard carried a funding condition, write it here too.

**Financing path.** Which loan types you'll pursue (DSCR, conventional investment, or both) and the down payment you're planning around. The lender makes the eligibility call later, in PA.6 and PA.7. For now, write the path and the assumed terms.

**Distance.** How far the house can be from you. This isn't only about convenience. Distance decides whether you can handle a problem yourself or must pay someone. Write it in driving hours.

**Time.** The hours per week you'll actually give it, from 0.3.

**Minimum reserves.** The property reserve and personal emergency savings you won't spend below. These are floors, not targets.

### PA.1.4 Describe the property

Now the property itself. Keep each line concrete enough that Sam's PA.2 and PA.3 filters can apply it.

**Market type.** Drive-to leisure, urban, event-driven, business travel. Name the type and the demand driver. Sam's PA.2 picks the actual market.

**Property type.** Detached single-family, townhouse, condo, small multi-unit. This matters for legality (condo and HOA rules), for lending (some lenders restrict condos or certain property types), and for insurance.

**Location inside the market.** Rules can change street by street. Zoning districts, HOA boundaries, and permit caps can all differ within one town. Write where you'll look ("within 10 minutes of the lake") and where you won't.

**Price range.** A floor and a ceiling. The ceiling comes from your money constraints and your downside rule, not from what the market charges. Section PA.1.7 shows how to calculate it.

**Guest capacity.** Bedrooms, bathrooms, and how many guests it sleeps. More capacity can mean higher ADR, and also more wear, more cleaning time, and more noise risk. Local rules may cap occupancy too.

**Amenities.** Separate what guests in this market expect (parking, reliable internet, a working kitchen) from what adds complexity (pools, hot tubs, docks, boats). Every amenity with water or height carries insurance and safety questions. Your insurer's quote will tell you which ones cost extra or aren't covered.

**Operating complexity.** Septic and wells, long private roads, snow access, older systems, big lots. None of these is a deal-breaker by itself. Each adds cost, inspection work, or response time. Decide how much of it you'll accept.

### PA.1.5 Hard requirements

Hard requirements are the lines a property must clear, every one, before you spend real time on it. They aren't preferences. If a property fails one, you pass.

Five belong in almost every STR buy box:

1. **Legal STR use at the exact address.** Unhosted short-term rental use, for a non-owner-occupied house, must be allowed at that address. Any HOA or deed restrictions must allow it too. Airbnb itself tells hosts to check local laws and HOA rules before hosting ([Airbnb Help Center, article 1397](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1397)). Use the SR.1 checklist and record the official source.
2. **Acceptable insurance.** A written quote from an insurer willing to cover STR use of this house, at a premium your underwriting can carry. Platform protection is not a substitute; PA.12 covers that.
3. **Lender fit.** The property type, location, and projected income fit a lender's written guidelines for the loan you plan to use.
4. **Maximum cash to close and total cash in.** From PA.1.3. No exceptions for "a great house".
5. **Minimum downside performance.** A number for how badly the weak case may perform at your actual loan quote. For example, set a floor on weak-case annual cash flow after debt service. Then add a minimum base-case ratio of net operating income to principal and interest.

Add a sixth if 0.3 surfaced it. For Maya and Chris, it's "the deal must work with self-management".

### PA.1.6 The pass rule

The pass rule is one or two sentences you agree on in advance. It tells you what to do when a listing doesn't fit.

A good pass rule has three parts:

- **The trigger.** Any failed hard requirement. Or a price above the ceiling. Or a preference score below a stated level.
- **The action.** Pass, and log why. Don't "keep an eye on it" unless the price drops below the ceiling.
- **The change process.** The buy box changes only on purpose: in writing, with the new numbers, signed by every owner. Never during a showing.

Why log the pass? Because after 20 passes you'll see a pattern. Maybe every house in your price range fails the insurance quote. That's information for Sam's PA.2 market choice, not a reason to lower your standards.

### PA.1.7 Worked example: Maya and Chris's buy box, tested on Cedar Ridge

Maya and Chris finished 0.3 with "proceed to PA.1, with conditions". Here's how they turn their goals and numbers into a buy box. Canonical figures come from the course example file. New figures are marked (assumption).

**Their goal hierarchy**

| Rank | Goal | How it shapes the search |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Protect the household | Emergency fund stays at $51,000; property reserve stays at 6 months PITIA |
| 2 | Long-term ownership, 10+ year hold (assumption) | Favor durable houses and stable rules over a short-term price opportunity |
| 3 | Positive base-case cash flow while self-managing | Base case must be positive after debt service and reserves |
| 4 | Personal use, about 10 days, off-peak | Drive time matters; personal days go in slow weeks |
| 5 | Tax planning | Tie-breaker only; no deal is bought for depreciation |
| 6 | Portfolio growth | Not before the OP.10 review and OP.11 decision on property two |

Desired involvement: self-manage with a local cleaner and an on-call handyman, about 3 hours a week on average (assumption, from 0.3).

**Their constraints**

- Maximum cash to close: $115,000 (assumption).
- Maximum total cash in, including $32,000 of furnishing: $145,000 (assumption).
- Funding condition from 0.3: no offer until liquid savings cover the full stack.
- Financing: DSCR at the hypothetical canonical quote (7.25%, 1 point, 25% down), with a conventional quote requested for comparison.
- Distance: 2.5 hours' drive or less from home (assumption: they live in the metro that Cedar Ridge's market serves).
- Reserves: 6 months PITIA as property reserve (assumption), plus $51,000 personal emergency savings (assumption).

**Turning the money constraints into a price ceiling.** On the DSCR terms, cash to close is 25% down plus 3% closing costs. Add 1 point on a loan of 75% of price. So cash to close is 0.25 + 0.03 + 0.0075 = 28.75% of the price.

- Cash-to-close cap: $115,000 / 0.2875 = $400,000.
- Total-cash-in cap: ($145,000 - $32,000) / 0.2875 = $393,043.

The tighter cap wins. Their ceiling is **$393,000 contract price** on these terms.

**The downside rule.** Maya and Chris set two numbers (assumption):

- Weak-case annual cash flow after principal and interest: no worse than -$6,000.
- Base-case NOI divided by annual principal and interest: at least 1.20.

These are their own rules, not a lender's ratio. PA.6 covers how lenders define coverage, and the canonical file shows three different ratios for the same house.

**The buy box itself**

| Field | Maya and Chris |
|---|---|
| Market type | Drive-to lake leisure market; Sam's PA.2 selects the market |
| Location | Within 2.5 hours of home; within a 10-minute drive of lake access (assumption) |
| Property type | Detached single-family; no HOA, or HOA with written STR permission; no condos |
| Size | 3 to 4 bedrooms, 2+ baths, sleeps 6 to 8 |
| Price range | $325,000 (assumption) to $393,000 contract price |
| Financing | DSCR 25% down; conventional quote for comparison |
| Amenities wanted | Parking for 3 cars, reliable internet, outdoor space, year-round road access |
| Complexity limits | No pool; hot tub only if the insurer quotes it; septic and well accepted with inspection and water test; punch list under $4,000 |
| Hard requirements | (1) Legal unhosted STR use at the address, and HOA if any; (2) written STR insurance quote at or under $3,500 a year (assumption); (3) lender fit in writing; (4) cash to close at or under $115,000 and total cash in at or under $145,000; (5) weak case no worse than -$6,000 a year, base NOI/P&I at least 1.20; (6) works with self-management and a named local cleaner plus backup |
| Pass rule | Pass on any listing that fails a hard requirement, or whose price is above $393,000 unless the seller has shown they'll accept a price at or under it. Log every pass with the reason. The box changes only in writing, signed by both. |

**Test 1: Cedar Ridge at the contract price, $385,000.**

| Check | Result | Pass? |
|---|---|---|
| Cash to close | $110,688, under $115,000 | Yes |
| Total cash in | $142,688, under $145,000 | Yes |
| Weak-case cash flow | -$5,475, better than -$6,000 | Yes |
| Base NOI / annual P&I | $29,209 / $23,637 = 1.24 | Yes |
| Insurance | $3,200 a year in the canonical model; written quote needed | Pending |
| Legal STR use | Address-level check needed (PA.2, SR.1) | Pending |
| Property fit | 3 bed, 2 bath, sleeps 8, no HOA, drive-to lake market | Yes |

At $385,000, Cedar Ridge fits the box, subject to the two pending checks.

**Test 2: Cedar Ridge at the asking price, $399,000.** Same revenue and operating costs as the canonical case. One change: property tax scales with price at 1% (assumption, which matches the canonical $3,850 on $385,000). All figures below are assumptions computed with python.

| Check | Result | Pass? |
|---|---|---|
| Cash to close | $114,713 | Yes, barely |
| Total cash in | $146,713, over $145,000 | **No** |
| Weak-case cash flow | -$6,475, worse than -$6,000 | **No** |
| Base NOI / annual P&I | 1.19, under 1.20 | **No** |
| Base-case cash flow | $4,572 | (for reference) |

At asking price, the same house fails three hard requirements. So the $14,000 negotiation from $399,000 to $385,000 is what makes Cedar Ridge a deal for Maya and Chris. If the seller had refused to go below $399,000, their pass rule says walk. PA.8 covers setting that walk-away price.

The downside rule also gives a price ceiling on its own. For a house with Cedar Ridge's weak-case revenue, the weak case reaches -$6,000 at about $392,000 (assumption, same property-tax scaling). That sits right next to the $393,000 cash ceiling, which is a good sign that the two rules agree.

**Test 3: a pass example.** A four-bedroom house with a pool, listed at $435,000 in the same market (hypothetical). On their DSCR terms, cash to close would be $125,063 and total cash in $157,063. It fails the cash caps before anyone looks at revenue. It also has a pool, which is outside their complexity limit. Pass, logged as "over cash cap; pool". No underwriting needed.

That's the value of the box. The $435,000 house never takes an evening of their time.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

Fill this in and save it as one page. Bring it to Sam's PA.2 and PA.3.

### PA.1.9 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the city or county licensing office that unhosted STR use is allowed at each address that passes your other filters. Use the SR.1 checklist. If the answer is unclear, an STR attorney.

**Confirm with:** your insurance agent the premium and exclusions for STR use on each finalist, before the diligence window.

**Confirm with:** your lender that the property type, location, and planned use fit their loan, and the terms you've used in section C.

**Confirm with:** your CPA before tax planning is ranked above 4 in your goal list, so the ranking reflects your household's actual situation.

### PA.1.10 Next lesson

Take your buy box page to Sam's PA.2, Choosing a Market for Ownership: Regulatory-Safe, Cash-Flowing Areas. As you watch, apply your hard requirement 1 to each market Sam screens, and note which markets fit your drive-time and price range. Then Sam's PA.3, Deal Sourcing: Building a Pipeline of 5-10 Qualified Properties, shows how to fill a pipeline. Use your pass rule as the filter and keep the pass log going. Sam's PA.4, Reading a Full Underwriting Package: Revenue, Expenses, Cap Rate & Cash-on-Cash Return, is where your downside rule gets tested on a real listing. Sam's PA.5, Cost Segregation & Year-One Depreciation, Explained, covers the tax timing from 0.2 in detail. After those four, I pick up with PA.6, DSCR Loans 101: How They Qualify the Property, Not You. There, the financing line in your buy box becomes a written lender quote.

- Cedar Ridge figures (B1 to B6): `reference/recurring-examples.md`, all assumptions. New figures are assumptions computed with python from `tools/examples.py`. They include the $115,000 and $145,000 cash caps, the $393,000 and $392,000 ceilings, and the -$6,000 and 1.20 rules. Also: the $399,000 and $435,000 tests, 1% tax scaling, location rules, the $3,500 insurance cap, and the $325,000 floor.

<a id="lesson-PA.6-dscr-loans-101"></a>
## PA.6  DSCR Loans 101: How They Qualify the Property, Not You

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 1: Property Acquisition |
| Code | PA.6 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's PA.4 Reading a Full Underwriting Package (the revenue and expense inputs); lesson 0.3 (reserves and readiness); PA.7 (DSCR vs. conventional); PA.8 (offer ceiling); OP.11 (refinance) |

### PA.6.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can read a DSCR loan quote the way the lender reads it. You'll know which income the lender puts on top of the ratio and which payment goes underneath. You'll also see why a ratio above 1.0 can still leave you losing money. You'll leave with a written question list for two lenders and a table for comparing their written offers on the same house.

### PA.6.1 What the title means, and what it leaves out

The tracker calls this lesson "How They Qualify the Property, Not You." That's shorthand. It points at the main difference from a regular mortgage. A DSCR lender sizes the loan mostly on the property's rental income, not your pay stubs.

But the lender still looks at you. Kiavi's own [complete guide to DSCR rental loans](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans) says the lender considers the borrower's credit score, the available down payment, and the property's coverage ratio. Expect a DSCR lender to check some mix of these:

- your credit score and credit history
- how much cash you're putting in (the loan-to-value, or LTV)
- your reserves after closing, if that lender requires them
- the property type, condition, and appraisal
- whether short-term rental use is legal at that address
- the entity you'll hold title in, and who signs a personal guarantee

So "qualifies the property" means the property's income replaces your W-2 income in the math. It doesn't mean nobody asks about you. Kiavi's terms are one lender's terms. Other lenders publish different minimums, and the same lender can change its terms. Treat every lender page in this lesson as an example, then get your own written quote.

### PA.6.2 The ratio itself: income on top, payment underneath

DSCR stands for debt service coverage ratio. In plain words: how many dollars of property income the lender counts for every dollar of loan payment.

The top number (the numerator) is some version of the property's income. The bottom number (the denominator) is some version of the payment. Every lender writes its own definition. Kiavi's material describes a common version: gross rent divided by PITIA, which is principal, interest, property taxes, insurance, and any HOA dues ([Kiavi DSCR guide](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans)). Kiavi's material also says lenders calculate it with different inputs. Some use gross rent, some use a vacancy-adjusted figure, and some include items like flood insurance in the payment while others leave them out.

That flexibility is the whole problem. The same house can produce very different ratios depending on three choices the lender makes:

1. Which income: gross booking revenue, a discounted version of it, market rent from the appraiser, or net operating income after expenses.
2. Whose income estimate: your pro forma, the appraiser's opinion, a third-party data report, or actual booking history.
3. Which payment: principal and interest only, or full PITIA.

Kiavi's guide says a ratio of at least 1.1 is needed to prequalify and describes 1.25 or higher as strong. Your lender's minimum may be different, and pricing often changes with the ratio. Ask for the definition in writing before you compare any two quotes.

### PA.6.3 Cedar Ridge: one house, three ratios

Maya and Chris are buying Cedar Ridge for $385,000 with 25% down. All figures below are assumptions from the canonical Cedar Ridge example (Example B). The hypothetical DSCR quote is 7.25%, 1 point, 30-year fixed.

Start with the payment underneath. The loan is $288,750. Monthly principal and interest is $1,970. Property tax ($3,850 a year) and STR insurance ($3,200 a year) add $587.50 a month. That makes monthly PITIA $2,557, or $30,684 a year.

Now put three different incomes on top.

**Ratio 1: gross booking revenue / PITIA.** Base-case gross revenue is $74,275 (212 booked nights at a $300 ADR, plus cleaning fees).

$74,275 / $30,684 = **2.42**

**Ratio 2: gross revenue discounted 20% / PITIA.** Some lenders haircut projected STR income. The 20% haircut here is an assumption, not any lender's published rule.

$74,275 x 0.80 = $59,420. Then $59,420 / $30,684 = **1.94**

**Ratio 3: NOI before property tax and insurance / PITIA.** Net operating income is revenue after operating costs. Base-case NOI is $29,209, and that figure already has tax and insurance taken out. Add them back so they aren't counted twice (once in costs, once in PITIA): $29,209 + $3,850 + $3,200 = $36,259.

$36,259 / $30,684 = **1.18**

Same house, same loan, same year. The ratio runs from 1.18 to 2.42. The lender's written definition decides which one counts.

Now run the weak case. The canonical weak case drops ADR to $265 and occupancy to 50%. Gross revenue falls to $57,330 and NOI to $18,162. I computed the three ratios in Python from those canonical inputs:

| Definition | Base case | Weak case |
|---|---:|---:|
| Gross revenue / PITIA | 2.42 | 1.87 |
| Gross revenue x 80% / PITIA | 1.94 | 1.49 |
| NOI before tax and insurance / PITIA | 1.18 | 0.82 |

All figures are assumptions from the Cedar Ridge example.

### PA.6.4 Why a lender ratio above 1.0 doesn't mean you make money

Look at the weak case on the gross definition: 1.87. That sounds comfortable. It says the house brings in almost twice its loan payment.

Here's what Maya and Chris actually keep in the weak case. Weak-case NOI is $18,162. Annual principal and interest on the DSCR loan is $23,637. NOI already covers tax and insurance, so cash flow is NOI minus principal and interest:

$18,162 - $23,637 = **-$5,475 a year**, about -$456 a month.

A lender ratio of 1.87 and an owner losing $456 a month. Both are true at once.

The reason is simple. A gross-revenue ratio ignores the platform fee, the cleaner, utilities, supplies, software, repairs, and the capital reserve. An STR carries far more operating cost than a long-term rental where the tenant pays utilities. On Cedar Ridge, base-case operating costs are $45,066 on $74,275 of revenue. The gross ratio never sees that $45,066.

Even the base case is thin. Base-case cash flow is $29,209 - $23,637 = $5,572 a year, about $464 a month, before any reserves the lender requires you to hold.

So use the lender's ratio for one question only: will this lender make this loan at this size? Use your own NOI and cash flow for the other question: should you buy this house? Sam's PA.4 lesson builds that NOI line by line. Bring his operating-cost total to every lender conversation.

### PA.6.5 How the definition changes the loan size

The definition does more than pass or fail you. It can shrink the loan.

Say a lender uses the NOI-based definition (Ratio 3) and requires a minimum of 1.20 (assumption). The most PITIA it will accept is $36,259 / 1.20 = $30,216 a year. Take out $7,050 of tax and insurance and the most principal and interest it allows is $23,166 a year. At 7.25% over 30 years, that supports a loan of about **$282,989**.

Maya and Chris asked for $288,750. This lender would cut the loan by about $5,761, and they'd bring that much more cash to closing.

Under the 80% gross definition with a 1.25 minimum (assumption), the same house supports a loan well above what they asked for. The binding limit there becomes the lender's maximum LTV, not the ratio.

I computed both loan sizes in Python from the canonical inputs. The lesson for you: ask each lender for the maximum loan its definition supports on your numbers, not just the rate.

### PA.6.6 How a lender verifies STR income with no history

Cedar Ridge has no booking history under Maya and Chris. Neither will most houses you buy to convert. So where does the lender's income number come from?

Lenders use a few sources, and each lender picks its own:

- The appraiser's market rent. Kiavi says it requires an appraisal on its rental loans. Its rental loan page says the qualifying rent is the lower of 110% of appraised market rent or a valid lease rent ([Kiavi rental loans](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/rental)). That structure fits a long-term lease. Ask how the lender applies it to a house with nightly bookings and no lease.
- Short-term rental revenue. Kiavi publishes that it finances short-term rentals in some markets and that borrowers there can qualify on nightly rental revenue ([Kiavi Arizona DSCR programs](https://www.kiavi.com/arizona-dscr-loan-programs)). The page I could confirm through search doesn't say which data source or haircut it applies. Ask.
- Third-party revenue estimates. Some lenders accept a report from an STR data provider. Ask which provider, which comparable set, and whether they discount it.
- Actual history. On an existing STR, a lender may ask for 12 or 24 months of platform statements or tax returns. The seller's history may not transfer to you, and it may not reflect your pricing or costs.

The lender's number will rarely match your pro forma. It shouldn't. The lender is protecting a loan, so it will usually lean lower on income and ignore upside. When the lender's income is lower than yours, don't argue the loan up. Ask whether your pro forma is the one that's too high.

**Confirm with:** each lender, in writing, which income source it uses for a no-history STR purchase, any haircut, and whether the appraiser must be STR-experienced.

### PA.6.7 The other terms that decide the real cost

The rate gets all the attention. Six other terms decide what the loan actually costs and how much room you have later.

**Down payment and LTV.** Kiavi's guide says DSCR loans typically need a larger down payment than a primary-residence mortgage, often 20% to 25% ([Kiavi DSCR guide](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans)). The figure depends on property, lender, and borrower. Maximum LTV can drop for lower credit scores, lower ratios, or STR use. Cedar Ridge assumes 25%, which is $96,250.

**Rate and points.** A point is an upfront fee equal to 1% of the loan, usually paid to lower the rate. The [CFPB explains points and lender credits](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/how-should-i-use-lender-credits-and-points-also-called-discount-points-en-136/): points cost more upfront and less over time; credits do the reverse. On Cedar Ridge, 1 point is $288,750 x 1% = $2,888. Closing costs rise from $11,550 (3% of price) to $14,438.

**Prepayment penalty.** Many DSCR loans charge a fee if you pay the loan off early, including through a refinance or sale. A common shape is a step-down. Take a 3-2-1 structure (an example, not a standard). The penalty is 3% of the balance in year one, 2% in year two, 1% in year three, then nothing. Kiavi publishes 3-, 5-, and 7-year step-down options for its rental loans ([Kiavi rental loans for investors](https://www.kiavi.com/rental-loans-for-investors)). Other lenders use flat percentages, months of interest, or no penalty at a higher rate.

This matters for Cedar Ridge. The canonical example refinances at the end of year 2 (Example B7). The DSCR balance then is $282,951. Under a 3-2-1 schedule, a payoff in month 24 is still in year two:

$282,951 x 2% = **$5,659 penalty** (assumption: 3-2-1 schedule)

The canonical refinance shows $15,725 of cash out. Subtract the penalty and it drops to $10,066. Wait one more month, into year three, and the penalty falls to 1%. State law and loan type can limit prepayment penalties. Ask the lender how its penalty applies in your state.

**Reserves.** Some lenders require cash left in the bank after closing, usually measured in months of PITIA. Search results from Kiavi's site describe its DSCR process as having no cash reserve requirement ([Kiavi DSCR loans](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/dscr)). Other lenders do require reserves, so ask. Six months of PITIA on Cedar Ridge (assumption) is 6 x $2,557 = $15,342. Lesson 0.3 covered why that cash is on top of your cash to close.

**Entity and guarantor.** Many DSCR lenders let you close in an LLC. Kiavi's site says LLC borrowing is available for its DSCR rental loans, with requirements that vary ([Kiavi DSCR loans](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/dscr)). A lender may also ask the owners to sign a personal guarantee, which makes you personally liable if the LLC defaults. Ask who must guarantee, and whether title must be in the LLC before closing. PA.10 covers the entity decision itself.

**Permit and property eligibility.** A lender may refuse STR income where short-term rental use isn't allowed. It may also ask for proof of the permit. Some lenders exclude condos with rental restrictions, rural acreage, or unusual property types. Bring your SR.1 compliance checklist to the first call.

### PA.6.8 Maya and Chris's liquidity after the DSCR loan

Put these terms against the buyers' actual cash. Maya and Chris have $210,000 in liquid savings (canonical).

| Line | Amount |
|---|---:|
| Down payment (25%) | $96,250 |
| Closing costs incl. 1 point | $14,438 |
| Cash to close | $110,688 |
| Furnishing budget | $32,000 |
| Total cash in | $142,688 |
| Lender reserves, 6 months PITIA (assumption) | $15,342 |
| Savings left for everything else | $51,970 |

$210,000 - $142,688 - $15,342 = $51,970. That remaining $51,970 still has to cover their personal emergency fund and a weak first season. The weak case loses $5,475 a year. PA.8 turns this table into their offer ceiling.

### PA.6.9 The questions you send to two lenders

Send the same written questions to at least two lenders, on the same house, on the same day if you can. Rates change daily, so quotes from different days can differ for reasons that have nothing to do with the lender.

1. What is your exact DSCR formula? Which income goes on top, and which payment goes underneath?
2. For a short-term rental with no history, which income source do you use? Do you discount it? By how much?
3. What minimum ratio do you require for this loan, and does the rate or maximum LTV change with the ratio?
4. What is the maximum loan you'll offer on this address with my numbers?
5. What credit score tier am I in, and how does it change the rate or LTV?
6. What are the rate, points, and all lender fees? Please send a written quote or Loan Estimate.
7. What prepayment penalty applies? Show the schedule by year.
8. How many months of reserves do you require after closing, and which accounts count?
9. Can I close in an LLC? Who must sign a personal guarantee?
10. What proof of legal STR use or a permit do you need, and when?
11. Does your appraiser need STR experience? Who orders the market-rent or revenue report?
12. How long is the rate lock, and what does an extension cost?

### PA.6.10 Comparing two written offers

Put the two written answers side by side. The illustration below shows the format. Lender A is the canonical DSCR quote. Lender B is invented for this exercise.

| Term | Lender A (canonical) | Lender B (assumption) |
|---|---:|---:|
| Rate | 7.25% | 7.50% |
| Points | 1.0 | 0.0 |
| DSCR definition | Gross / PITIA | NOI-based / PITIA |
| Minimum ratio | ask | 1.20 |
| Max loan on Cedar Ridge | $288,750 | about $276,096 |
| Prepayment | ask | 3-2-1 |
| Reserves | ask | 6 months PITIA |
| Guarantee required | ask | yes |

All Lender B figures are assumptions. Lender B's maximum loan uses the NOI method from PA.6.5. It allows at most $23,166 a year of principal and interest. At 7.50% over 30 years, that supports about $276,096 (computed in Python).

Read this table in order. First, does the lender make the loan you need at the size you need? Lender B's loan is about $12,654 short, so Maya and Chris would need that much more cash. Second, what's the full upfront cost, including points and fees? Third, what does it cost you to leave early? Rate comes after those. A lower rate with a 5-year prepayment penalty may cost more than a higher rate if you plan the year-2 refinance in OP.11.

The [CFPB's guide to comparing loan offers](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/compare/compare-loan-estimates/) tells borrowers to request several Loan Estimates and focus on the fees that vary by lender. One catch for DSCR loans. Federal rules treat credit to buy a rental you won't live in as business-purpose credit ([Regulation Z commentary to § 1026.3](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/interp-3/)). So a DSCR lender may give you a term sheet instead of a Loan Estimate. Ask for every fee in writing either way.

### PA.6.11 Exercise: your DSCR lender worksheet

Fill this in for your own property with each lender's written answers. Use Sam's PA.4 package for your revenue and operating-cost lines.

| Line | Your number | Lender 1 | Lender 2 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Purchase price | | | |
| Loan amount requested | | | |
| Monthly P&I | | | |
| Monthly tax + insurance + HOA | | | |
| Annual PITIA | | | |
| Lender's income figure (and source) | | | |
| Lender's DSCR (their formula) | | | |
| Minimum ratio required | | | |
| Max loan the lender will offer | | | |
| Your base-case NOI (PA.4) | | | |
| Your weak-case NOI | | | |
| Your cash flow, base (NOI minus P&I) | | | |
| Your cash flow, weak | | | |
| Points and lender fees ($) | | | |
| Prepayment schedule | | | |
| Reserves required ($) | | | |
| Guarantee / entity terms | | | |
| Permit proof required | | | |
| Date of quote, rate lock length | | | |

Decision rule: if the lender's ratio passes but your weak-case cash flow is negative, write down how you'll cover the shortfall and for how long. If you can't answer that, you're not ready to make the offer in PA.8.

### PA.6.12 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** each lender, the DSCR formula, income source for a no-history STR, minimum ratio, maximum loan, points, fees, reserves, and prepayment schedule, all in writing and dated.

**Confirm with:** the lender and your attorney, whether a prepayment penalty is enforceable in your state and how it applies to a refinance or sale.

**Confirm with:** your attorney, what a personal guarantee commits you to and whether title must sit in the LLC at closing.

**Confirm with:** the local licensing office, that STR use and any permit the lender asks for are available at this exact address (use your SR.1 checklist).

**Confirm with:** your CPA, how points and loan costs are treated for tax purposes on a rental.

### PA.6.13 Next lesson

You now have one financing path priced out. PA.7 puts that DSCR quote next to a conventional quote on the same house and the same buyers. Bring your filled worksheet, because the conventional side will ask for your income documents, not just the property's.

### PA.6 sources

- [Kiavi: The Complete Guide to DSCR Rental Property Loans](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans): borrower credit, down payment, and DSCR all considered; DSCR as gross rent / PITIA with lender variation; 1.1 to prequalify; 20-25% typical down payment. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com); direct fetch blocked.
- [Kiavi: Rental Property Loans](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/rental): qualifying rent is the lower of 110% of appraised market rent and valid lease rent; appraisal required on rental loans; FICO and leverage as qualifiers. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com).
- [Kiavi: DSCR Loans for Real Estate Investors](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/dscr): LLC borrowing available; no cash reserve requirement. Search summaries stated these facts but did not tie each sentence to a single page; see sources-D.md Unresolved. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com).
- [Kiavi: Arizona DSCR loan programs](https://www.kiavi.com/arizona-dscr-loan-programs): Kiavi finances STRs in some markets and borrowers can qualify on nightly rental revenue. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com).
- [Kiavi: Rental Loans for Real Estate Investors](https://www.kiavi.com/rental-loans-for-investors): 3-, 5-, and 7-year step-down prepayment options. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com).
- [CFPB: points and lender credits](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/how-should-i-use-lender-credits-and-points-also-called-discount-points-en-136/): what a point is and the trade-off. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (consumerfinance.gov).
- [CFPB: Compare and negotiate your loan offers](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/compare/compare-loan-estimates/): request several Loan Estimates, rates change daily, compare fees that vary. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (consumerfinance.gov).
- [CFPB: Regulation Z commentary, § 1026.3 exempt transactions](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/interp-3/): credit to acquire non-owner-occupied rental property is deemed business purpose. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (consumerfinance.gov).
- Canonical Cedar Ridge numbers: reference/recurring-examples.md (Example B1, B2, B4, B5, B7); derived figures computed in Python from tools/examples.py on 2026-09-25.
