<a id="lesson-PA.7-dscr-vs-conventional"></a>
## PA.7  DSCR vs. Conventional Financing: Which Path Fits Your Deal

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 1: Property Acquisition |
| Code | PA.7 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | PA.6 (DSCR terms and lender questions); Sam's PA.4 Reading a Full Underwriting Package; PA.8 (offer ceiling); PA.10 (entity and title); OP.11 (refinance and property #2) |
| Tracker title note | The live tracker spells this title "DSCR vs. Conventional Financing: Which Path Fits Your Heal." That's a typo for "Deal," and this heading uses "Deal." |

### PA.7.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can put a DSCR quote and a conventional quote side by side for the same house and the same buyer. You'll see the difference in cash to close, monthly payment, five-year cost, paperwork, and flexibility. You'll use a decision tree to pick which route to pursue first, and you'll know that the lender makes the final eligibility call.

### PA.7.1 Two routes, one house

A DSCR loan sizes the loan mostly on the property's income. PA.6 covered how. A conventional loan sizes it mostly on you: your documented income, your existing debts, your credit, and your assets. The property still matters, but your household is the main borrower.

That one difference changes almost everything else. It changes which documents you gather and how long underwriting takes. It changes whether you can close in an LLC, and what happens to your borrowing capacity for the next property.

Here's the canonical side-by-side for Cedar Ridge. All figures are assumptions from Example B4. Both quotes are hypothetical: DSCR at 7.25% with 1 point, conventional at 6.875% with no points, both 30-year fixed, both 25% down.

| | DSCR quote | Conventional quote |
|---|---:|---:|
| Loan amount | $288,750 | $288,750 |
| Down payment | $96,250 | $96,250 |
| Monthly P&I | $1,970 | $1,897 |
| Monthly PITIA | $2,557 | $2,484 |
| Annual debt service (P&I) | $23,637 | $22,763 |
| Closing costs incl. points | $14,438 | $11,550 |
| Cash to close | $110,688 | $107,800 |
| Total cash in (with furnishing) | $142,688 | $139,800 |
| Principal paid in year 1 | $2,795 | $3,005 |
| Annual cash flow, base case | $5,572 | $6,446 |
| Annual cash flow, weak case | -$5,475 | -$4,601 |
| Annual cash flow, base with 18% co-host | -$7,798 | -$6,924 |
| Cash-on-cash, base case | 3.9% | 4.6% |

Reserves are not in these totals. Either lender may require months of PITIA on top of cash to close.

Two things stand out. The conventional quote is cheaper on every line. And neither quote fixes the weak case: both lose money when ADR drops to $265 and occupancy to 50%. Financing changes the size of the loss. It doesn't change the house.

### PA.7.2 How a conventional lender qualifies you

On a conventional loan, the lender underwrites your household. I'll use Fannie Mae's Selling Guide as the example because it's public. Your loan may follow Fannie Mae, another investor's guide, or a lender's own portfolio rules. Ask which guide applies.

Four things carry most of the weight.

The first is your debt-to-income ratio (DTI): your monthly debt payments divided by your gross monthly income. Fannie Mae's [DTI section, B3-6-02](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-6-02/debt-income-ratios) sets 36% as the maximum for manually underwritten loans. It allows up to 45% when the borrower meets the credit score and reserve requirements in the Eligibility Matrix. For loans run through Desktop Underwriter (DU), Fannie Mae's automated system, the maximum is 50%.

The second is documented income. W-2 wages, tax returns, and pay stubs. Self-employment income usually needs more paperwork and a longer history. The lender decides which income counts and how it's averaged.

The third is the occupancy type. Fannie Mae classifies a property as a principal residence, a second home, or an investment property ([B2-1.1-01 Occupancy Types](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b2-1.1-01/occupancy-types)). Investment property loans carry an extra price adjustment (an LLPA, or loan-level price adjustment), which is one reason investment rates run higher. For a one-unit investment property purchase, Fannie Mae's [Eligibility Matrix](https://singlefamily.fanniemae.com/media/20786/display) shows a maximum LTV of 85%, so at least 15% down. Credit score and other factors can push that lower.

Maya and Chris plan about 10 personal-use days a year at Cedar Ridge. Don't guess the occupancy type from that number. Tell the lender your real plan and let the lender classify it. Fannie Mae's [general rental income section, B3-3.8-01](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-3.8-01/rental-income) says rental income from a principal residence or second home generally can't be used to qualify. It lists narrow exceptions. So the occupancy type also decides whether STR income is even on the table.

The fourth is reserves and your other properties. Fannie Mae's [reserve rules, B3-4.1-01](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-4.1-01/minimum-reserve-requirements) add reserve requirements in one situation: the borrower has multiple financed properties and the new loan is on a second home or investment property. The requirement grows with the number of financed properties. Fannie Mae also sets rules on how many financed properties one borrower can have ([B2-2-03](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b2-2-03/multiple-financed-properties-same-borrower)). Maya and Chris would have two: their home and Cedar Ridge.

### PA.7.3 Maya and Chris's DTI, before any STR income

Here's a rough DTI check, and it's only an illustration. The lender runs the real one.

Maya and Chris earn $185,000 a year combined (canonical). That's $185,000 / 12 = $15,417 a month.

I need two numbers the canonical file doesn't give. Say their primary home payment (principal, interest, tax, insurance) is $2,400 a month (assumption) and car and card payments are $400 a month (assumption).

Today: ($2,400 + $400) / $15,417 = **18.2%**

Add the full Cedar Ridge conventional PITIA of $2,484 and count zero rental income:

($2,400 + $400 + $2,484) / $15,417 = **34.3%**

I computed both in Python. At 34.3%, they'd sit under all three Fannie Mae limits described above, even if the lender gave the STR no income credit at all. Their DTI rules depend on their actual debts, credit, reserves, and the underwriting system the lender uses, so treat this as a screening check.

This matters for the decision tree later. For a strong two-income household, the STR income question may not decide the loan. For a buyer whose DTI is already near the limit, it can decide everything.

### PA.7.4 Fannie Mae's new short-term rental income guidance

You'll hear people say conventional loans can't use Airbnb income. Don't repeat that. It's out of date.

On September 2, 2026, Fannie Mae published [Announcement SEL-2026-08](https://singlefamily.fanniemae.com/news-events/announcement-sel-2026-08-selling-guide-updates) ([PDF](https://singlefamily.fanniemae.com/media/48051/display)). It restructured the rental income chapter and added a section titled [B3-3.8-03, Rental Income from the Subject Property: Short-Term Rental](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-3.8-03/rental-income-subject-property-short-term-rental). The announcement says it introduces new requirements for using short-term rental income, including alternative documentation options.

On timing, the announcement says lenders are encouraged to put the changes in place immediately. Lenders must apply them to all loans with application dates on or after November 1, 2026. So on a loan application dated before November 1, 2026, the answer depends on whether your lender has already adopted the new section. Ask the loan officer directly.

Here's what I could not confirm from the text itself, because the full section wasn't readable from this research environment. The production brief describes B3-3.8-03 as allowing documented STR income for certain one-unit investment-property transactions. I couldn't independently verify the exact eligibility conditions. That includes which occupancy types qualify and whether it covers purchases, refinances, or both. It also includes the required operating history and which alternative documents a lender may accept. Those conditions decide whether Cedar Ridge qualifies. Here's the conditional version:

- If the lender confirms Cedar Ridge's transaction fits B3-3.8-03, it may count documented STR income under that section's calculation rules.
- If the property is classified as a second home, rental income generally can't be used to qualify (B3-3.8-01).
- If the transaction doesn't fit the section, the lender may still approve the loan on your W-2 income alone. The DTI check above suggests that's possible for Maya and Chris.

[Production note: before recording, the lender reviewer should read B3-3.8-03 in full and replace this list with its exact conditions. See the Unresolved list in parts/sources-D.md.]

**Confirm with:** the lender, whether the loan follows Fannie Mae's guide, whether B3-3.8-03 applies to this transaction and application date, and exactly which STR documents it will accept.

### PA.7.5 Five-year cost, not just monthly payment

The monthly payments differ by $73. That looks small. Over five years it adds up, and the points add more on day one.

I ran both loans for 60 months in Python. All figures are assumptions from the canonical quotes.

| Over 5 years | DSCR | Conventional | Difference |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Points paid at closing | $2,888 | $0 | $2,888 |
| Other closing costs (3% of price) | $11,550 | $11,550 | $0 |
| Total P&I paid | $118,187 | $113,813 | $4,374 |
| Interest paid | $101,956 | $96,502 | $5,454 |
| Principal paid | $16,231 | $17,311 | -$1,080 |
| Loan balance at month 60 | $272,519 | $271,439 | $1,080 |
| Interest + points + closing costs | $116,394 | $108,052 | **$8,342** |

The DSCR loan costs Maya and Chris about $8,342 more over five years. That's $5,454 of extra interest plus $2,888 of points. They'd also owe $1,080 more at the end, which is already inside the extra interest figure.

Cash flow tells the same story from the operating side. Holding revenue flat for five years (assumption):

- Base case: DSCR $5,572 x 5 = $27,860. Conventional $6,446 x 5 = $32,230.
- Weak case: DSCR -$5,475 x 5 = -$27,375. Conventional -$4,601 x 5 = -$23,005.

Now add the exit. The canonical sale happens at the end of year 5 (Example B8). Say the DSCR loan has a 5-year step-down penalty of 5-4-3-2-1 (assumption; an example structure, not a standard). A payoff in month 60 is still in year five, so the penalty is 1%:

$272,519 x 1% = **$2,725**

With a 3-2-1 schedule (assumption), there'd be no penalty at year five. Ask the conventional lender to confirm in writing whether any prepayment penalty applies to its loan.

### PA.7.6 Comparing the written quotes

The CFPB's [guide to comparing loan offers](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/compare/compare-loan-estimates/) tells you to request several Loan Estimates and focus on the fees that vary by lender. It also warns that rates change daily, so quotes issued on different days may differ for market reasons. Get both quotes on the same day if you can.

Compare these lines on each written quote:

1. Interest rate, and whether it's locked, for how long.
2. APR where available. The CFPB explains that the [APR includes the rate plus points and certain other charges](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-the-difference-between-a-mortgage-interest-rate-and-an-apr-en-135/), so it's usually higher than the rate. It helps compare loans with different rates and fees.
3. Points and lender credits, in dollars.
4. Estimated cash to close. The CFPB's [Loan Estimate explainer](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/loan-estimate/) says it includes your down payment and closing costs, minus your deposit and any seller credits.
5. Monthly payment, both principal and interest and full PITIA.
6. Reserves required after closing, in dollars.
7. Prepayment penalty, year by year.
8. What the lender will require at refinance: seasoning period, maximum cash-out LTV, and how it will count STR income then.

One wrinkle. Federal rules treat credit to buy a rental property you won't occupy as business-purpose credit ([Regulation Z commentary to § 1026.3](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/interp-3/)). That can mean a lender isn't required to give you a Loan Estimate on this kind of loan. Some lenders will anyway. Others send a term sheet. Either way, ask for every rate, fee, and penalty in writing, in the same layout, so the comparison is fair. A DSCR term sheet may not show an APR. That's why the five-year table above matters.

### PA.7.7 Flexibility and downside

Price isn't the only difference. Here's what each route tends to give and take. Confirm every line with the actual lender.

| Question | DSCR (typical, varies by lender) | Conventional (typical, varies by lender) |
|---|---|---|
| Whose income qualifies? | Mostly the property's | Mostly yours, plus rental income where eligible |
| Close in an LLC? | Often allowed ([Kiavi example](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/dscr)) | Ask; often in personal names |
| Paperwork | Shorter: no tax returns at Kiavi, per its site | Full income and asset documentation |
| Effect on your DTI now | Not used to qualify this loan | Central to approval |
| Effect on the next loan | A future conventional lender will count this loan under its own rules | Same |
| Prepayment penalty | Common | Ask |
| Rate and cost | Usually higher | Usually lower for strong borrowers |
| Reserves | Lender-specific | Rise with number of financed properties (B3-4.1-01) |

Fannie Mae's [rules on other real estate owned, B3-6-06](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-6-06/qualifying-impact-other-real-estate-owned) govern how a future conventional lender counts Cedar Ridge. That matters when Maya and Chris apply for property two. Keep clean records from day one: booking statements, the Schedule E the CPA files, and the loan payment history. OP.11 picks this up.

### PA.7.8 Decision tree

Use this tree to decide which lender to call first. It is a way to order your calls. The lender makes the actual eligibility decision.

**Branch A: first purchase, documented W-2 income.**
1. Is your DTI with the new full payment and no rental income under the lender's limit? If yes, get a conventional quote first.
2. Will the lender classify the property as an investment property? If no, ask what that means for rental income and occupancy rules.
3. Do you need to close in an LLC now? If yes, and the conventional lender won't allow it, get a DSCR quote.
4. Get a DSCR quote anyway as a backup. Keep it live until the conventional approval is in writing.

**Branch B: refinance of an STR you already own.**
1. Do you have 12 months or more of platform statements and a filed Schedule E? Ask the conventional lender whether B3-3.8-03 or its own rules would count that income.
2. Does your current loan have a prepayment penalty? Price it in dollars before you compare.
3. Will the refinance leave the property's weak-case cash flow at or above your rule? If not, the refinance is making the property riskier.
4. Compare the conventional refinance with a DSCR refinance on the same day, same loan amount.

**Branch C: complex or thin income documentation.**
This covers self-employed borrowers with a short history, recent job changes, large write-offs, or DTI already near the limit.
1. Ask a conventional lender for a pre-underwrite of your income before assuming you don't qualify.
2. If your documented income won't carry the payment, a DSCR loan may fit. Expect a higher cost and plan for it in PA.8.
3. Check that the property's weak-case cash flow works on DSCR pricing. The DSCR route removes the income test. It doesn't remove the risk.

### PA.7.9 Worked example: which path for Maya and Chris

Run Cedar Ridge through Branch A.

Step 1. DTI with the full conventional PITIA and no STR income: 34.3% (illustration, assumptions). That's under Fannie Mae's limits described above. Conventional quote first.

Step 2. Occupancy type. They plan about 10 personal-use days a year and rent the rest. They tell the lender that plan and ask for the classification in writing. This lesson assumes investment property.

Step 3. LLC. They'd like liability separation, but the lender's rules and PA.10's entity work aren't done yet. They ask the conventional lender whether an LLC can hold title and note the answer.

Step 4. Cost. Conventional saves $2,888 at closing and about $8,342 over five years (computed above). Base-case cash flow is $874 a year better: $6,446 - $5,572.

Step 5. Backup. They keep the DSCR quote live. If the conventional underwriting stalls on occupancy, reserves, or documentation, they switch without losing the house.

Their decision, written down: pursue conventional first; keep DSCR as the backup; use the DSCR numbers in PA.8 until the conventional approval is in writing. That last choice is deliberate. You set your offer ceiling on the more expensive loan you might end up with.

### PA.7.10 Exercise: your financing comparison

Fill in one column per written quote. Use your PA.6 worksheet for the DSCR side.

| Line | Quote 1: ______ | Quote 2: ______ |
|---|---:|---:|
| Lender, loan type, date of quote | | |
| Rate / APR (if shown) | | |
| Points ($) and lender credits ($) | | |
| Loan amount and LTV | | |
| Monthly P&I / monthly PITIA | | |
| Estimated cash to close | | |
| Reserves required ($) | | |
| Prepayment penalty by year | | |
| Occupancy type the lender assigned | | |
| Rental income counted? Which method? | | |
| Your DTI per the lender | | |
| Title: personal names or LLC? | | |
| 5-year interest + points + closing costs | | |
| Base-case annual cash flow (your NOI minus P&I) | | |
| Weak-case annual cash flow | | |
| Refinance terms the lender would expect | | |

Then answer in one line each:

1. Which branch of the decision tree are you on?
2. Which quote costs less over your expected hold?
3. Which quote keeps weak-case cash flow closer to breakeven?
4. What would make you switch to the backup quote?

### PA.7.11 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the conventional lender, the occupancy classification, whether the loan follows Fannie Mae's guide, whether B3-3.8-03 applies to this transaction and application date, your DTI, the reserves required for all financed properties, and whether an LLC can hold title.

**Confirm with:** the DSCR lender, the items in PA.6's question list, especially the prepayment schedule and maximum loan.

**Confirm with:** your CPA, how you'll document STR income on your tax return so a future lender can use it, and how points are treated.

**Confirm with:** your attorney, what signing in personal names versus an LLC means for liability and title (PA.10).

### PA.7.12 Next lesson

You've picked a financing route and a backup. PA.8 uses the more expensive of the two quotes to set Maya and Chris's walk-away price before they negotiate. Bring your comparison table and Sam's PA.4 underwriting package.

### PA.7 sources

- [Fannie Mae B3-3.8-03, Rental Income from the Subject Property: Short-Term Rental](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-3.8-03/rental-income-subject-property-short-term-rental): section exists under the restructured rental income chapter. URL brief-provided; section title confirmed via WebSearch (selling-guide.fanniemae.com) 2026-09-25; full text not readable (fetch blocked).
- [Fannie Mae Announcement SEL-2026-08](https://singlefamily.fanniemae.com/news-events/announcement-sel-2026-08-selling-guide-updates) and [PDF](https://singlefamily.fanniemae.com/media/48051/display): published September 2, 2026; new STR income requirements with alternative documentation options; encouraged immediately, required for application dates on and after November 1, 2026. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (singlefamily.fanniemae.com).

<a id="lesson-PA.8-offer-strategy"></a>
## PA.8  Offer Strategy & Negotiation for Investment Property

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 1: Property Acquisition |
| Code | PA.8 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's PA.4 Reading a Full Underwriting Package (the NOI inputs); PA.6 and PA.7 (loan terms); PA.9 (due diligence during the contingency period); SR.1 (compliance checklist) |

### PA.8.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can set a walk-away price before you talk to the seller. You'll build it from conservative net operating income, your loan terms, and the cash you actually have. You'll know which contract terms protect that number: earnest money, contingencies, appraisal gap, seller concessions, closing date, and furniture. And you'll record every negotiation decision as proceed, renegotiate, or walk, with the reason.

### PA.8.1 Set the ceiling before you see the seller's number

The asking price is the seller's opening position. It tells you what the seller hopes to get. It says nothing about what the house can carry as a rental.

A common way STR offers go wrong is working backward from the asking price. The buyer sees $399,000, then nudges the nightly rate up until the spreadsheet works. Now the offer depends on the most optimistic line in the model.

Reverse the order. Decide the most you'll pay first, from numbers you'd defend to a lender. Then negotiate below that number. If the seller won't come under it, you walk, and you already know why.

Sam's PA.4 lesson builds the underwriting package: revenue, expenses, cap rate, and cash-on-cash return. This lesson takes two lines from that package, base-case NOI and weak-case NOI, and turns them into a price. Don't redo PA.4's work here. Bring his outputs.

### PA.8.2 Three tests that set a walk-away price

Your walk-away price is the lowest of three ceilings.

**Test 1: base-case cash flow.** At your expected revenue, the house must produce at least a minimum annual cash flow after the loan payment. You set the minimum. It should cover more than zero, because base-case years still bring surprises.

**Test 2: weak-case survival.** In a weak year, the house should break even. If it can't, you need a written, funded plan for the shortfall. A buyer who can't fund a weak year should hold the strict breakeven line.

**Test 3: cash.** Everything you put in, plus the reserves you promised yourself, must fit inside the cash you have. Count the down payment, closing costs, points, furnishing, lender reserves, a weak-year reserve, and your personal emergency fund.

Use the more expensive financing quote for all three tests. Maya and Chris chose conventional first in PA.7, with DSCR as the backup. Until the conventional approval is in writing, they underwrite on the DSCR quote: 7.25%, 1 point, 25% down.

Why cash flow and not cap rate? The tracker asks for an offer ceiling from conservative NOI and cash required. Cap rate uses NOI but ignores your loan. Cash flow shows what the loan does to that NOI. Both belong in Sam's PA.4 package. This lesson uses the one that decides whether you can hold the house.

### PA.8.3 Worked example: Cedar Ridge's walk-away price

Canonical inputs (all assumptions from Example B): base-case NOI $29,209; weak-case NOI $18,162; property tax $3,850 and insurance $3,200, already inside NOI; furnishing $32,000; closing costs 3% of price plus points. Maya and Chris have $210,000 in liquid savings.

Three new rules are theirs to set. I've marked them as assumptions:

- Minimum base-case cash flow: $5,000 a year (assumption).
- Weak-case rule: a shortfall no larger than $6,000 a year (assumption), with two years of that shortfall held in a separate reserve.
- Personal emergency fund untouched: $30,000 (assumption). Lender reserves: 6 months of PITIA (assumption).

I ran every step in Python. Here's the arithmetic.

**Test 1.** NOI already covers tax and insurance, so cash flow is NOI minus principal and interest.

Maximum annual P&I = $29,209 - $5,000 = $24,209, or $2,017 a month.

At 7.25% over 30 years, $2,017 a month supports a loan of about $295,732.

With 25% down, the loan is 75% of price. Price ceiling = $295,732 / 0.75 = **$394,310**.

**Test 2, strict version.** Breakeven in the weak case means P&I can't exceed weak NOI.

Maximum annual P&I = $18,162. That supports a loan of about $221,863.

Price ceiling = $221,863 / 0.75 = **$295,818**.

That's $103,182 under the asking price. Could more cash close the gap instead? At $385,000, a weak-case breakeven loan of $221,863 means a down payment of $163,137, which is 42.4% of the price. Add closing costs and the $32,000 furnishing budget and their $210,000 is gone. Under the strict rule, Cedar Ridge doesn't work anywhere near the asking price.

**Test 2, funded-shortfall version.** Maya and Chris have two W-2 incomes and savings. They decide in writing that they can carry a weak-year shortfall of up to $6,000 a year, and they set aside two years of it.

Maximum annual P&I = $18,162 + $6,000 = $24,162. Price ceiling = **$393,544**.

That's a real choice, and they're making it on purpose. A buyer without their income or savings should use the strict test and pass on this house.

**Test 3.** Total cash needed at a price P has seven parts. They are 25% down, 3% closing costs, 1 point, $32,000 furnishing, 6 months of PITIA, two years of weak-case shortfall, and the $30,000 emergency fund. The cash ceiling is the highest price where that total stays at or under $210,000. Python finds it at about **$409,984**.

**The walk-away price.** Take the lowest ceiling: $393,544 from the funded weak-case test, just under Test 1's $394,310. Round down: **$393,000**.

The asking price of $399,000 fails. Their eventual contract price of $385,000 passes.

Here's the same check at four prices. All figures are assumptions, DSCR quote.

| Price | Loan (75%) | Total cash in | Annual P&I | Base cash flow | Weak cash flow |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| $399,000 | $299,250 | $146,712 | $24,497 | $4,712 | -$6,335 |
| $392,000 | $294,000 | $144,700 | $24,067 | $5,142 | -$5,905 |
| $385,000 | $288,750 | $142,688 | $23,637 | $5,572 | -$5,475 |
| $372,000 | $279,000 | $138,950 | $22,839 | $6,370 | -$4,677 |

At $399,000, both tests fail: base cash flow is under $5,000 and the weak shortfall is over $6,000.

At $385,000, the cash plan looks like this:

| Use of cash at $385,000 | Amount |
|---|---:|
| Total cash in (cash to close + furnishing) | $142,688 |
| Lender reserves, 6 months PITIA (6 x $2,557) | $15,342 |
| Weak-year reserve, 2 x $5,475 | $10,950 |
| Personal emergency fund | $30,000 |
| **Total committed** | **$198,980** |
| Unallocated from $210,000 | $11,020 |

One more caution. The model holds property tax at $3,850. Some counties reassess after a sale, and the tax could rise. Ask your agent or the county assessor what the tax would be at your price before you finalize the ceiling.

### PA.8.4 The terms that protect your ceiling

Price is one term. The others decide how much risk you carry between signing and closing. Purchase contracts are state-specific, often on a standard form your agent uses. Treat everything below as questions for your agent and attorney, not as rules.

**Earnest money.** This is the deposit you put down when the seller accepts, often held by an escrow or title company. It shows the seller you're serious. It goes toward your cash to close. The CFPB's [Loan Estimate explainer](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/loan-estimate/) notes that your estimated cash to close is reduced by any deposit you've already paid. Whether you get it back if the deal fails depends on the contract and which contingencies are still open. A larger deposit can make an offer stronger. It also raises what you could lose if you miss a deadline.

**Inspection contingency.** This gives you a set number of days to inspect and then cancel or renegotiate. For an STR, you're also testing the business: permits, HOA or deed restrictions, insurance quotes, septic capacity for your guest count. PA.9 builds that list. Make sure the period is long enough to get STR insurance quotes and a written answer from the licensing office.

**Financing contingency.** This lets you cancel if your loan isn't approved by a date. Match the date to your lender's realistic timeline, not the seller's preference. If you're on the conventional route with a DSCR backup, the date needs room for a switch.

**Appraisal contingency and appraisal gap.** Lenders generally size the loan on the lower of the price or the appraised value; ask yours to confirm. If the appraisal comes in low, the loan shrinks and you need more cash. An appraisal contingency lets you renegotiate or cancel. An appraisal gap clause promises the seller you'll cover some or all of a shortfall with cash. Only offer gap coverage you've already counted in Test 3.

**Seller concessions.** The seller pays some of your closing costs, usually as a credit. The CFPB's Loan Estimate explainer notes that seller credits reduce your estimated cash to close. Lenders can limit how much the seller contributes and what the money can pay for, and the limit may depend on the occupancy type. Ask your lender for its limit in dollars before you ask the seller.

**Closing date.** Two things drive it. Your rate lock has an expiration date, and extensions cost money. Your launch season matters too: closing in time to furnish, photograph, and list before the lake season is worth more than a small price cut. Work backward from the date you want the first guest.

**Furniture and equipment.** A seller may offer to include furniture, a golf cart, a boat lift, or a hot tub. Ask three questions. Does it fit your design and guest plan (OP.6)? Is it in usable condition? How does your lender treat personal property included in the price? Ask before you agree to pay for it. If you want an item, list it clearly in the contract or on a separate bill of sale. If you don't, say it's excluded so the seller removes it.

**Existing bookings.** If the house already takes guests, ask whether future reservations transfer, who keeps those payments, and whether the platform even allows a transfer. PA.9 picks this up.

### PA.8.5 Before you waive or shorten a contingency

Sellers sometimes ask for a shorter inspection period, no financing contingency, or appraisal gap coverage. Get written answers to these before you agree.

Ask your agent:

1. If I shorten the inspection period, can we still fit the inspection, the septic or sewer check, and the STR insurance quote?
2. What happens to my earnest money if I cancel after this contingency expires?
3. What's normal in this market for this type of house? Is the seller's request unusual?
4. Can the permit or licensing answer arrive before the inspection period ends?

Ask your lender:

1. How many days do you realistically need to approve this loan with this property type?
2. What happens to my loan if the appraisal comes in $10,000 low?
3. Does your approval depend on proof of STR permit eligibility? When do you need it?
4. What's your limit on seller credits for this occupancy type?
5. When does my rate lock expire, and what does an extension cost?

If any answer puts your earnest money at risk for a problem you can't test in time, keep the contingency.

### PA.8.6 What changes after a low appraisal or an inspection finding

Two practice scenarios, both what-ifs on the accepted $385,000 contract. All figures are assumptions. I set one more rule for Maya and Chris: keep at least $10,000 of the $210,000 unallocated after every commitment (assumption).

**Scenario 1: the appraisal comes in at $375,000.**

The lender lends 75% of the lower value: $375,000 x 0.75 = $281,250. That's $7,500 less than planned.

| Option | Price | Cash to close | Total committed | Unallocated | Rule met? |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Pay the full gap | $385,000 | $118,112 | $204,870 | $5,130 | No |
| Split the gap | $380,000 | $112,962 | $199,720 | $10,280 | Yes |
| Seller drops to value | $375,000 | $107,812 | $194,570 | $15,430 | Yes |

The smaller loan lowers PITIA to $2,506, so reserves and the weak-year shortfall shrink a little. Base cash flow rises to $6,186 and the weak case improves to -$4,861 in every option, because the loan is the same $281,250.

Decision rule: renegotiate. Ask the seller to drop to $375,000. Accept anything up to $380,000. If the seller holds at $385,000 and the appraisal contingency is still open, walk. They'd have to spend their margin to pay a price the appraiser didn't support.

**Scenario 2: the inspection finds $4,500 of work (assumption).**

Say the water heater is at the end of its life and several deck boards are soft. If Maya and Chris absorb the cost, total committed rises to $198,980 + $4,500 = $203,480. Unallocated cash falls to $6,520, under their $10,000 rule.

Decision rule: renegotiate. Ask the seller to repair before closing, or to reduce the price. Ask the lender first whether a seller credit can go toward repairs or only toward closing costs. If the finding is a safety issue for guests, it gets fixed before launch either way. PA.12 covers that sign-off.

A different finding changes the answer. A failing septic system sized for fewer guests than you planned isn't a price problem. It can break the business plan. That's a walk unless the fix and the permit both work on paper.

### PA.8.7 Mock offer and counteroffer

Here's how the Cedar Ridge negotiation could run. The terms and amounts are assumptions for teaching, except the canonical asking and contract prices.

**Round 1: Maya and Chris offer.**

| Term | Offer |
|---|---|
| Price | $372,000 |
| Earnest money | $3,720 (1%) |
| Inspection contingency | 10 days |
| Financing contingency | 30 days |
| Appraisal contingency | Yes, no gap coverage |
| Seller concessions | None requested |
| Closing | 45 days |
| Furniture | Excluded; seller removes |

Why $372,000? It's $21,000 under their $393,000 walk-away price. That leaves room for two counters without crossing the line.

**Round 2: the seller counters.**

| Term | Counter |
|---|---|
| Price | $392,000 |
| Earnest money | 2% |
| Inspection contingency | 7 days |
| Closing | 30 days |
| Furniture | Included for an extra $8,000 |

Maya and Chris check the counter against their tests. $392,000 is under $393,000, but only by $1,000. Base cash flow is $5,142, barely above the $5,000 minimum. The weak shortfall is $5,905, just inside $6,000. It passes on paper with almost no margin.

The terms matter too. Seven days isn't enough to get the septic inspection and an STR insurance quote, according to their agent. A 30-day close is tight for their lender. The furniture doesn't fit the design plan in OP.6, and their lender told them it won't count it in the loan (assumption for this mock).

**Round 3: Maya and Chris counter.**

| Term | Counter |
|---|---|
| Price | $385,000 |
| Earnest money | $7,700 (2%), accepting the seller's request |
| Inspection contingency | 10 days |
| Financing contingency | 30 days |
| Appraisal contingency | Yes, no gap coverage |
| Closing | 45 days |
| Furniture | Excluded |

They gave the seller what cost them little: a larger deposit they plan to put toward closing anyway. They held the terms that protect their ceiling: inspection time, financing time, and the appraisal contingency.

The seller accepts at $385,000, which is $14,000 (3.5%) under asking. That's the canonical contract price. No seller credit, so the canonical cash to close of $110,688 stands.

**Their decision log.**

| Date | Event | Numbers | Decision | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Day 0 | Offer sent | $372,000 | Proceed | $21,000 under walk-away |
| Day 2 | Seller counter | $392,000, 7-day inspection | Renegotiate | Price inside ceiling by $1,000; inspection period too short |
| Day 3 | Contract accepted | $385,000, 10-day inspection | Proceed | Passes all three tests; $11,020 unallocated |
| If appraisal low | Value $375,000 | Gap $10,000 | Renegotiate; walk above $380,000 | Unallocated cash rule |
| If inspection finds work | $4,500 | | Renegotiate | Unallocated cash rule |

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### PA.8.8 Exercise: your walk-away worksheet and decision log

**Part 1: set your ceiling.** Use Sam's PA.4 numbers and your more expensive financing quote.

| Input | Your number | Source |
|---|---:|---|
| Base-case NOI | | PA.4 |
| Weak-case NOI | | PA.4 |
| Rate, term, points | | Written quote (PA.6 / PA.7) |
| Down payment % | | Written quote |
| Closing costs % | | Lender or agent estimate |
| Furnishing and launch budget | | OP.6 plan |
| Liquid cash available | | Bank statements |
| Minimum base-case cash flow | | Your rule |
| Weak-case rule (breakeven, or maximum funded shortfall) | | Your rule |
| Personal emergency fund kept aside | | Your rule |
| Lender reserves required | | Written quote |
| Minimum unallocated cash | | Your rule |

| Ceiling | Formula | Result |
|---|---|---:|
| Test 1 | (Base NOI minus minimum cash flow) → max loan at your rate → divide by loan-to-price | |
| Test 2 | (Weak NOI plus allowed shortfall) → max loan → divide by loan-to-price | |
| Test 3 | Highest price where total committed cash ≤ liquid cash | |
| **Walk-away price** | Lowest of the three, rounded down | |

To turn an annual P&I limit into a loan amount, use your lender's calculator or a spreadsheet's PV function: PV(rate/12, months, -monthly payment).

**Part 2: your offer terms.** Fill in price, earnest money, each contingency and its days, appraisal gap (if any, and the dollar cap), seller concessions (in dollars, within the lender's limit), closing date, and included or excluded items.

**Part 3: your decision log.** For every offer, counter, appraisal, and inspection result, record the date, the numbers, and one of three decisions: proceed, renegotiate, or walk. Write the reason in one line, tied to a test or a rule.

### PA.8.9 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** your real estate agent, the local purchase contract form, contingency periods, earnest money handling, and what's normal in this market.

**Confirm with:** your attorney, what each contingency lets you do, when your earnest money is at risk, and how any appraisal gap clause is worded.

**Confirm with:** your lender, the maximum seller credit for this occupancy type, whether it can cover repairs, how personal property in the price is treated, the approval timeline, and the rate lock expiration.

**Confirm with:** the county assessor or your agent, the likely property tax at your purchase price.

**Confirm with:** your insurer, an STR quote inside the inspection period.

**Confirm with:** the local licensing office, that STR use is allowed at this address before the inspection contingency expires (SR.1).

### PA.8.10 Next lesson

Maya and Chris have an accepted contract at $385,000 and a 10-day inspection clock running. PA.9 fills that window: STR-specific inspections, the appraisal, and the title review, with a tracker that assigns an owner and a deadline to every item. Bring your decision log, because every finding in PA.9 gets a proceed, renegotiate, or walk entry.

### PA.8 sources

- [CFPB: Loan Estimate explainer](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/loan-estimate/): estimated cash to close includes down payment and closing costs, minus the deposit already paid and seller credits. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (consumerfinance.gov).
- [CFPB: Compare and negotiate your loan offers](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/compare/compare-loan-estimates/): background on comparing loan terms before committing. Brief-provided; checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch.
- [CFPB: Schedule a home inspection](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/schedule-a-home-inspection/): inspection vs. appraisal distinction (developed in PA.9). Brief-provided, not independently opened.
- Canonical Cedar Ridge numbers: reference/recurring-examples.md (B1, B2, B4). All ceilings, the four-price table, the cash plan, and both scenarios were computed in Python from tools/examples.py on 2026-09-25.

<a id="lesson-PA.9-due-diligence"></a>
## PA.9  Due Diligence: STR-Specific Inspections, Appraisal & Title Review

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 1 Property Acquisition |
| Code | PA.9 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's PA.4 Reading a Full Underwriting Package (the baseline this lesson revises); PA.8 Offer Strategy (the contingencies this lesson uses); PA.11 Closing Day; resource SR.1 Regulatory compliance checklist |

### PA.9.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you can run a diligence sequence on a house you have under contract. You'll test the building and the proposed rental business before your contingency deadlines expire. You'll know which professional verifies each risk, what written evidence to save, and how each finding changes the underwriting you brought from Sam's PA.4. The output is a filled diligence tracker and a dated proceed, renegotiate, or walk decision.

### PA.9.1 The clock starts when the contract is signed

Your accepted offer from PA.8 bought you time, and that time is the real asset in this phase. Most purchase contracts give the buyer a window to inspect and a window to secure financing, sometimes with a separate appraisal contingency. The names and lengths come from your contract and your state's forms. Put every deadline in a calendar the day you sign.

A problem you find after a contingency expires is still your problem, but you've lost most of your bargaining power. The CFPB puts it plainly. With a satisfactory-inspection contingency, you should be able to cancel without penalty if serious flaws turn up ([CFPB, Schedule a home inspection](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/schedule-a-home-inspection/)). That protection only works while the window is open and only in the form your contract states.

So order the work by what can kill the deal fastest. Legal use and the insurance quote come first, because no repair fixes a ban or an uninsurable house. Inspections come next, because specialists need scheduling time. Title and survey review run in parallel. The lender orders the appraisal; your job is to know when it's due.

Open one folder for the property on day one. Every report, quote, email, and photo goes in it with a date in the file name. You'll use the same folder at closing in PA.11 and in the operations handoff in PA.12.

### PA.9.2 Two questions, not one

A regular homebuyer asks one question during diligence: is this house sound? You have to ask a second one. Can this house run the rental business in Sam's underwriting, at his guest count and price?

Those two questions need different people. An inspector can tell you the roof has four years left. Only the county can tell you whether it will issue a rental permit. Only the insurer can tell you whether it will cover eight guests on a lake. Your tracker names each owner in writing.

Treat the PA.4 underwriting as a hypothesis. Every finding confirms a line or changes it. At the end you rerun the numbers.

### PA.9.3 Property condition: the STR inspection list

Start with a general home inspection by an inspector you've checked out yourself. The CFPB notes that inspectors may not be required to be licensed in some areas. It suggests checking references and your state or county licensing authority before you hire ([CFPB](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/schedule-a-home-inspection/)). Then add specialists for the systems a general inspector only looks at from the surface.

Tell the inspector the plan: eight guests, turnovers every few days, people who don't know the house. Here is the list I'd hand over for a house like Cedar Ridge.

1. Roof. Age, remaining life, flashing, gutters, signs of past leaks in the attic. A roof near the end of its life is a capital expense you'll pay while guests are booked.
2. HVAC. Age, service history, capacity for the full house, and whether the system can be set and monitored remotely.
3. Water supply. If the house is on a private well, the water test is on you. The EPA says its public drinking water rules don't apply to private wells. It recommends testing yearly for coliform bacteria, nitrates, dissolved solids, and pH ([EPA, Protect your home's water](https://www.epa.gov/privatewells/protect-your-homes-water)). The CDC also recommends using certified labs ([CDC, Guidelines for testing well water](https://www.cdc.gov/drinking-water/safety/guidelines-for-testing-well-water.html)). Test during the contingency, not after closing.
4. Sewer or septic. Lake houses are often on septic. The EPA notes that many states require a septic inspection when real estate transfers. For a typical household, it suggests inspection every 1 to 3 years and pumping every 3 to 5 years ([EPA, Frequent questions on septic systems](https://www.epa.gov/septic/frequent-questions-septic-systems)). An STR is not a typical household. More guests and more laundry mean more load. Ask the septic inspector to pump the tank, inspect the baffles and drain field, and tell you what bedroom count the system was permitted for. The county health department usually holds that record.
5. Pests. The CFPB says a lender may require a pest certificate, and you may want one even when it isn't required ([CFPB](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/schedule-a-home-inspection/)). Wood near water gets checked for rot and insects.
6. Electrical. Panel condition, capacity for a hot tub or EV charger you may add later, GFCI protection near water, and any exposed wiring.
7. Access and parking. Can a cleaner reach the house in winter? Is the road public or private, and who plows it? Is there parking for the guest count without blocking a neighbor?
8. Pool, hot tub, dock, and decks. The CPSC points out that pools and spas should have drain covers that meet federal standards ([CPSC vacation rental safety tips](https://www.cpsc.gov/s3fs-public/VacationRentalSafetytips002_0.pdf?VersionId=MKcrFCYmuih6xmpoX59wcdmexPwzAXrO)).
9. Fire and life safety. Smoke and carbon monoxide alarms, exits from every sleeping room, and extinguishers. Airbnb's own safety rules list blocked exits, inoperable smoke detectors, and exposed wiring as hazards hosts must address ([Airbnb, Safety requirements for homes](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2904)).
10. Flood exposure. Look up the address on the [FEMA Flood Map Service Center](https://msc.fema.gov/portal/search). FEMA says most homeowners insurance does not cover flood damage and flood coverage is a separate policy ([FEMA, Flood insurance](https://www.fema.gov/flood-insurance)).

Every finding gets a cost. Ask for a written range and record the midpoint in your tracker.

### PA.9.4 Business suitability: can this address run this business?

A normal home inspection never touches this half. You're testing five things.

The first is legal use of this exact parcel. You and Sam checked the market in PA.2, but a market answer is not an address answer. Use the SR.1 checklist to confirm zoning, the STR permit category, any cap or waitlist, and whether the permit is tied to the owner. Airbnb's own guidance says hosts are responsible for checking permitting, zoning, safety, and health rules ([Airbnb, Responsible hosting in the United States](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1376)). Get the licensing office's answer in writing if you can.

The second is whether anything transfers. Ask whether the permit transfers to a new owner or you must apply fresh, and how long that takes. If the house has bookings on the calendar after your closing date, your contract needs to say who honors them and who keeps the money. Don't rely on a verbal answer from the listing agent.

The third is private restrictions. Cedar Ridge has no HOA. That doesn't mean there are no rules. Recorded covenants on the lot can limit rentals or commercial use, and those show up in the title work covered in PA.9.6.

The fourth is the insurance quote. Get a bindable quote for a short-term rental policy before the inspection window closes. Ask what conditions the insurer attaches, such as railing repairs or added alarms, and when those must be done. Airbnb says AirCover for Hosts is not a substitute for your own insurance ([Airbnb, AirCover for hosts](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3733)). PA.12 covers the policy details after closing.

The fifth is guest capacity. Sam's underwriting assumes the house sleeps 8. Check that number against three limits: the septic permit's bedroom count, any occupancy cap in the local permit, and what the insurer will write. The lowest of the three is your real capacity. If it's lower than 8, your revenue line changes.

### PA.9.5 Inspection versus appraisal

The CFPB draws the line clearly. A home inspector works for you and looks for flaws. An appraiser is an independent professional whose job is to give the lender an estimate of market value ([CFPB](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/schedule-a-home-inspection/)). A clean appraisal does not mean the septic works. A clean inspection does not mean the house is worth your price.

On a first-lien dwelling loan, you're entitled to a copy of the appraisal before closing ([CFPB, 12 CFR 1002.14](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/14/)). Read its comparable sales. For a DSCR loan, ask whether the appraisal includes a rent or income analysis and which figure the lender will use.

PA.8 already worked the low-appraisal decision. In diligence, your job is to know the cash effect the day the report lands. Lenders generally size the loan on the lower of price or appraised value, so confirm your lender's rule in writing. Say Cedar Ridge appraised at $375,000 (assumption). At 75%, the loan drops to $281,250 and the down payment rises to $103,750. Points fall to $2,812, so closing costs become $14,362. Cash to close rises to $118,112, which is $7,424 more than the canonical $110,688.

### PA.9.6 Title review: who else has a claim on this land?

The title company searches public records and issues a title commitment. Read it yourself, with your attorney or closing agent. You're the one who knows how you plan to use the land.

A commitment usually has two parts that matter to you. The requirements list what must happen before the title company will insure, such as paying off the seller's mortgage or releasing a lien. The exceptions list what the policy won't cover, such as recorded easements and covenants. Anything in the exceptions stays with the land after you buy it.

Look for four things in that list.

1. Liens. A lien from an unpaid contractor or unpaid tax should be paid from the seller's proceeds at closing. The CFPB gives exactly these examples of claims owner's title insurance can protect against ([CFPB, What is owner's title insurance](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-owners-title-insurance-en-164/)).
2. Easements. A neighbor's right to walk to the lake across your lot changes privacy and guest experience. A utility easement may limit where you build a hot tub pad.
3. Covenants and restrictions. Read every recorded restriction for rental or business-use language.
4. Survey matters. A survey shows where the lot lines, structures, dock, and driveway actually sit. The CFPB lists survey fees among the closing services you can often shop for ([CFPB, Shop for title insurance and other closing services](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/shop-for-title-insurance-and-other-closing-services/)). A lake lot with a dock and shared access is reason enough to order one.

Now the two title policies. Lender's title insurance protects the lender. The CFPB says most lenders require it, and it covers only claims affecting the loan ([CFPB, What is lender's title insurance](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-lenders-title-insurance-en-163/)). Owner's title insurance protects you. The CFPB describes it as optional coverage you may want to protect your equity ([CFPB, What is owner's title insurance](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-owners-title-insurance-en-164/)). On an investment property with about $96,000 of your own cash in it, I'd want the owner's policy priced and decided on purpose. PA.11 shows where it appears on the closing figures.

### PA.9.7 Evidence, owners, and deadlines

The decision column only has four answers. Accept means you'll live with it and budget for it. Seller fixes means the seller repairs before closing, with proof. Credit or price means you negotiate money. Walk means you terminate inside the contingency window. Write the decision the day you make it, and write why.

Save every report, answer, and quote in the property folder. The insurer, lender, and permit office will ask for several of them again.

`[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]`

### PA.9.8 Worked example: Cedar Ridge diligence

Maya and Chris have Cedar Ridge under contract at $385,000 with a DSCR loan quote. Their PA.8 terms give them a 10-day inspection contingency, a 30-day financing contingency, an appraisal contingency, and a 45-day closing (assumption). Sam's PA.4 baseline is the canonical base case: gross booking revenue $74,275, operating costs $45,066, NOI $29,209, and DSCR debt service of $23,637. That leaves $5,572 of annual cash flow, or about $464 a month. The weak case is -$5,475.

All findings below are assumptions for teaching.

**Cedar Ridge diligence tracker (all figures are assumptions)**

| # | Item | Verified by | Deadline | Finding | Cost | Decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---:|---|
| 1 | STR permit for this parcel | County licensing office, in writing | Day 5 | Permitted use. Seller's permit does not transfer. New owner applies. Occupancy cap 2 per bedroom plus 2 (8 guests). | $400 / year | Accept. Apply right after closing. |
| 2 | Insurance quote | STR insurance agent | Day 6 | Quote at $3,200 / year. Conditions: repair dock stair railing and add alarms on lower level within 30 days of the policy start. | See rows 7 and 8 | Accept with conditions. |
| 3 | Roof | General inspector, roofer estimate | Day 8 | Asphalt shingles with 4 to 6 years of life left. Replacement estimate $14,000. | $2,800 / year sinking fund | Asked for a roof credit. Seller declined. Accept and fund it. |
| 4 | Septic | Licensed septic inspector | Day 8 | Tank pumped. Broken outlet baffle, missing effluent filter. County record shows a 3-bedroom design. | $1,850 repair | Seller fixes before closing, with invoice. |
| 5 | Septic, ongoing | Septic inspector | Day 8 | Guest load warrants yearly inspection and more frequent pumping. | $400 / year | Accept. Add to operating budget. |
| 6 | Well water | Certified lab | Day 7 | Total coliform present. Nitrates within limits. | $350 shock and retest; $1,650 UV system; $220 / year lamp and test | Seller treats and installs UV before closing, with invoice. Buyer retests before first guest and budgets the annual cost. |
| 7 | Dock stair railing | General inspector | Day 8 | Loose posts on stairs to the dock. | $2,300 | Accept. Repair within the insurer's 30 days. |
| 8 | Smoke and CO alarms | General inspector | Day 8 | No alarms on lower sleeping level. | $380 | Accept. Install before first guest. |
| 9 | HVAC | General inspector | Day 8 | 14 years old, working, no service record. | $275 service | Accept. Service after closing. |
| 10 | Title commitment | Title company, attorney | Day 15 | Mechanic's lien of $3,100 from a 2024 contractor. Recorded footpath easement for a neighbor to reach the lake. | $0 to buyers | Seller pays the lien from proceeds. Accept easement and disclose it in the listing. |
| 11 | Survey | Licensed surveyor | Day 18 | Dock and driveway inside lot lines. Footpath matches the easement. | Included in closing costs | Accept. |
| 12 | Appraisal | Lender's appraiser | Day 21 | Value supports the contract price. | None | Proceed. |
| 13 | Future bookings | Seller, per contract | Day 10 | Seller has no bookings after closing date. | None | Confirm in writing. |

Now rerun the numbers. Four findings add recurring annual costs that PA.4's generic lines don't cover.

| Added annual cost (assumption) | Amount |
|---|---:|
| Roof sinking fund ($14,000 / 5 years) | $2,800 |
| Septic inspection and pumping program | $400 |
| Well UV lamp and annual water test | $220 |
| County STR permit renewal | $400 |
| **Total added annual cost** | **$3,820** |

You might ask why the roof isn't already covered by the 5% capital reserve of $3,714. It could be, if nothing else ever broke. That reserve was a generic percentage. Now you have a known $14,000 bill in about five years, and it would eat almost four years of the whole reserve. Fund the known item on its own line and let the 5% cover the surprises.

The revised base case works out like this.

- Revised NOI: $29,209 - $3,820 = $25,389.
- Revised annual cash flow on the DSCR loan: $25,389 - $23,637 = $1,752, about $146 a month.
- Revised weak-case cash flow: $18,162 - $3,820 - $23,637 = -$9,295.

Now check the cash. PA.8 left Maya and Chris $11,020 of unallocated cash after their Test 3 set-asides, including two years of weak-case shortfall. The first draft of this tracker had the buyers paying for all the repairs: $350 + $1,650 + $2,300 + $380 + $275 = $4,955. The weak-case shortfall also grows by $3,820 a year, or $7,640 over the two years PA.8 funds. That's $4,955 + $7,640 = $12,595, which is $1,575 more than they have. On their own rule, the deal fails.

So they renegotiated inside the 10-day window. They asked for a $7,000 roof credit (assumption). The seller refused a credit or a price cut. The seller did agree to make repairs before closing: the $1,850 septic work plus the $2,000 well treatment and UV system. The seller also pays the $3,100 lien from their proceeds. None of those items touch the buyers' cash to close, which stays at $110,688. You'll see that figure again in PA.11.

The buyers now pay only the railing, alarms, and HVAC service after closing: $2,300 + $380 + $275 = $2,955. Their cash test becomes $2,955 + $7,640 = $10,595, inside the $11,020 by $425. Total cash in rises from $142,688 to $145,643. Cash-on-cash in the base case falls from 3.9% to 1.2% ($1,752 / $145,643).

Now the decision. The house is sound enough to buy. The business still works in the base case, barely, and the weak case got worse by $3,820 a year. The cash test passes by $425, which is thin. Maya and Chris decided to proceed and wrote down a new rule. If the first 12 months run closer to the weak case, they revisit the co-host and pricing plan at the OP.10 review.

A buyer with less cash could read the same file and walk inside the 10-day window. Both answers are defensible if they're written before the deadline.

### PA.9.9 Exercise: your diligence tracker

Copy this tracker for your property. Fill in "Verified by" and "Deadline" the day you sign, before any findings come in. Decisions are accept, seller fixes, credit or price, or walk.

| # | Item | Verified by | Deadline | Finding | Cost (one-time / annual) | Decision and evidence file |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | STR use and permit for this parcel | Licensing office |  |  |  |  |
| 2 | Permit transfer or new application timeline | Licensing office |  |  |  |  |
| 3 | Recorded covenants or HOA rules on rentals | Title company, attorney |  |  |  |  |
| 4 | Bindable STR insurance quote and conditions | Insurance agent |  |  |  |  |
| 5 | Flood zone and flood quote | FEMA map, agent |  |  |  |  |
| 6 | Guest capacity (septic, permit, insurer) | County, insurer |  |  |  |  |
| 7 | Roof | Inspector, roofer |  |  |  |  |
| 8 | HVAC | Inspector, HVAC tech |  |  |  |  |
| 9 | Water (public or well test) | Certified lab |  |  |  |  |
| 10 | Sewer or septic | Septic inspector |  |  |  |  |
| 11 | Pests and wood damage | Pest inspector |  |  |  |  |
| 12 | Electrical | Inspector, electrician |  |  |  |  |
| 13 | Access, parking, winter road | You, inspector |  |  |  |  |
| 14 | Pool, hot tub, dock, decks | Inspector, specialist |  |  |  |  |
| 15 | Fire and life safety | Inspector |  |  |  |  |
| 16 | Title commitment: liens | Title company |  |  |  |  |
| 17 | Title commitment: easements | Title company, attorney |  |  |  |  |
| 18 | Survey | Surveyor |  |  |  |  |
| 19 | Appraisal and loan size | Lender |  |  |  |  |
| 20 | Existing bookings or deposits | Seller, per contract |  |  |  |  |

Then complete the rerun box.

| Rerun of PA.4 baseline | Before diligence | After diligence |
|---|---:|---:|
| Gross booking revenue (change only if capacity changed) | | |
| Operating costs | | |
| NOI | | |
| Annual debt service | | |
| Annual cash flow, base case | | |
| Annual cash flow, weak case | | |
| One-time repairs you pay after closing | | |
| Total cash in | | |
| Cash-on-cash, base case | | |

Decision rules to apply before the window closes:

1. If legal STR use at this address is prohibited or unresolved, walk or extend the window in writing. Don't close on "probably."
2. If no insurer will write a policy at a price your numbers can carry, walk.
3. If real guest capacity is lower than the underwriting assumed, rerun revenue before anything else.
4. If the revised base case is negative, renegotiate or walk.
5. If the revised numbers break the cash test you set in PA.8, renegotiate or walk.
6. Record the decision, the date, and the reason in one sentence.

### PA.9.10 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** your real estate attorney or agent. They confirm contingency deadlines, written extensions, and how to terminate without losing earnest money.

**Confirm with:** the local licensing or zoning office. They confirm STR use for the parcel, whether a permit transfers, occupancy caps, and new-application timing.

**Confirm with:** the county health department or septic inspector. They confirm the permitted bedroom count and design capacity of the septic system.

**Confirm with:** your insurance agent. They confirm the policy can be bound, its conditions, and its price for your guest count, plus any flood coverage.

**Confirm with:** your lender. It confirms how the loan is sized after the appraisal and whether the appraisal includes an income analysis.

**Confirm with:** the title company and your attorney. They confirm which liens will be paid at closing, what each exception means, and whether a survey is required.

### PA.9.11 Next lesson

Next comes whose name goes on the deed and the loan. PA.10 Entity & Tax Structure Setup treats title owner, operator, and tax treatment as three separate choices. Bring your lender's written answer on whether it lends to an LLC, and the insurer's answer on who can be the named insured.

### PA.9 sources

- [CFPB, Schedule a home inspection](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/schedule-a-home-inspection/): inspection contingency, inspector vs appraiser, pest certificate, inspector licensing varies. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary (direct fetch blocked).
- [CFPB, 12 CFR 1002.14 Rules on providing appraisals](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/14/): copy of appraisals for first-lien dwelling loans. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [CFPB, What is lender's title insurance](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-lenders-title-insurance-en-163/): most lenders require it; covers only the lender's loan. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [CFPB, What is owner's title insurance](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-owners-title-insurance-en-164/): protects the owner against prior claims such as unpaid taxes or contractors. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [CFPB, Shop for title insurance and other closing services](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/close/shop-for-title-insurance-and-other-closing-services/): title and survey among shoppable services. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [EPA, Frequent questions on septic systems](https://www.epa.gov/septic/frequent-questions-septic-systems): inspection at transfer in many states; inspect every 1 to 3 years, pump every 3 to 5 years. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [EPA, Protect your home's water](https://www.epa.gov/privatewells/protect-your-homes-water): private well owners responsible for testing; annual test list. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [CDC, Guidelines for testing well water](https://www.cdc.gov/drinking-water/safety/guidelines-for-testing-well-water.html): use certified labs. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [FEMA Flood Map Service Center](https://msc.fema.gov/portal/search) and [FEMA, Flood insurance](https://www.fema.gov/flood-insurance): address flood map lookup; homeowners policies generally exclude flood. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [CPSC vacation rental safety tips](https://www.cpsc.gov/s3fs-public/VacationRentalSafetytips002_0.pdf?VersionId=MKcrFCYmuih6xmpoX59wcdmexPwzAXrO): pool and spa drain covers, alarms. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [Airbnb, Safety requirements for homes](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2904): hazards hosts must address. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [Airbnb, Responsible hosting in the United States](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1376): host responsibility for permits, zoning, insurance. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
- [Airbnb, AirCover for hosts](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3733): not a substitute for the host's own insurance. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search summary.
