<a id="lesson-OP.6-furnishing-interior-optimization"></a>
## OP.6  Furnishing & Interior Optimization for an Owned Property

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 2, Operations launch |
| Code | OP.6 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's OP.1 Listing Creation Across Airbnb, VRBO & Booking.com (uses the room inventory and photo list from this lesson); Sam's OP.2 2026 Listing Optimization; PA.12 handoff packet; B6 depreciation illustration (furnishings as 5-year property, CPA to confirm) |

### OP.6.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you can turn a furnishing budget into a room-by-room purchase plan for a specific house. The plan will match a named guest, a sleeping layout the listing can state truthfully, and a safety list your insurer can read. You'll also leave with a delivery schedule, a linen cycle, owner storage, and a replacement estimate. Last comes a room inventory Sam can use in OP.1 to build the listing.

### OP.6.1 Start with the guest, not the catalog

Most furnishing mistakes start in a store. Someone falls for a sofa, then makes the house fit it. Reverse that: write down who books this house and what they'll do, then buy for that person.

A guest profile has four parts: who comes, how many sleep over, what they do all day, and what they complain about at comparable houses. Read that last part in reviews of the comparable set from Sam's PA.4 package.

Then ask what each profile demands. Families with small children need a table for everyone, a crib that meets current standards, and furniture that can't tip. A work group needs a real desk and reliable internet. A lake crowd needs towels you don't mind seeing on a dock.

Then write down what this house is *not* for. If you won't allow parties, don't buy a sound system. If the lower level has stairs only, don't imply step-free access. Every accessibility claim in the listing has to be true on the day a guest arrives. Airbnb's [ground rules for hosts](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2895) say the listing page should accurately describe the home and the amenities available from check-in to checkout.

### OP.6.2 Sleeping layout: the one number guests check twice

The sleeping layout drives guest decisions, your linen count, your cleaning time, and your insurance conversation. Set it before you buy anything. Count sleeping spots by bed, not by room. If your advertised guest count depends on a sofa bed, say so plainly in the listing.

Airbnb's [sleeping arrangements article](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1224) says a bunk bed counts as 2 beds in the bed count shown on the listing. So six beds can mean six people or twelve, depending on bed types.

Before you settle the layout, check three things. First, local occupancy limits and any permit condition on guest count, which PA.9 should have captured. Second, egress: every sleeping room needs a fire exit your building or fire official accepts. Third, insurance: a guest count that differs from the policy can create a claim problem.

### OP.6.3 Work space, storage, outdoor use

Three areas get skipped in most furnishing budgets, then show up in reviews.

A work space is a desk, a chair, a lamp, and outlets within reach. If you claim a "dedicated workspace," a guest expects exactly that. If the internet can't hold a video call, don't advertise remote work. Test the speed at the desk and write the result in the handoff packet.

Storage means two different things. Guests need somewhere to put a suitcase and hang clothes for a four-night stay. The owner needs a locked place for personal items, spare inventory, and cleaning stock. Mixing the two produces the classic review: "closets were full of the owner's stuff."

Outdoor use is where lake houses earn their rate and where many injuries happen. A grill needs cleaning, fuel checks, and a placement your insurer and fire rules accept. A fire pit, dock, kayak, or hot tub each carries its own insurance question. If you can't maintain it every turnover, don't list it.

### OP.6.4 Safety and insurability before style

Safety equipment is the first line in the budget, not the last.

Airbnb's [safety requirements for homes](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2904) say hosts must state whether smoke and carbon monoxide alarms are present. If present, they must work and hosts must test them regularly. The same page says hosts must address fire and electrical hazards, including blocked exits, inoperable smoke detectors, and exposed wiring. It also covers poorly maintained appliances such as hot tubs, gas appliances, and furnaces, and physical risks like unsafe walls or windows. Hosts are responsible for any area guests can use during the stay.

The Consumer Product Safety Commission publishes a one-page [vacation rental safety tips sheet](https://www.cpsc.gov/s3fs-public/VacationRentalSafetytips002_0.pdf?VersionId=MKcrFCYmuih6xmpoX59wcdmexPwzAXrO) and related [news releases](https://www.cpsc.gov/Newsroom/News-Releases/2022/Make-Safety-a-Priority-When-Staying-in-a-Vacation-Rental-Home). Its recommendations include:

- Smoke alarms on every level, outside each sleeping area, and inside every bedroom.
- Carbon monoxide alarms on every level, outside sleeping areas.
- At least one fire extinguisher.
- TVs and furniture anchored to the wall, and no dangling window-covering cords.
- Pools and spas with compliant drain covers and a fence at least four feet high with self-closing, self-latching gates.
- Cribs that meet CPSC standards, with a bare sleep space (fitted sheet only).
- Cleaning supplies locked or out of children's reach, and a recall check on child products at [SaferProducts.gov](https://www.saferproducts.gov).

That's consumer guidance, not your local code. Your city, county, or state may require more, such as interconnected alarms. Treat the list as a planning floor and ask the fire marshal.

Security devices come next, because they affect privacy and listing accuracy. Airbnb's [device rules](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3061) ban security cameras and recording devices that monitor indoor spaces, even if they're turned off. Exterior cameras are allowed if the listing discloses their location. They can't point into the house or cover areas with a greater privacy expectation, such as an enclosed outdoor shower or sauna. Noise decibel monitors that measure only sound level are allowed in common spaces. They must be disclosed and kept out of bedrooms, bathrooms, and sleeping areas. Hidden cameras are prohibited. So buy a doorbell camera if you want one, place it outside, and write its location into the listing.

### OP.6.5 Durability, cleaning time, and replacement cost

Every item you buy has three prices: the purchase price, the minutes it adds to each turnover, and the cost of replacing it. The cheapest sofa is often the most expensive one by year three.

A few buying rules that hold up:

- Buy performance or washable fabrics in the living room. Removable covers let the cleaner handle a spill without a service call.
- Put waterproof encasements on every mattress on day one. A stained mattress is a replacement; a stained encasement is a wash.
- Buy white or one-color bedding in one line, so sets mix and match.
- Choose dishware you can rebuy by the piece, and keep spares on a shelf.
- Skip glass tabletops and lamps that need an app to turn on.

Think in cleaning minutes too. Twelve decorative pillows add time to every turnover. Your cleaner is paid per turnover, so extra minutes become either rushed work or a higher rate later.

### OP.6.6 The budget: purchase plan, spares, linen cycle, and a reserve

The furnishing budget is a fixed input from the underwriting. Maya and Chris set $32,000 for Cedar Ridge, and the financing table counts it in total cash in ($142,688 on the DSCR quote). Your job is to spend it well, not to find more.

Build the plan in five layers, in this order:

1. Safety and access items. These are fixed; nothing gets cut here.
2. Beds and mattresses, because they carry the listing and the reviews.
3. Linens and towels, sized to a cycle.
4. Kitchen, dining, living, work space, outdoor.
5. Delivery, assembly, spares, owner storage, and a contingency line.

The linen cycle sets how much bedding you buy. A common setup is three sets per bed: one on the bed, one in the wash, one clean on the shelf. Three sets let a same-day turnover happen even if the laundry falls behind. Towels follow the guest count, not the bed count: three sets per guest.

A breakage reserve covers what guests break and what wears out. In year one, that's mostly glasses, towels, and the odd lamp. The worked example shows how to fund it.

B6 treats the full $32,000 as 5-year property for its depreciation illustration. Linens, consumables, and delivery may be treated differently. The CPA makes that call (OP.6.10).

### OP.6.7 Worked example: Cedar Ridge

Cedar Ridge is a three-bedroom, two-bath house in a drive-to lake market, sleeps 8, no HOA. Maya and Chris plan about 10 personal-use days a year. The furnishing budget is $32,000. The base case expects 61 stays a year, averaging 3.5 nights.

**Guest profile (assumption).** Two related families or two couples with kids, arriving Friday, doing lake days, cooking most dinners. Midweek bookings in shoulder season lean toward remote workers and small groups. No parties; no pets at launch.

**Floor plan (assumption).** A main level with the kitchen, dining area, living room, primary bedroom, and bath 1. A walkout lower level with bedroom 2, a bunk room, and bath 2. A deck faces the lake.

**Sleeping layout.** Every bed type is listed exactly as installed.

| Room | Beds | Sleeps | Beds shown on listing |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| Primary bedroom (main level) | 1 king | 2 | 1 |
| Bedroom 2 (lower level) | 1 queen | 2 | 1 |
| Bunk room (lower level) | 2 twin-over-twin bunks | 4 | 4 (each bunk counts as 2) |
| **Total** | 6 sleeping surfaces | **8** | **6** |

No sofa bed is needed to reach 8, so the listing never depends on one.

**First draft, then cuts.** The first draft of the room-by-room plan came to $33,565 (assumption, computed in python). That's $1,565 over budget with no contingency at all. So we cut before buying anything. Safety, mattresses, and linens stayed untouched. The cuts came from style items and labor:

| Item cut | First draft | Final | Saved |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Sectional sofa | $2,400 | $2,000 | $400 |
| Accent chairs (2) | $900 | $600 | $300 |
| Living room art and decor | $500 | $300 | $200 |
| 65-inch TV | $700 | $550 | $150 |
| King bed frame | $900 | $700 | $200 |
| Primary dresser | $700 | $550 | $150 |
| Queen bed frame | $650 | $500 | $150 |
| Bedroom 2 dresser | $600 | $450 | $150 |
| Outdoor dining set | $1,200 | $950 | $250 |
| Outdoor lounge chairs (4) | $600 | $450 | $150 |
| Dining chairs (8) | $960 | $800 | $160 |
| Living room rug | $400 | $300 | $100 |
| Delivery and assembly (owner assembles small items) | $2,000 | $1,600 | $400 |
| **Total cuts** | | | **$2,760** |

Final purchases: $33,565 - $2,760 = $30,805. Contingency: $32,000 - $30,805 = **$1,195**, about 3.7% of the budget.

**Final room-by-room plan.** All figures are assumptions.

| Room or group | Main items | Amount |
|---|---|---:|
| Primary bedroom | King frame and mattress, encasement, 2 nightstands, 2 lamps, anchored dresser, blackout curtains, rug, art, luggage rack | $3,860 |
| Bedroom 2 | Queen frame and mattress, encasement, 2 nightstands, 2 lamps, anchored dresser, curtains, rug, art, luggage rack | $3,090 |
| Bunk room | 2 twin-over-twin frames ($1,300), 4 twin mattresses ($1,200), 4 encasements, nightstand, 4 reading lights, anchored dresser, curtains, rug, hooks | $3,920 |
| Living room | Performance-fabric sectional, 2 accent chairs, coffee and side tables, TV with mount or anti-tip strap, anchored media console, rug, lamps, art, games | $5,220 |
| Dining area | Table for 8, 8 chairs, lighting | $1,850 |
| Kitchen | Cookware, knives, dinnerware/glassware/flatware for 12, coffee makers, small appliances, utensils and bakeware | $1,670 |
| Bathrooms (2) | Hair dryers, mats, curtains, bins, mirrors | $500 |
| Work nook | Desk, chair, lamp, power strip | $510 |
| Deck and lake side | Outdoor dining set, 4 lounge chairs, gas grill, path lighting | $2,050 |
| Linens and towels | See linen cycle below | $4,005 |
| Safety and access | 5 smoke alarms, 3 CO alarms, 3 extinguishers, first aid kit, anti-tip kits, smart lock, exterior doorbell camera, common-area noise monitor | $1,280 |
| Owner storage and spares | Lockable owner closet ($350), spares shelf ($600) | $950 |
| Launch logistics | Delivery and assembly ($1,600), initial consumables ($300) | $1,900 |
| **Purchases subtotal** | | **$30,805** |
| Contingency (unspent balance moves to breakage reserve) | | $1,195 |
| **Total** | | **$32,000** |

The alarm count follows the CPSC placement guidance for this assumed layout. There's one alarm in each of the three bedrooms and one outside the sleeping areas on each level, for five. There's one CO alarm on each level outside sleeping areas, plus one near the furnace, for three. The fire marshal may change that count.

**Linen cycle, step by step.**

- Beds: 1 king + 1 queen + 4 twins = 6 beds.
- Sheet sets at 3 per bed: 6 × 3 = 18 sets. Cost: (1 × 3 × $90) + (1 × 3 × $75) + (4 × 3 × $45) = $270 + $225 + $540 = $1,035.
- Duvet covers at 3 per bed: (3 × $70) + (3 × $60) + (12 × $40) = $210 + $180 + $480 = $870.
- Duvet inserts, one per bed plus a spare king: $120 + $100 + (4 × $60) + $120 = $580.
- Pillows: 4 on the king, 4 on the queen, 1 per twin = 12, plus 4 spares = 16 at $25 = $400. Protectors: 16 at $10 = $160.
- Bath towel sets at 3 per guest: 8 × 3 = 24 sets at $25 = $600.
- Lake towels: 16 at $15 = $240. Kitchen towels and extra bath mats: $120.
- Linen total: $1,035 + $870 + $580 + $400 + $160 + $600 + $240 + $120 = **$4,005**.

With 61 stays and all 6 beds stripped each turnover, the house runs 61 × 6 = 366 sheet-set changes a year. Spread across 18 sets, each set gets washed about 20 times a year. Plan on replacing linens on a rolling basis, not all at once.

**Replacement pace and the breakage reserve.** New furniture doesn't fail on schedule, but it does wear out. The table below spreads each group over an assumed replacement life. These lives are planning assumptions, not tax lives.

| Group | Cost | Assumed life (years) | Per year |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Linens, towels, mattress encasements | $4,375 | 2 | $2,188 |
| Kitchen and bath smallwares | $2,170 | 3 | $723 |
| Outdoor furniture and grill | $2,050 | 4 | $512 |
| TV, lock, doorbell camera, noise monitor | $1,200 | 5 | $240 |
| Mattresses | $3,500 | 7 | $500 |
| Case goods, upholstery, rugs, lighting, decor | $14,110 | 8 | $1,764 |
| Alarms, extinguishers, first aid | $550 | 10 | $55 |
| **Total replacement pace** | | | **$5,982** |

Delivery, assembly, consumables, the owner closet, and the spares shelf ($2,850 combined) are left out; they aren't replaced on a cycle.

That $5,982 a year is about $98 per stay, or 8.1% of the $74,275 base-case gross. B1 carries repairs at 5% ($3,714) and a capital reserve at 5% ($3,714), $7,428 together. Furnishing replacement alone would use about 81% of both, before the roof or furnace. Bring that question to Sam's OP.10 review: do the reserves cover furnishings as well as the building?

For year one, Cedar Ridge funds breakage two ways. The $1,195 contingency, if unspent, moves into a separate breakage account at launch. Then Maya and Chris earmark part of the monthly repairs line for linens and smallwares. Any single replacement over $200 (assumption) needs owner approval; OP.8 builds that into the SOP.

**Delivery and setup schedule (assumption).** Day 0 is possession after closing (PA.11). Photo day is Day 21.

| When | What happens | Who |
|---|---|---|
| Before closing | Order long-lead items (sectional, mattresses, bunks) with delivery on or after Day 3 | Maya |
| Day 0-2 | Change locks, install smart lock, safety walk, deep clean | Chris, handyman |
| Day 3-5 | Beds, bunks, mattresses, dressers delivered and assembled; anchor tall furniture | Assembly service, handyman |
| Day 6-9 | Living and dining furniture, TV mount, work nook | Assembly service, Chris |
| Day 10-12 | Alarms, extinguishers, first aid kit, doorbell camera, noise monitor installed and noted | Handyman, Chris |
| Day 13-15 | Kitchen and linens stocked; owner closet locked; spares labeled | Maya, cleaner |
| Day 16-17 | Test stay: the owners sleep there and use every item | Owners |
| Day 18-20 | Punch list fixes; first full clean to the OP.8 standard | Cleaner, handyman |
| Day 21 | Photos; room inventory handed to Sam's OP.1 setup | Photographer, Maya |

The test stay is the step people skip. It finds the missing shower hook and the blind that won't close.

**Owner storage.** A locked lower-level closet holds personal items for the 10 personal-use days, spares, and cleaning stock. Only the owners and the cleaner have the code. The listing says one closet is locked and unavailable to guests.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### OP.6.8 Photo-ready checklist and room inventory for OP.1

Airbnb's [search explanation](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/39) says quality is a major factor in how listings appear. It says quality assessment includes listing content like photos and videos, ratings and reviews, host communication, and listing characteristics. Airbnb's resource center also notes that guests can [filter by amenities](https://www.airbnb.com/resources/hosting-homes/a/the-amenities-guests-want-25). An amenity you have but don't list can't show in a filtered search. An amenity you list but don't have becomes an accuracy problem. Airbnb can also ask hosts to [confirm photo accuracy](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2881) by taking new photos of the space in person through the app. So the photos have to show the house as a guest will find it.

Photo-ready checklist for the evening before photos:

- Every bed made in the standard set, the way the cleaner will make it every turnover.
- Every lamp working; every surface cleared except items the guest will actually find there.
- Safety equipment in place and visible where required (extinguisher, alarm locations noted).
- Doorbell camera location photographed for the disclosure; no camera anywhere indoors.
- Owner closet locked; no personal items in guest areas.
- Outdoor furniture set up as guests will use it; grill clean and covered.
- One shot of each bed type so the sleeping arrangement is visible.
- Work nook with the chair and lamp in place; internet speed test result saved.

Room inventory for Sam's OP.1 (a sample row per room; the full sheet lists every room):

| Room | Beds / seating | Listed amenities in this room | Safety items in this room | Photo shots needed | Notes for listing text |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Primary bedroom | 1 king | Blackout curtains, luggage rack, dresser | Smoke alarm | Wide, bed detail | Main level |
| Bunk room | 2 twin-over-twin bunks | Reading lights, dresser, hooks | Smoke alarm | Wide, bunk detail | Lower level, stairs only |
| Deck | Dining for 6-8, 4 loungers | Gas grill, lighting | Extinguisher nearby (per marshal) | Wide, lake view | Grill rules in house manual |

Sam's OP.1 lesson turns this sheet into the listing on each channel. Your job is to hand him facts that are true and complete.

### OP.6.9 Exercise: furnishing plan for your address

Fill this in for your property before you buy anything.

**Part A: guest and layout**

| Question | Your answer | Source or check |
|---|---|---|
| Primary guest profile (who, how many, what they do) | | Comparable listing reviews, PA.4 |
| What this house is not for | | House rules draft |
| Maximum occupancy allowed at this address | | Permit / licensing office, date |
| Bed list by room and bed type | | |
| Sleeps (sum of beds) | | Must match the listing and policy |
| Accessibility features you can truthfully claim | | Measured on site |

**Part B: budget build** (total must equal your underwriting budget)

| Layer | Items | Budget | Actual quote | Variance |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 1. Safety and access | | | | |
| 2. Beds and mattresses | | | | |
| 3. Linens and towels (3 sets per bed, 3 towel sets per guest) | | | | |
| 4. Rooms (kitchen, dining, living, work, outdoor) | | | | |
| 5. Delivery, assembly, spares, owner storage | | | | |
| Contingency (target 3-5%, assumption) | | | | |
| **Total** | | | | |

**Part C: decision rules**

- If the draft is over budget, cut layer 4 before layers 1-3.
- If contingency falls below 3% (assumption), cut again before ordering.
- If the replacement pace exceeds your repairs line, flag it for OP.10.
- If an accessibility, safety, or outdoor claim can't be verified on site, leave it out of the listing.

### OP.6.10 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the local licensing office or building department on maximum occupancy, bedroom egress, and any permit condition tied to beds or guest count.

**Confirm with:** the local fire marshal on smoke and CO alarm type, placement, interconnection, and extinguisher requirements for this address.

**Confirm with:** the insurer on the listed guest count, bunk beds, grill, fire features, water access, and any pool or hot tub.

**Confirm with:** the CPA on how furnishings, linens, supplies, delivery, and assembly are classified. Also ask whether B6's 5-year treatment of the full $32,000 holds for your purchases.

**Confirm with:** Airbnb's current help pages on device rules and listing accuracy before publishing, since platform policies change.

### OP.6.11 Next lesson

The house is furnished, safe, and photographed, and the listing facts are ready. Now someone has to keep it that way after every stay. OP.7 Building Your Ops Team: Cleaner, Co-Host, or Property Manager decides who cleans, restocks, inspects, answers guests, and holds a key. It also decides who can approve spending, such as the $200 replacement limit set here.

### OP.6 sources

- [Airbnb help 2895, ground rules for hosts](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2895): listing accuracy. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.

- [Airbnb help 2904, safety requirements](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2904): alarm disclosure, testing, hazards. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.

PDF brief-provided; content checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary; fetch blocked.
- [Airbnb help 3061, cameras and devices](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3061): indoor camera ban, exterior disclosure, noise monitors. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb help 39, how search works](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/39): quality includes photos and listing characteristics. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb Resource Center, amenities guests want](https://www.airbnb.com/resources/hosting-homes/a/the-amenities-guests-want-25): amenity filters. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.

<a id="lesson-OP.7-building-your-ops-team"></a>
## OP.7  Building Your Ops Team: Cleaner, Co-Host, or Property Manager

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 2, Operations launch |
| Code | OP.7 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | OP.6 (inventory, spending limit); OP.8 SOPs (written for the people chosen here); Sam's OP.4 Tech Stack Architecture (where messages and tasks live); 6.1 Cleaners and 6.2 Handyman (retained Roadmap lessons); 6.5 When to Bring In a Local Property Manager (the arbitrage version of this decision); 12.x Co-Hosting Masterclass |

### OP.7.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can list every job a stay requires and name who does each one, plus a backup. You'll decide what access each person gets on the platform, with money, with guest data, and with keys. You'll compare a set of hired vendors against a co-host or manager using your own numbers. And you'll spot the places where you'd pay two people for the same task.

### OP.7.1 Start with the jobs, not the job titles

"Should I hire a co-host or a property manager?" is the wrong first question. Those titles cover very different scopes, and the scopes overlap. Start with the list of jobs every stay needs. Then decide who does each one.

Here are the jobs for a single owned house, grouped by when they happen:

- Every stay: turnover cleaning, laundry, restocking consumables, a damage and condition check, and guest messaging from booking to checkout.
- On demand: maintenance and repairs, lockouts, supply runs, and access for vendors.
- Around the clock: emergencies and anything that can't wait until morning.
- On a schedule: inspections (filters, alarms, grill, deck, water heater), bookkeeping, tax filings, and permit renewals.
- Owner-only: pricing approval, compliance decisions, bank and platform ownership, and the performance review.

Notice what's in the last group. Some jobs you can delegate, but you can't hand off responsibility for them. The owner keeps title to the house, the loan, and the platform account. The owner answers to the city, the lender, and the insurer.

### OP.7.2 The role map and the backup rule

Once you have the job list, write one name beside each job. Then write a second name. The backup rule is simple: every role that touches a guest stay needs a backup who has already been trained and given access. Access for a backup has to be ready before the emergency, not handed over during it.

The roles for a small owned STR usually look like this:

| Role | Covers | Typical payment |
|---|---|---|
| Turnover cleaner | Cleaning, laundry, restock, condition photos | Per turnover |
| Backup cleaner | Same, when the primary can't | Per turnover, often at a premium |
| Handyman / on-call vendor | Repairs, inspections, vendor access | Retainer plus hourly, or hourly |
| Emergency contact | After-hours first response within a set drive time | Retainer, or part of a co-host scope |
| Guest messaging | Inquiries, check-in details, questions, issues | Owner time, co-host fee, or a messaging service |
| Inspections | Scheduled walk-throughs and safety checks | Handyman retainer or co-host scope |
| Bookkeeping | Categorizing income and expenses, reconciling payouts | Owner, bookkeeper, or CPA |
| Supply replenishment | Keeping par levels of consumables and linens | Cleaner, co-host, or owner by delivery order |

The retained Roadmap lessons 6.1 Cleaners and 6.2 Handyman cover finding and training those vendors. This lesson is about fitting them together for a house you own.

### OP.7.3 The access matrix: five kinds of access

Every person on the team needs some access. Give each one the least access that still lets them do the job. There are five kinds of access to decide, and they aren't the same thing.

1. Platform access. Can they see the calendar? Message guests? Change prices or the listing?
2. Spending authority. How much can they spend without asking, and on what?
3. Guest data. Do they see names, phone numbers, and messages? Do they store them anywhere?
4. Keys and codes. Physical keys, a lock code, the owner closet code, the Wi-Fi admin login.
5. After-hours responsibility. Who answers the phone at 2 a.m., and how fast must they get there?

The mistake most owners make is giving full platform access to someone who only needed a calendar. Access given for convenience is hard to take back later without friction.

### OP.7.4 Airbnb's co-host permission levels

Airbnb lets a listing owner add co-hosts with different permission levels. The [co-host permissions page](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534) describes three levels. The descriptions below come from search summaries of that page and related help pages, checked 2026-09-25. Check the exact labels in your account before you record or rely on them.

| Level | What it allows (per Airbnb's help pages) | Fits |
|---|---|---|
| Full access | Manage the listing and calendar, message guests, view payouts and transaction history, manage other co-hosts and their permissions, and submit reimbursement requests on the host's behalf | A full-service co-host or manager you have vetted and contracted |
| Calendar and messaging access | View (not edit) the calendar and message guests | A messaging co-host or a cleaner-coordinator who talks to guests |
| Calendar access | View the calendar and check-in and checkout details. These co-hosts don't appear as co-hosts on the listing | A cleaner or handyman who only needs the turnover schedule |

A few more points from Airbnb's help pages matter for owners:

- The listing owner is responsible for co-hosts, including co-hosts added by a full-access co-host. Airbnb's [co-host page](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534) tells owners to choose them with care.
- Only the listing owner can set up co-host payouts, per Airbnb's [co-host payouts article](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3389). Payouts can be set up for co-hosts at any permission level. When the host's earnings exceed the co-host payouts, co-hosts are paid first.
- According to Airbnb's co-host help pages ([payouts](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3389), [permissions](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534)), co-hosts can't view or change the owner's payout method or taxpayer information.
- The [Co-Host Additional Terms](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3264) say the host stays responsible for a co-host's actions and obligations from before the co-host was removed.

That second point about payment order deserves a pause. If a co-host is paid a percentage through Airbnb, their share comes out before yours on each booking. That's convenient. It also means you should check the payout math monthly, because a wrong percentage goes straight out the door.

Least-needed access for Cedar Ridge looks like this. The cleaner gets calendar access. A messaging co-host gets calendar and messaging access. Nobody gets full access unless they're managing the listing under a written agreement.

### OP.7.5 Vetting and the written agreement

A handshake works until the first damaged sofa. Every role that touches the house or guests gets a short written agreement. It doesn't need to be long. It needs to answer the questions you'd otherwise argue about later.

Agreement terms to cover:

| Term | What the agreement should say |
|---|---|
| Scope | Which jobs from the list in OP.7.1 are included, by name. Anything not listed is extra or not included. |
| Fees and fee base | Per turnover, hourly, flat monthly, or percentage. If a percentage, of what: nightly revenue only, nightly plus cleaning fees, or payout after platform fees. |
| Quality standard | The OP.8 checklist is the standard, with photo proof of each room after every turnover. |
| Response time | How fast they reply to a message and how fast they can be on site, by hour of day. |
| Spending authority | A dollar limit per item and per month without approval, and what counts as an emergency exception. |
| Insurance | General liability for any vendor who works in the house. Workers' compensation and auto coverage may apply depending on the state and the vendor's setup. |
| Data handling | Guest information stays in the platform or PMS. No screenshots of guest IDs, no personal copies of phone lists, deletion on termination. |
| Keys and codes | Individual lock codes, changed when someone leaves. No shared master code. |
| Backup coverage | Who covers when they're sick or booked, and who pays the difference. |
| Termination and handover | Notice period, return of keys, removal from the platform, and handover of future bookings and records. |

Some states and cities require a real estate or property management license for anyone who manages a rental for someone else for pay. That can apply to a co-host or manager. Ask before you sign.

**Confirm with:** an attorney or the state licensing board on whether the person you're hiring needs a license. Ask the insurer what vendor coverage your policy requires.

Vetting runs before the agreement. Ask for two references from other STR owners, not from friends. Ask for proof of insurance. Ask the cleaner to do one paid trial turnover to the OP.8 standard before the first guest. Ask the co-host how many properties they handle and who covers when two emergencies happen at once. [Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### OP.7.6 Vendor set vs. co-host vs. manager, and how to avoid paying twice

There are three broad setups for an owned house.

A **vendor set** means the owner hires each role separately and coordinates them. The owner does guest messaging and approvals. This costs the least in fees and the most in owner time.

A **co-host** usually takes on some or all of messaging, coordination, restocking, and local response. Cleaning and repairs are often still billed separately. Scope varies widely, which is why the agreement matters.

A **full manager** usually takes on the listing, pricing, messaging, coordination, and owner statements. Many managers use their own cleaners and vendors, and some add markups.

Paying twice happens when two people are paid for the same job. It usually happens quietly. The owner keeps a handyman inspection retainer after the co-host's scope already includes monthly inspections. Or the owner pays a messaging service while the co-host is also answering guests. Or the manager charges a supply markup while the cleaner also bills for supplies.

The fix is a task ledger. Write every job from OP.7.1 in one column. Beside each job, name exactly one payer. If two names appear, cut one or rewrite a scope.

### OP.7.7 Worked example: Cedar Ridge

Maya and Chris live about two hours away, and both work full time. The base case expects 61 stays a year (212 booked nights, 3.5-night average stay), with $74,275 gross booking revenue. On the DSCR quote, annual debt service (P&I) is $23,637.

**Option A: vendor set (B1).** A local cleaner at $150 per turnover ($9,150 a year). An on-call handyman and inspection retainer at $150 a month ($1,800 a year). Maya handles messaging; Chris handles approvals and bookkeeping.

- NOI: $29,209.
- Cash flow on the DSCR quote: $29,209 - $23,637 = **$5,572**.

**Option B: full-service local co-host at 18% (B3).** Same cleaner and handyman retainer, plus 18% of gross booking revenue.

- Co-host fee: 18% × $74,275 = $13,370.
- NOI: $29,209 - $13,370 = $15,839.
- Cash flow on the DSCR quote: $15,839 - $23,637 = **-$7,798**.

That is a $13,370 swing from positive to negative. The house doesn't carry an 18% co-host at the base case.

**Where Option B pays twice.** Suppose the co-host's scope includes monthly inspections and after-hours response. Then the $150 retainer duplicates part of that scope. Drop the retainer (all figures computed in python):

- NOI: $15,839 + $1,800 = $17,639.
- Cash flow: $17,639 - $23,637 = **-$5,998**.

Still negative, but $1,800 better, just from removing a duplicate.

**The fee base matters.** The same 18% charged on nightly revenue only, not on cleaning fees:

- Nightly revenue: 212 nights × $300 = $63,600. Cleaning fees collected: $74,275 - $63,600 = $10,675.
- Fee: 18% × $63,600 = $11,448 (assumption about the fee base), which is $1,922 less than B3.
- NOI with retainer kept: $29,209 - $11,448 = $17,761. Cash flow: **-$5,876**.

**Option C: messaging-only co-host at 10% (assumption).** The co-host gets calendar and messaging access and answers guests. Maya and Chris keep the cleaner and the handyman retainer and handle approvals.

- Fee: 10% × $74,275 = $7,428.
- NOI: $29,209 - $7,428 = $21,781.
- Cash flow on the DSCR quote: $21,781 - $23,637 = **-$1,856**.
- On the conventional quote ($22,763 debt service): $21,781 - $22,763 = -$982.
- Weak case (gross $57,330, NOI $18,162): fee $5,733, NOI $12,429, cash flow -$11,208.

**Break-even fee.** How much percentage fee can the base case carry before cash flow hits zero on the DSCR quote?

- Room before any co-host: $29,209 - $23,637 = $5,572.
- $5,572 / $74,275 = **7.5%** of gross booking revenue. On the conventional quote it's 8.7%.

Any percentage co-host above 7.5% of gross turns the base case negative on the DSCR loan.

**Pricing owner time.** Suppose Maya and Chris spend about 2 hours per stay on messaging and coordination (assumption). That's 2 × 61 = 122 hours a year.

- Option C costs $7,428 / 122 = about $61 for each hour of owner time it replaces.
- Option B costs $13,370 / 122 = about $110 per hour.

That's the real decision: is an hour of their time worth $61 or $110, given a cash flow that goes negative?

**A flat-fee alternative (assumption).** A messaging service at $400 a month costs $4,800 a year, about 6.5% of gross. NOI: $29,209 - $4,800 = $24,409. Cash flow: $24,409 - $23,637 = **$772**. Flat fees don't grow when revenue grows, and they don't shrink in a weak season either.

Summary, all on the DSCR quote:

| Setup | Added cost | NOI | Annual cash flow |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| A: vendor set (B1) | $0 | $29,209 | $5,572 |
| B: 18% co-host plus retainer (B3) | $13,370 | $15,839 | -$7,798 |
| B without duplicate retainer | $11,570 | $17,639 | -$5,998 |
| B at 18% of nightly only | $11,448 | $17,761 | -$5,876 |
| C: 10% messaging-only co-host | $7,428 | $21,781 | -$1,856 |
| Flat messaging service, $400/month | $4,800 | $24,409 | $772 |

Rows after B3 are assumptions computed in python from the canonical Cedar Ridge figures.

**The Cedar Ridge team.** Given these numbers, Maya and Chris choose a vendor set with a narrow messaging backup:

| Role | Primary | Backup | Platform access | Spending authority | Guest data | Keys / codes | After hours |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Turnover cleaner | Local cleaner | Second cleaner, trained on one paid trial | Calendar access | Supplies up to $75 per turnover (assumption), receipts required | Guest first name and party size only, from the calendar | Own lock code; owner closet code | No |
| Handyman | On-call handyman (retainer) | Second handyman or HVAC company | None; tasks sent by owner | Up to $200 per job without approval (assumption) | None | Own lock code, active during job window | Yes, within 60 minutes (assumption) |
| Guest messaging | Maya | Chris | Listing owner | Goodwill gestures up to $100 (assumption) | Full, in platform only | n/a | Yes, phone on until 10 p.m.; handyman after |
| Emergency contact | Handyman | Chris by phone; 911 for any fire, CO, or injury | None | Emergency exception, owner informed within 1 hour | Name and phone of the guest during the incident | Own lock code | Yes |
| Inspections | Handyman, monthly | Chris, on personal-use days | None | Within retainer | None | Own lock code | No |
| Bookkeeping | Chris | CPA at year end | Owner views payouts | n/a | Payout records | n/a | No |
| Supply replenishment | Cleaner reports par; Maya orders | Chris | Calendar access (cleaner) | Orders placed by owner | None | n/a | No |
| Pricing and listing | Maya, using Sam's OP.5 setup | Chris | Listing owner | n/a | n/a | n/a | No |

If Maya and Chris later add a messaging co-host, that person gets calendar and messaging access, not full access. The co-host's scope then replaces Maya's messaging row, and Maya's time moves to approvals.

### OP.7.8 Exercise: task ledger, access matrix, and cost check

**Part A: task ledger.** One payer per task. If a task has two payers, fix it before signing.

| Task | Who does it | Who pays | Paid how (per turn / hour / % / flat) | Duplicate? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Turnover cleaning | | | | |
| Laundry | | | | |
| Restock consumables | | | | |
| Condition photos | | | | |
| Guest messaging | | | | |
| After-hours response | | | | |
| Monthly inspection | | | | |
| Repairs | | | | |
| Supply ordering | | | | |
| Bookkeeping | | | | |
| Pricing | | | | |

**Part B: access matrix.** Fill in one row per person, including backups.

| Person | Role | Platform level (none / calendar / calendar and messaging / full) | Spending limit per item / per month | Guest data | Keys / codes | After-hours duty | Backup name |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| | | | | | | | |

**Part C: cost check.** Use your own underwriting from Sam's PA.4.

| Line | Your number |
|---|---:|
| Gross booking revenue | |
| NOI with vendor set | |
| Annual debt service | |
| Cash flow with vendor set | |
| Break-even fee % = cash flow / gross | |
| Proposed co-host or manager fee % and base | |
| Proposed fee in dollars | |
| Duplicate costs removed | |
| Cash flow with proposed setup | |
| Owner hours per year replaced (estimate) | |
| Cost per owner hour replaced | |

**Decision rules.**

- If the proposed fee is above your break-even fee, cash flow goes negative in the base case. Proceed only if you've decided to fund that from other income, and write that down.
- If the weak case goes negative by more than your reserves can cover for a year, reduce the scope or change the fee base.
- If any role has no trained backup, don't open the calendar until it does.
- If anyone has more platform access than their row requires, lower it now.

### OP.7.9 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** an attorney or the state real estate licensing board on whether a paid co-host or manager needs a license in this state.

**Confirm with:** the insurer on required vendor coverage, and whether a co-host or manager must be named or listed on the policy.

**Confirm with:** the CPA on how co-host payouts through Airbnb are reported, and whether any contractor needs a year-end information return.

**Confirm with:** Airbnb's current co-host help pages and your account screens on permission level names and what each allows, before recording.

### OP.7.10 Next lesson

You've named who does each job and what they can touch. Now they need instructions they can follow without calling you. OP.8 SOPs: Cleaning, Guest Communication & Emergency Response writes the turnover checklist, the message sequence, and the emergency matrix for the Cedar Ridge team. It then walks the team through a normal turnover, a guest problem, and a real emergency.

### OP.7 sources

- [Airbnb: What co-hosts can do (help 1534)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534). Permission levels (full access; calendar and messaging access; calendar access), owner responsibility for co-hosts. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (airbnb.com) summaries; direct fetch blocked. Exact labels to be confirmed in-app.
- [Airbnb Resource Center: New Co-Host permissions and simple payouts](https://www.airbnb.com/resources/hosting-homes/a/new-co-host-permissions-and-simple-payouts-587). Background on permission levels. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb: How co-host payouts work (help 3389)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3389). Only the listing owner sets up co-host payouts; co-hosts paid first. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb: Co-Host Additional Terms of Service (help 3264)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3264). Host remains responsible for co-host actions before removal. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- Canonical Cedar Ridge figures: `reference/recurring-examples.md` (B1, B2, B3, B4). New scenarios (10% messaging co-host, fee-base variant, flat service, break-even fee, owner hours) computed in python on 2026-09-25 and marked as assumptions.

<a id="lesson-OP.8-sops-cleaning-guest-communication-emergency"></a>
## OP.8  SOPs: Cleaning, Guest Communication & Emergency Response

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 2, Operations launch |
| Code | OP.8 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | OP.6 (inventory, linen cycle, $200 approval limit); OP.7 (role map and access matrix); Sam's OP.4 Tech Stack Architecture (where messages and tasks run); SR.5 tool comparison (AI messaging criteria); 9.3 Dealing With Damages and 9.4 How to Avoid Parties & Smoking (retained Roadmap lessons) |

### OP.8.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you'll have a short operating playbook for your house that a backup cleaner or a new co-host can follow without calling you. It covers a turnover checklist with photo proof, par levels, and a missed-clean escalation. It also covers a message sequence with a named owner for every message. It also covers an emergency matrix for seven kinds of incident. You'll see all of it run at Cedar Ridge: a normal turnover, a guest problem, and a real emergency.

### OP.8.1 What makes an SOP usable

A standard operating procedure (SOP) is a written routine that produces the same result no matter who follows it. Most STR checklists fail that test. They say "clean kitchen" and leave the standard to the cleaner's mood.

Every SOP in this lesson has the same seven parts:

1. Trigger: what starts it (a checkout, a message, an alarm).
2. Owner: one named role, taken from the OP.7 role map.
3. Steps, in order.
4. Standard: what "done" looks like, specifically.
5. Proof: a photo, a timestamp, or a reading.
6. Escalation: who to call, by when, if a step fails.
7. Record: where the result gets written down.

Keep each SOP to one page. If it runs longer, split it. The backup cleaner reads it in the driveway, not at a desk.

### OP.8.2 Turnover SOP with photo proof

The turnover is the SOP that runs most often. At Cedar Ridge's base case, it runs 61 times a year. Airbnb's [ground rules for hosts](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2895) set cleanliness as an expectation and name the basics. Hosts should clean between every stay: laundry, trash, vacuuming or sweeping, and wiping surfaces. Your SOP turns that into a standard.

Trigger: guest checkout in the calendar. Owner: turnover cleaner (calendar access only, per OP.7). Window: checkout 10 a.m., check-in 4 p.m. (assumption), so six hours.

| Step | Standard | Photo proof |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Arrive and log start | Start time recorded in the task app | Front door on arrival |
| 2. Walk-through before touching anything | Note damage, missing items, smoke smell, extra guests' signs, anything left behind | Any damage, before cleaning |
| 3. Strip all beds, start laundry | All 6 beds stripped whether used or not | None |
| 4. Kitchen | Dishes clean and put away, appliances wiped inside and out, fridge emptied of guest food, trash and recycling out | Counters, open fridge |
| 5. Bathrooms | Toilets, showers, sinks, mirrors, floors; hair check on every surface | Each bathroom, wide |
| 6. Bedrooms | Beds made in the standard set from the shelf, surfaces dusted, under beds checked | Each bed |
| 7. Living, dining, work nook | Surfaces, cushions, remotes, floors vacuumed or mopped | Each room, wide |
| 8. Deck and grill | Furniture set, grill grates clean, cover on, no guest trash | Deck, grill |
| 9. Restock | Consumables to par (OP.8.3) | Supply shelf |
| 10. Safety check | Alarms present and not disabled; extinguishers in place; no indoor device added; owner closet locked | Each alarm location, weekly test logged |
| 11. Final walk and lock | Lights off, thermostat to vacancy setting, doors locked | Front door on exit |
| 12. Report | Checklist complete, damage and low-stock items flagged | All photos uploaded |

Photos serve two purposes. The owner can check the work from two hours away, and the photos are evidence if a guest later reports a problem. The retained lesson 9.3 Dealing With Damages covers how to use damage photos in a claim.

Airbnb's [safety requirements](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2904) say that if smoke and CO alarms are present, hosts must keep them working and test them regularly. The turnover checks that no alarm has been removed or disabled. A full button test on a schedule goes in the monthly inspection. Set the test schedule with the alarm manufacturer's instructions and your fire marshal.

### OP.8.3 Linen and consumable inventory

Par level means the amount you keep on hand. When stock falls to the reorder point, someone orders more. Write both numbers on the shelf label so nobody has to guess.

Linens follow the OP.6 cycle. Cedar Ridge has 18 sheet sets (three per bed for six beds) and 24 towel sets (three per guest for eight guests). One set is on each bed, one is in the laundry, one is on the shelf. The cleaner counts the shelf at every turnover. If any bed has fewer than one clean set on the shelf after the beds are made, the cleaner flags it.

Consumables run about $25 per stay at Cedar Ridge ($1,525 a year across 61 stays). Keep stock for four stays on site (assumption), about $100 worth, and reorder at two stays. That leaves room for a back-to-back weekend without a supply run.

| Item | Par (stays) | Reorder at | Counted by | Ordered by |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| Toilet paper, paper towels | 4 | 2 | Cleaner | Maya |
| Soap, shampoo, conditioner | 4 | 2 | Cleaner | Maya |
| Dish soap, dishwasher pods, sponges | 4 | 2 | Cleaner | Maya |
| Trash bags | 4 | 2 | Cleaner | Maya |
| Coffee, tea, sugar, oil, salt, pepper | 4 | 2 | Cleaner | Maya |
| Laundry detergent | 4 | 2 | Cleaner | Maya |
| Sheet sets on shelf | 1 per bed | 1 per bed | Cleaner | Maya |
| Towel sets on shelf | 8 | 8 | Cleaner | Maya |

Replacements follow the approval limits from OP.6 and OP.7. The cleaner replaces a broken glass or a stained towel from the spares shelf and notes it. Anything over $200 (assumption) needs owner approval before purchase. The cleaner never pays for replacements out of pocket without a written OK.

### OP.8.4 Missed-clean escalation

The worst operational failure is a guest arriving to a dirty house. It usually starts with a cleaner who doesn't show up and a host who finds out at 3:30 p.m. The escalation ladder catches it by late morning.

| Time on turnover day | Check | If it fails | Who acts |
|---|---|---|---|
| 8 p.m. the night before | Cleaner confirms tomorrow's turnover in the task app | Owner calls the cleaner | Maya |
| 9 p.m. the night before | Cleaner still not confirmed | Owner alerts the backup cleaner to hold the slot | Maya |
| 11:00 a.m. | Arrival photo received | Call the cleaner; if no answer in 15 minutes, send the backup | Maya |
| 11:30 a.m. | Backup cleaner en route | Tell the arriving guest there may be a short delay; offer a later check-in time | Maya |
| 2:30 p.m. | Turnover more than half done (photo count) | Decide: delay check-in, or send a second cleaner | Maya |
| 3:45 p.m. | All photos in, report complete | Owner reviews photos before releasing the door code | Maya |
| After | Missed clean recorded | Second miss in 90 days triggers a scope review | Chris |

The backup cleaner is paid a same-day premium, $200 instead of $150 (assumption). If that happens four times a year, it costs 4 × $50 = $200 more. That's cheap next to a refund and a one-star cleanliness review.

### OP.8.5 Message sequence from booking to checkout

Every guest gets the same core messages at the same moments. Automation sends most of them. A named person owns each one and checks it.

Airbnb lets hosts send [scheduled quick replies](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2897) automatically, triggered by events like a new booking, check-in, or checkout. The same help page says hosts can skip, send early, or edit an upcoming scheduled message without changing the template. When hosting with a team, members with guest management permissions share the scheduled replies, and messages are sent from the team owner. Airbnb also offers suggested quick replies, which use AI to suggest one of your existing replies for common questions.

| When | Message | Sent by | Owner who checks it | What must be right |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 5 minutes after booking | Thanks, what to expect, house rules summary, guest count check | Scheduled | Maya | Names the correct guest count and no-party rule |
| 7 days before | Trip prep: directions, parking, lake access rules, what to bring | Scheduled | Maya | Seasonal details current |
| 1 day before, 10 a.m. | Check-in details: address, door code, Wi-Fi, where the house manual is | Scheduled, held for review | Maya | Correct unique code for this stay |
| Check-in day, evening | Short check-in follow-up: "Anything missing?" | Manual | Maya | Sent by a person who can act on the answer |
| Mid-stay (stays of 4+ nights) | Offer a restock or answer questions | Manual | Maya | Optional; skip for short stays |
| 1 day before checkout | Checkout steps: dishes, trash, thermostat, lock | Scheduled | Maya | Steps match the cleaner's expectations |
| After checkout | Thanks for staying | Scheduled | Maya | Neutral wording; see OP.9 on review requests |

Human review is a written weekly step, not a hope. Every Monday, the message owner opens the list of upcoming scheduled messages for the week. They check each one against the booking: guest count, dates, the lock code, and any special arrangement.

Automation needs limits. Write these into the SOP and into any AI messaging tool you use:

- No automated or AI reply goes to a message about smoke, fire, gas, alarms, leaks, injury, police, lockouts, or refunds. A person answers those immediately.
- No automated message sends a changed door code. A person sends it after checking the lock.
- An AI suggestion is a draft. A person approves it before it sends, unless it's an approved template for a routine question.
- Any exception granted (early check-in, late checkout, extra guest) gets written into the booking notes so the cleaner sees it.

SR.5 compares AI messaging tools on these criteria. Sam's OP.4 shows where messages and tasks live in the tech stack.

### OP.8.6 Emergency matrix

An emergency matrix tells the person on the spot what to do first and who to call. It also says who can stop work and what to record. Write it for the guest and the team together, and put the guest's part in the house manual.

Airbnb's help center says to contact [local emergency services](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3063) first if there's an emergency in progress. Airbnb also describes an [Urgent Support Line](https://news.airbnb.com/expanding-the-urgent-support-line) in its app for hosts and guests during active reservations. Neither replaces 911, and neither replaces your own on-call person.

Stop-work authority means the right to halt a turnover, a repair, or the next booking because something isn't safe. At Cedar Ridge, anyone on the team can stop work for safety. Only an owner can reopen the calendar, and only after the right professional signs off.

| Incident | First action (guest or first person on site) | Who responds | Stop-work authority | Record |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fire or smoke | Get everyone out, then call 911 from outside | 911; after-hours contact goes to site; owner notified | Anyone; owner blocks the calendar | Incident report, fire department report number, photos, insurer notice |
| CO alarm | Get everyone out to fresh air, then call 911 | 911 or fire department; gas utility; licensed HVAC tech | Anyone; no one re-enters until the fire department clears it | Incident report, alarm location, readings if given, technician report |
| Water leak | Shut off water at the main (location marked in manual); move away from electrical | Handyman; plumber; owner | Handyman or owner | Photos, shutoff time, plumber invoice, insurer notice if damage |
| Lockout | Guest messages or calls the on-call number | Messaging owner resends or resets code; handyman on site if lock fails | n/a | Time locked out, cause, fix |
| Injury | Call 911 for anything serious; first aid kit location in manual | 911; owner notified immediately | Owner decides whether the area stays closed | Incident report (facts only), photos of the area, insurer notice |
| Power, water, or internet outage | Check the utility's outage page or house manual steps | Messaging owner; handyman if on-site check needed | Owner decides whether the stay continues | Outage times, utility reference, any guest credit |
| Severe weather | Follow official local warnings; shelter location in manual | Messaging owner sends a warning message; handyman after the storm | Owner decides on arrivals and relocations | Warning time, messages sent, damage photos |

Keep a laminated contact sheet inside the owner closet door and a copy in the house manual. It lists 911, non-emergency police and fire, the utilities, and the water shutoff location. It also lists the on-call phone, handyman, plumber, HVAC company, and insurer's claim line. Fill in the real local numbers before the first booking.

**Confirm with:** the local fire department on CO and fire response steps for this house. Ask the insurer how fast they need incident notice and what records they want.

### OP.8.7 Guest privacy and safety devices in the SOPs

Privacy rules belong in the SOPs, because the people who break them are often well-meaning. A cleaner might want to set up a "nanny cam" to watch for parties. That's not allowed.

Airbnb's [device rules](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3061) prohibit security cameras and recording devices that monitor indoor spaces, even if they're off. Exterior cameras must be disclosed in the listing and can't watch indoor spaces or areas like an enclosed outdoor shower. Noise decibel monitors that don't record sound are allowed in common spaces if disclosed, and not in bedrooms, bathrooms, or sleeping areas.

Put three lines in the turnover SOP. The cleaner confirms no new device has appeared indoors. The noise monitor is still in its disclosed common-area spot. The doorbell camera still points only at the entry. Put one line in the data SOP too: camera footage and guest details stay in the platform or the device's own app. Nobody downloads them to a personal phone or shares them outside the team. The retained lesson 9.4 How to Avoid Parties & Smoking covers the party-prevention side.

### OP.8.8 Worked example: three walk-throughs at Cedar Ridge

**Normal turnover (Sunday).** A family of seven checks out at 10:00 a.m. after a 3.5-night stay. The next group of eight arrives at 4:00 p.m.

- 8:05 p.m. Saturday: the cleaner confirms in the task app.
- 10:25 a.m.: arrival photo received. The walk-through finds one chipped mug and a lake towel with a stain.
- 10:40 a.m.: all six beds stripped and laundry started. Six clean sets go on the beds and six used sets go to the wash. That leaves 18 - 6 - 6 = 6 clean sets on the shelf.
- 10:45 a.m. to 1:50 p.m.: kitchen, bathrooms, bedrooms, living areas, deck (about 3 hours, assumption).
- 1:55 p.m.: restock. Consumables for one stay cost about $25; the shelf drops from four stays to three, above the reorder point of two.
- 2:00 p.m.: the mug is replaced from the spares shelf and the towel goes to the rag bin. Both are logged. Total under $200, so no approval is needed.
- 2:10 p.m.: safety check. All alarms in place, extinguishers mounted, no indoor devices, owner closet locked.
- 2:20 p.m.: final photos uploaded. Maya reviews them at 2:45 p.m. and releases the scheduled check-in message.
- Cost: cleaner pay $150; the guest paid a $175 cleaning fee.

**Guest problem (Tuesday).** A guest on a four-night midweek stay messages at 9:05 a.m.: "Internet is down. I have work calls today." The listing advertises a dedicated workspace.

- 9:05 a.m.: the message mentions no safety words, so an automated acknowledgment is allowed. The SOP still requires a person within 30 minutes (assumption). Maya replies at 9:15 a.m. with the house manual's reset steps: unplug the router for 30 seconds, wait five minutes.
- 9:35 a.m.: still down. Maya checks the internet provider's outage page. It shows an area outage with an estimated fix by 6 p.m.
- 9:40 a.m.: Maya tells the guest what she knows and when she'll update them. She doesn't guess at a restore time the provider hasn't given.
- 10:00 a.m.: Maya offers a goodwill credit of $100, inside her OP.7 limit. She pays it through the platform, not in cash, and doesn't tie it to a review. Airbnb's review policy prohibits offering something of value in exchange for a review (OP.9 covers this).
- 6:20 p.m.: service returns. Maya confirms with the guest.
- Record: outage times, provider reference, the credit, and one root-cause note. An advertised workspace with one internet line has no backup. Chris gets a quote for a cellular backup router. It's above $200 (assumption), so it goes on the owner decision list for the month-end review.

**Emergency (Saturday, 1:40 a.m.).** A guest calls the after-hours number: the CO alarm on the lower level is sounding.

- 1:40 a.m.: the handyman answers, per the OP.7 after-hours row. His first words follow the matrix: "Get everyone outside now, then call 911." He stays on the line until all eight guests are outside.
- 1:44 a.m.: the guest calls 911. The handyman texts Chris.
- 1:48 a.m.: the handyman leaves for the house (about 35 minutes away, assumption). He doesn't go inside; the fire department controls the scene.
- 2:05 a.m.: the fire department arrives. They find elevated CO near the furnace (a propane furnace, assumption) and shut off the gas supply. Nobody goes back in tonight.
- 2:15 a.m.: Chris uses the Airbnb app's urgent support option and messages the guest. Relocation spending is above any team member's limit, so Chris authorizes it himself as an emergency exception. He books a nearby hotel.
- 2:30 a.m.: stop-work. The handyman tapes a "do not occupy" notice to the front door. Chris blocks the calendar for the next arrival, due Sunday afternoon.
- 8:00 a.m.: a licensed HVAC technician inspects the furnace. The house stays closed until the technician and gas supplier clear it in writing.
- Record: incident report with times, alarm location, fire department report number, HVAC findings, hotel receipt, and messages sent. Chris notifies the insurer the same day.
- Follow-up: Chris decides whether to cancel or relocate Sunday's guests based on the technician's timeline. The monthly inspection adds a furnace service check before each heating season.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### OP.8.9 Exercise: build your one-page SOPs

Fill in one page per SOP for your property.

**SOP header (use for every SOP)**

| Field | Your entry |
|---|---|
| SOP name | |
| Trigger | |
| Owner (role from OP.7) | |
| Backup | |
| Steps (numbered) | |
| Standard for "done" | |
| Proof required | |
| Escalation (who, by when) | |
| Record (where) | |
| Last reviewed (date) | |

**Emergency matrix (fill in real local numbers)**

| Incident | First action | Who responds (name, phone) | Stop-work authority | Record | Local number verified (date) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fire or smoke | | | | | |
| CO alarm | | | | | |
| Water leak (shutoff location: ______) | | | | | |
| Lockout | | | | | |
| Injury | | | | | |
| Power / water / internet outage | | | | | |
| Severe weather (shelter location: ______) | | | | | |

**Decision rules**

- If a step has no named owner, the SOP isn't finished.
- If the backup hasn't done the SOP once for real or in a paid trial, they aren't a backup yet.
- If an automated message can send a door code or reply to a safety message, change the setting before the next booking.
- Review every SOP after any incident and at least every six months (assumption).

### OP.8.10 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the local fire department on the first actions for fire and CO alarms at this house, and alarm testing frequency.

**Confirm with:** the insurer on incident notice deadlines, required records, and whether relocation costs are covered.

**Confirm with:** the utilities (gas or propane, electric, water) on emergency numbers and shutoff procedures.

**Confirm with:** Airbnb's current help pages on scheduled messages, safety requirements, and device rules before recording.

### OP.8.11 Next lesson

The house now runs the same way whether Maya, Chris, or a backup is on duty. The next question is how guests experience that work, and what they say about it afterward. OP.9 Guest Experience & Review Strategy in the AI Ranking Era connects these SOPs to what Airbnb publishes about search. It also shows how to ask for reviews within Airbnb's rules and how to track what guests tell you.

- [Airbnb: Ground rules for home hosts (help 2895)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2895). Cleaning between every stay; communication expectations. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (airbnb.com) summary.
- [Airbnb: Safety requirements for homes (help 2904)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2904). Alarm disclosure, working order, regular testing; hazards. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb: Restrictions on security cameras and other devices (help 3061)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3061). Indoor camera ban, exterior disclosure, noise monitors. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb: Create scheduled quick replies (help 2897)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2897). Triggers, skip/send early/edit, team sharing, AI-suggested replies. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb: Promoting the safety of our community (help 3063)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3063). Contact local emergency services first in an emergency. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary (page attribution from search results).
